## IDEA-002 — FUNDSTATE: intermediary funding cost as a state variable for flow-driven price impact

- **ID / short name:** IDEA-002 / FUNDSTATE
- **Date added:** 2026-10-19 · **Cycle:** 1 · **Status:** **rejected (R-038, Cycle 58)** — was parked (P-004, feasibility precondition G-01 open) · **Version:** 1.1
- **Change notes:** v1.0 — initial dossier. The components are well established; the *combination*
  (funding state × order-flow impact asymmetry) is the candidate. **Status field corrected in Cycle
  37** — it still read "shortlisted" although the candidate had been parked as P-004 since Cycle 5
  (G-01: no public continuous dealer funding-value-adjustment series); the ranked shortlist already
  recorded it as parked. **v1.1 — Cycle 58: the candidate is REJECTED (R-038).** G-01 was executed and
  closed as a negative on three independent grounds (the proposed validation is tautological for the
  candidate's own conditioning variable; the disclosed-dealer-FVA target is annual, thin,
  non-comparable and not multi-year; and the state the candidate actually needs — dealer-specific
  balance-sheet capacity — is not publicly measurable, while every public ingredient is market-wide and
  therefore conditions only the *size* of an effect whose sign and existence are published). The
  candidate's own dossier (prediction 4) and its own parking entry (P-004) both prescribed rejection
  rather than re-specification in that case, and both were followed. Score withdrawn; the dossier below
  is retained as the historical record.

**One-sentence hypothesis.** When intermediary funding costs are high and dealers' balance sheets
are constrained, flow-driven price impact is larger and more persistent than when funding is cheap,
so the *sign and size* of the expected price response to a given order-flow shock depends on an
observable funding state.

**Plain-language description.** The same order-flow imbalance moves a price more, and moves it back
more slowly, when the dealers who absorb that flow are expensive to fund. So a directional signal
built on order flow should be scaled by a funding-state variable, and short-horizon flow strategies
should expect worse exit liquidity precisely when their signals work best.

**Mechanism (causal chain).**
1. Dealers intermediate client flow on their balance sheet; the cost of doing so is a funding
   cost that the dealer's shareholders bear as a debt overhang, and dealer quotations therefore
   embed it (S-010).
2. Funding costs are state-dependent: they reflect the marginal cost of balance sheet, which
   rises with volatility, with balance-sheet congestion, and with the discount rate applied to
   encumbered assets.
3. Under Brunnermeier–Pedersen (S-009), margin and funding constraints make market liquidity
   state-dependent and can make it "dry up" and become self-reinforcing; speculators' capital and
   volatility are state variables for liquidity and for risk premia.
4. Order-flow imbalance explains short-horizon price changes with an impact slope inversely
   proportional to market depth (S-011) — the impact relation is therefore not a constant; it
   inherits the liquidity state.
5. Combining: the flow-impact coefficient and the subsequent reversal are functions of the
   funding/liquidity state. A flow signal conditioned on the funding state should have a
   time-varying, state-dependent expected value, and the *dealer-intermediated* segment of the
   market should behave differently from internally managed assets in the same state.

**Expected return source.** Compensation for intermediation and liquidity provision that varies
with the funding state, plus a short-horizon reversal of temporary price pressure that is larger
when the pressure was larger.

**Originality.**
- *Closest known work:* Brunnermeier & Pedersen [S-009]; Andersen, Duffie & Song [S-010]; Cont,
  Kukanov & Stoikov [S-011]; Hasbrouck-style dealer inventory work; and the broader
  "liquidity premium" literature.
- *What is already known:* that funding constraints and market liquidity are linked; that dealer
  funding costs are a real, quantified wedge; that order flow drives short-horizon prices with a
  depth-dependent slope; that liquidity is a state variable with nonlinear effects.
- *Substantive differentiator claimed here:* a *joint* state model in which a publicly observable
  funding-state proxy is used to predict the **asymmetry between flow-driven continuation and
  reversal**, and to distinguish the dealer-intermediated part of the market from the
  self-cleared part. I am not aware of a published result that conditions flow-impact asymmetry on
  a funding state; the retrieved literature treats funding (S-009) and impact (S-011) separately.
- *Honest classification:* **informed synthesis; the linking relation is an unresolved conjecture.**
- *Confidence in the comparison:* moderate. The three components are solid; my claim is about the
  absence of a published *combination*, which I cannot establish exhaustively from a limited search.

**Target scope.** US equities and ETFs with substantial dealer intermediation; intraday to
multi-day horizons; daily rebalancing of a flow signal; regime conditions: exchange-traded,
clearing-fee and margin schedules observable; stress episodes (margin hikes, futures-basis
stress) are part of the state, not an exception to it.

**Observables.** Order-flow imbalance from trades and quotes; quoted depth; a funding-state proxy
(constructed from observable funding instruments — see below); market and sector volatility;
dealer-intermediation intensity (share of volume executed against dealers; the fraction of the
universe with listed options and active market makers); margin and clearing-fee schedules as
discrete funding shocks.

**Rule sketch (descriptive, non-executable).** Compute a daily order-flow imbalance signal per
name. Estimate the state-dependence of the impact coefficient by conditioning on the funding-state
proxy (a composite of observable funding spreads, basis, and margin/collateral policy dates).
Trade the continuation leg scaled by the state; stand aside, or reduce exposure, in states where
the estimated impact-to-reversal ratio is unfavourable; restrict the tradable universe to
dealer-intermediated names in states where the funding constraint binds.

**Persistence thesis.** The funding wedge is a *price* for balance-sheet risk, not an error; it
persists because balance-sheet risk is real and because dealers' funding is scarce in exactly the
states when their intermediation is most valuable. It decays if balance-sheet capacity becomes
elastic (e.g. through a permanently abundant capital base or non-dealer liquidity provision) or
if dealer economics stop embedding funding cost in quotes.

**Constraints.** Costs: intraday spreads and adverse selection are precisely what the strategy
consumes, so cost sensitivity is extreme. Capacity: flow-based strategies are capacity-limited in
exactly the states where the signal is strongest. Financing: a long/short intraday book is
capital-intensive and margin-sensitive — the strategy's own funding cost moves with the same state
variable that generates the signal, which is a self-referencing hazard that must be handled
explicitly. Borrow: short legs are subject to recall in stress. Venue/operational: intraday
execution quality, queue position, and message latency. Market impact: the signal is *about*
impact, so slippage estimation error is the central risk.

**Regime sensitivity, tail exposure, crash behavior, hidden beta.** Strongly regime-dependent by
construction: the state variable *is* the regime. Tail: in the tightest funding states, the
book is short exactly the liquidity it needs to unwind, and gaps are large. Crash behaviour: the
reversal leg can fail violently in a dislocation, because the temporary pressure becomes permanent
fundamental news. Hidden beta: a long/short book built from flow signals is nearly beta-neutral by
construction but not risk-neutral; funding-state conditioning may load on credit and duration
factors. Correlated risks: all positions respond to the same state variable, so the book is a
single macro bet in stress.

**Falsifiable predictions and rejection conditions.**
1. *State-dependent impact:* the estimated flow-impact slope is larger in high-funding-cost states
   than in low-cost states. **Kill:** no monotone relation, or the relation is fully explained by
   contemporaneous volatility and depth.
2. *State-dependent reversal:* the post-shock reversal is proportionally larger when the
   funding state is tight. **Kill:** reversal is state-independent once volatility and spread are
   controlled.
3. *Intermediation differential:* the effect is present in dealer-intermediated names and absent
   (or materially weaker) in self-cleared names in the same state. **Kill:** no differential.
4. *Proxy validity:* the constructed funding proxy must be validated against disclosed dealer
   funding costs; if the proxy carries no information about them, the candidate is untestable and
   must be dropped rather than re-specified.

**Main confounders and rival explanations.** Volatility (a funding-state proxy and an impact driver
simultaneously — the hardest confound); spread and depth as mediators rather than confounds;
liquidity as a *cause* of both funding stress and price impact (so conditioning may be
over-controlling); index and ETF rebalance flows; and the possibility that the apparent effect is
an unmodelled news/attention state.

**Data requirements and feasibility for a future independent validation.** TAQ or equivalent
order-level data (trades, quotes, order-book) for a multi-year sample; a constructed funding-state
proxy from public funding instruments; dealer balance-sheet disclosures for proxy validation
(gap G-01). Feasibility: **medium-low** — the binding constraint is the funding-state proxy, not
the flow data.

**Theory/evidence status.** Conceptual rationale: supported [S-009, S-010, S-011]. Mechanism
supported by sources: for each component; the joint relation is an unresolved conjecture. Prior
empirical support reported by others: for the components (S-009, S-011); not for the combination.
Unresolved: whether a public, defensible funding-state proxy exists (G-01).

**Remaining uncertainty / confidence.** Confidence in the components: high. Confidence in the
joint relation: low-to-moderate. **The principal risk to this candidate is feasibility, not
mechanism** — if G-01 cannot be closed, the candidate must be parked rather than re-specified.
Priority score 63/100; rank 6 of 7 (rank 4 of 4 at Cycle 1; the score is unchanged, and the rank
fell only because three candidates were added above it — and rose back one place when IDEA-006
fell below it after the G-09 closure — the parking stands, P-004). **Concrete
next question:** is there a public funding-state
proxy that demonstrably predicts disclosed dealer funding value adjustments over a multi-year
sample? (If not, park the candidate.)

---

## IDEA-003 — VRP-STATE: the variance risk premium as crash-state compensation, and the state gate on harvesting it

- **ID / short name:** IDEA-003 / VRP-STATE
- **Date added:** 2026-10-19 · **Cycle:** 1 · **Status:** **parked (P-012) — not ranked** · **Version:** 1.2
- **Change notes:** v1.0 — initial dossier. The aggregate variance risk premium is a known baseline;
  the candidate is the *state gate* on harvesting it. v1.1 (Cycle 30) — reclassified as an incremental
  variant of published conditional-VRP work; V-003 amended. v1.2 (Cycle 35) — **G-22 executed and closed
  against the candidate: no published work establishes that an option-surface-implied imminent-crash
  measure adds risk-adjusted value to a variance harvest beyond the published state-conditioned
  volatility-trading family (S-180), so by the rule registered in Cycle 32 the candidate is a known
  baseline with an unvalidated state overlay and must be parked. Parked, not rejected**; V-003 is marked
  never-to-execute; the score of 66.0 is retained for the record.

**One-sentence hypothesis.** The variance risk premium is compensation for bearing crash risk
rather than a free annuity, so a strategy that harvests it should only be run in states where the
crash is not imminent, and the premium's sign in normal times is not evidence that it can be
harvested unconditionally.

**Plain-language description.** Investors pay up for insurance against crashes; that is why
implied variance exceeds realized variance. A strategy that sells variance earns that insurance
premium — but only gets paid if the world does not crash, and the states in which the premium is
largest are often the states in which the crash is coming. The premium is only harvestable with a
state gate.

**Mechanism (causal chain).**
1. Asset pricing with rare disasters implies that investors demand an insurance premium for
   bearing disaster risk, and that demand generates a negatively skewed risk premium (S-016).
2. Skew laws and the differential pricing of individual equity options show the same demand
   operating at the single-name level (S-014).
3. The variance swap rate is a near-exact model-free synthesis of option prices and approximates
   the risk-neutral expected variance; realized minus implied variance is the variance risk
   premium, which is strongly negative for US equity indices (S-012).
4. The premium is a *state variable* for aggregate equity returns, with the strongest predictive
   power at a quarterly horizon (S-013) — and crash risk is itself a state that commands an
   elevated equity premium (S-015).
5. Therefore the premium-harvesting payoff is negatively skewed: it is earned in the many
   ordinary states and lost in the rare state, and the *conditional* value of harvesting depends
   on the state. A gate on the crash state is part of the mechanism, not an optimisation overlay.

**Expected return source.** Compensation for absorbing crash risk in the left tail, plus the
time-varying pricing of variance as a risk factor (S-013).

**Originality.**
- *Closest known work:* the variance risk premium literature [S-012, S-013]; disaster insurance
  pricing [S-016]; single-name skew and insurance demand [S-014]; crash-risk and the equity
  premium [S-015].
- *What is already known:* the premium exists, is large, predicts aggregate returns, and is
  associated with crash-risk states.
- *Substantive differentiator claimed here:* the *state-gated harvesting specification* — that
  the premium should be harvested only outside crash-imminent states, that the unhedged harvest is
  a crash-state bet, and that the gate should be an explicit, pre-specified, ex-ante-observable
  state (implied crash measures, not realized crash outcomes). Part of this is anticipated by
  [S-015] (crash-risk states carry higher premia) and by the tail-hedge-fund evidence; the
  candidate's claim is narrower: the *unconditional* harvesting strategy is a state-contingent
  bet, and the gate is the mechanism.
- *Honest classification:* **known baseline with a state-conditioning refinement.** Not a new
  premium. Any claim of novelty for "short variance" would be false; the candidate is about *when*
  to short it.
- *Confidence in the comparison:* high for the baseline; moderate for the gate specification.

**Target scope.** US equity index options and variance swaps; single-name options for the
cross-sectional version; monthly to quarterly holding horizons for the aggregate version; regime
conditions: the gate must be computable from option data only (no realized crash variables).

**Observables.** Model-free implied variance and variance-swap rate from listed options (S-012);
model-free realized variance for post hoc measurement only; option-implied crash measures (e.g.
out-of-the-money put-implied crash probabilities) as the gate; skew; term structure; liquidity of
the option surface (spread and depth at the strikes used).

**Rule sketch (descriptive, non-executable).** Each period, compute the model-free implied variance
and the variance risk premium; compute the gate from option-implied crash-risk measures. In states
where the gate indicates an elevated crash probability relative to its own history, hold no
variance-shortening position (or hold a reduced position); in ordinary states, take the
variance-shortening position, scaled by the premium and by the liquidity of the option surface.
Report the harvest net of option spreads, margin and financing, and vega risk.

**Persistence thesis.** The demand for crash insurance is structural (S-016) and is amplified by
the growth of index and single-name option usage; the premium persists because the supply of
insurance is limited by the balance sheet and risk tolerance of the writers. It decays if insurers
expand capacity, if retail flow dominates option volumes, or if the option market's own tail
becomes a crowded trade.

**Constraints.** Costs: option spreads on the traded strikes; vega and gamma risk on the position;
the P&L is a variance-swap-like payoff that must be replicated or directly accessed, and both
routes have their own basis risk. Financing/collateral: variance positions consume margin that
moves with volatility, i.e. exactly when the position loses. Liquidity: far out-of-the-money
options are cheap but wide; the liquid ATM/OTM region is where the premium is smallest. Capacity:
the premium harvest is capacity-limited at exactly the sizes where the supply of insurance is
scarce. Venue/operational: OTC variance swaps introduce counterparty and documentation risk;
listed-option replication introduces tracking error. Shorting constraints: not a short-sale-constrained
strategy in the equity sense, but writing options requires exchange-level approvals and margin.

**Regime sensitivity, tail exposure, crash behavior, hidden beta.** Extremely regime-sensitive:
the strategy is short the thing that spikes in a crisis. Tail exposure: the defining feature — a
single crash can dominate many years of premium. Crash behaviour: gap risk, exchange halts, and
the fact that the premium's *realisation* is a small number of large negative events. Hidden beta:
a variance-short book is long gamma and short vega, and it has a negative exposure to volatility
increases; the equity market beta of the harvest can be state-dependent. Correlated risks: all
variance positions across the surface are the same trade.

**Falsifiable predictions and rejection conditions.**
1. *Gate works:* the unconditioned harvest has a materially worse risk-adjusted outcome and a
   fatter left tail than the state-gated harvest, on a pre-specified gate. **Kill:** the gate
   provides no incremental risk-adjusted benefit out of sample.
2. *Premium predictability:* the premium state predicts subsequent aggregate returns in the
   direction reported by [S-013]. **Kill:** no predictive relation in the modern sample (note
   [S-013]'s own caveat that the power of other predictors has decayed).
3. *Crash-state linkage:* the premium and the option-implied crash state co-move such that
   harvesting is least attractive exactly when the premium is highest. **Kill:** the premium is
   highest in states where crashes are *less* likely.
4. *Cost threshold:* the harvest survives option-spread and financing costs at a plausible
   institutional cost. **Kill:** the gross premium is mostly transaction cost.
5. *Structural change:* post-2010 single-name option structure (0DTE, retail flow) has not
   eliminated the mechanism. **Kill:** the single-name insurance channel has been arbitraged
   away or reversed (gap G-05).

**Main confounders and rival explanations.** The premium may be compensation for a variance risk
factor rather than for crash risk per se (S-012's own alternative) — these are hard to separate;
the measured "premium" partly reflects the volatility risk of the *hedging* programme; a
volatility-feedback story can generate similar options-pricing patterns; measurement error in
realized variance (closing-price noise) can inflate the estimated premium; and the gate can be
overfitted to the same history that defines the premium.

**Data requirements and feasibility for a future independent validation.** Listed option chains
(option quotes with model-free implied variance) and intraday prices for realized variance
(S-012, S-013 use OptionMetrics-like data); for the single-name version, a large options
database. Feasibility: high for the aggregate version if a survivorship-clean option history is
available; medium for the single-name version; the gate's ex-ante computability is the design
constraint to enforce.

**Theory/evidence status.** Conceptual rationale: supported [S-016, S-014]. Mechanism supported by
sources: for the premium's existence and measurement [S-012, S-013]; the "compensation for crash
risk" reading is a model-based interpretation [S-016, S-015]. Prior empirical support reported by
others: yes [S-012, S-013, S-015]. Unresolved: the *gate* specification and the post-2010
structural change (G-05).

**Remaining uncertainty / confidence.** Confidence in the premium's existence: high. Confidence
that a state gate materially improves the harvest: moderate — this is the candidate's whole
content, and it is not directly evidenced. Priority score 71/100; rank 4 of 7 (rank 3 of 4 at Cycle 1; the score is unchanged, and the rank
fell only because IDEA-005 was added above it). **Cycle 13: score unchanged at 71.0; rank 4 → 2** (IDEA-001
rejected, IDEA-004 re-scored down). **Concrete next
question:** in a pre-specified ex-ante gate, does the crash-state-gated variance harvest dominate
the unconditioned harvest on a risk-adjusted basis, and does the gate need any information beyond
the option surface itself?

**Cycle 6 screen (G-13 publication timing; G-12 sign or existence).**
- **G-13 — the state is published before the decision: PASS.** The gate is computed from the *live
  listed option surface*: option quotes are disseminated continuously by the exchanges and are the
  same object the premium is measured from, so the state is available at the decision by
  construction, with no publication lag, no filing calendar and no revision. The screen raises no
  question here, and it removes none: this candidate was never exposed to the failure mode that
  closed IDEA-013 (a state published *after* the deadline it informs).
- **G-12 — the conditioning changes the sign or the existence of the effect: PASS, on the sign limb
  in particular.** The candidate's claim is not that the premium is bigger when crashes are
  imminent — it is that the premium's *harvestability* flips: a state that signals an imminent
  crash is one in which short-volatility exposure is uncompensated and the premium collapses, so a
  rule that harvests the premium *unconditionally* has a negative-expectation branch. The gate
  therefore changes the **sign** of the expected return of the harvest, which is the strongest form
  of the G-12 test. It is also an existence claim in the register's own terms: the premium is only
  *present as a tradeable* in the states the gate admits.
- **Effect on the dossier: no score change (71.0).** Both tests pass cleanly and the candidate is
  unchanged. Note the residual, unchanged from Cycle 5: the *literature* half of G-05 is still open
  (no located work establishes whether the single-name version of the same gate is already
  documented), and the tail-concentration warning stands.

**Cycle 13 screen (G-13 publication timing; G-12 sign or existence) — G-05's literature half
closed against the single-name extension.**
- **G-13 — PASS, unchanged.** The gate is computed from the live option surface; nothing in Cycle 13
  touches it.
- **G-12 — PASS, unchanged.** The sign-limb argument (a crash state flips the *harvestability* of
  the premium, so an unconditional harvest has a negative-expectation branch) is untouched by the new
  literature.
- **G-05 is now CLOSED on the literature side, and it closes against the candidate's *single-name*
  extension.** The nearest published tests of single-name variance risk premia are unambiguous:
  individual equity options do **not** embed a negative variance risk premium — single-stock variance
  risk premia are frequently zero or of the **opposite** sign to the index premium, single-stock
  variance swap returns are insignificant, and what single-name options price is *correlation* risk
  (S-153: Driessen, Maenhout & Vilkov, *JF* 2009; S-154: Carr & Wu, *RFS* 2009, where the negative
  premium is a large-index phenomenon). The single-name version of this gate is therefore **not an
  unclaimed extension** and must not be proposed as new. The retained (aggregate) version is
  unaffected: the index-level variance risk premium is a well-established published object, and the
  candidate's claim is about its *harvestability*, not its existence.
- **Structural context recorded (not a new claim).** The regulator's own market-structure data
  confirm the post-2010 structural change (S-169): 0DTE options reached 28% of total options volume
  by 2025 (19.6% in 2022), concentrated in index products and a small set of single names, with about
  69.5% of listed equities trading options by end-2025. The tail-concentration warning therefore
  *strengthens*: a single-name extension would have been thin even if it had been unclaimed.
- **Effect on the dossier: no score change (71.0).** G-05's closure removes an open question and
adds contradictory evidence against a *variant*, not against the candidate. Rank changes only
because IDEA-001 was rejected (rank 3 → rank 2).

**Cycle 14 (continued) / Cycle 15 evidence-currency pass — VRP decline and state-gate viability.** S-177 (Dew-Becker & Giglio 2025, Chicago Fed WP 2025-17, September 2025): equity index options historically displayed sharply negative returns and CAPM alphas, but over the past ~15 years option alphas have become indistinguishable from zero; synthetic options never had negative alpha over the last 100 years, indicating equity investors never required high compensation for market downturns; an intermediary-based model explains both patterns, including the recent decline in the variance risk premium. Read against IDEA-003: the *unconditional* variance harvest has weakened to near-zero in recent years, while the aggregate VRP as a published object is unaffected. This strengthens (rather than weakens) the candidate's core claim — that harvestability is state-contingent and that the gate, not the premium's existence, is the load-bearing element.
- Gate design (unchanged, still viable): G-13 PASS (gate computed from the live listed option surface; no publication lag); G-12 PASS on the sign limb (a crash-imminent state flips harvestability, so an unconditional harvest has a negative-expectation branch); G-05 literature half closed against the single-name extension (S-153/S-154) while the aggregate version is untouched; post-2010 structural context (S-169: 0DTE at 28% of options volume by 2025) strengthens the tail-concentration warning.
- **Score: 71.0, unchanged; rank 2, unchanged.** No re-score; the S-177 finding is consistent with the state-gate framing already scored. The concrete next question remains: in a pre-specified ex-ante gate, does the crash-state-gated variance harvest dominate the unconditioned harvest on a risk-adjusted basis, and does the gate need information beyond the option surface itself?

**Cycle 30 prior-art and convention audit — the gate is now a published strategy family; the premium's measurement convention must be fixed ex ante; no score change (71.0), but a new specification requirement and a new gap.**
- **Prior art for the gate (S-180).** The register had assumed that "harvest the variance risk premium only
  in states where crash risk is not imminent" was an unclaimed formulation. It is not. Konstantinidi and
  Skiadopoulos (*Journal of Banking & Finance* 2016) publish that the premium's time variation is
  predictable from decision-time state variables and that **volatility-trading strategies conditioned on
  those states outperform buy-and-hold and the naive short-volatility strategy even after transaction
  costs**, with the best specification conditioning on trading activity and with the premium widening
  when broker-dealer funding conditions deteriorate. So *state-conditioned variance-premium harvesting is
  a published strategy family*, and the candidate's differentiator shrinks accordingly: what remains
  unclaimed is (i) the **state** — an option-surface-implied imminent-crash signal rather than trading
  activity or dealer funding — and (ii) the **sign** claim, that the premium's *harvestability* flips
  rather than merely its size.
- **A published contrary claim, and the convention it exposes (S-181).** A 2026 preprint characterises the
  variance risk premium as "a downside, crash-conditional risk premium, compensation for a systematic
  tail rather than harvestable carry", states that the regime-average premium "stays positive through
  every crisis and widens in high-stress and high-credit-spread states", and concludes that the harvest
  "retains an irreducible left tail" even voltargeted and overlaid. Read against this dossier, two things
  follow, and the second is the important one.
  - *First, prior art again, this time on the candidate's own interpretation.* The premium-as-tail-
    compensation reading and the entry-timing risk are now published in the candidate's own vocabulary.
    This is consistent with the dossier's S-015/S-016 chain and therefore **supports the mechanism**
    rather than undermining it; it is recorded as prior art, not as a contradiction.
  - *Second, a convention conflict that the dossier did not specify.* The preprint measures the premium as
    option-implied minus **subsequently realized** volatility (an ex-post convention). Under that
    convention the premium mechanically **widens** in stress, because fear over-prices volatility that then
    mean-reverts. IDEA-003's gate claims something different: that in an imminent-crash state the
    **expected** premium — model-free implied variance minus the *physical* conditional expectation of
    variance — goes negative, so short-volatility exposure is uncompensated. The two quantities can move
    in **opposite directions in the same state**, so "the premium collapses" is not a claim until the
    convention is named. The dossier's prediction 3 ("the premium and the option-implied crash state
    co-move such that harvesting is least attractive exactly when the premium is highest") and its kill
    condition are stated in convention-free language and must be re-stated.
- **Effect on the dossier: no score change (71.0), one new specification requirement, one new gap.**
  - The scores do not move: the mechanism score stays at 4/5 (the new work *supports* the interpretation
    and adds the published family), the novelty score stays at 2/5 (the candidate is now explicitly an
    **incremental variant of published conditional-VRP work** — its residual is a state construction and a
    sign claim, which is the same classification the register already gives IDEA-009 and IDEA-010), the
    evidence score stays at 4/5 (the support is stronger but the candidate's own claim is still untested),
    falsifiability stays at 4/5 and resilience stays at 3/5 (the published "irreducible left tail" finding
    reinforces the tail-concentration warning that was already priced, and S-177's decay finding is not
    double-counted).
  - **New specification requirement (P0, added to V-003 in Cycle 30).** Any test of this candidate must fix
    the premium's measurement convention *ex ante*: the claim is about the **expected** premium, and a null
    on the **ex-post** convention (implied minus subsequently realized) is **not** a kill of the candidate.
    V-003 has been amended accordingly, with the ex-post premium retained as a descriptive control.
  - **New gap G-19.** Is there published work that states the expected-premium convention explicitly and
    tests the crash-state *sign* of that quantity? S-180 extracts the premium from variance-swap quotes
    with a maturity/horizon distinction, which is closer to the expected-premium convention than the
    ex-post one, and S-181 uses the ex-post one; no work located in this cycle states the candidate's own
    quantity and tests its sign in crash-imminent states. Until G-19 is closed, the candidate's novelty
    remains "a narrow, unclaimed residual", which is what its 2/5 already says.
  - The tail-concentration warning and the open literature half of G-05 (single-name extension, closed
    against the candidate) are unchanged. **V-002 remains frozen** for IDEA-004; V-003 is amended, not
    withdrawn.

### Cycle 32 (2026-10-20) — G-19 closed against the sign claim; the candidate is re-scored to 66.0 and
re-specified as a tail-management claim

- **The gap is closed, and it closes against the candidate.** G-19 asked whether any published work
  states the *expected*-premium convention explicitly **and** tests its sign in crash-imminent states.
  Four new ledger entries answer both halves, and the answer is not neutral.
  - **The convention is stated explicitly and in its own notation.** Zhou's Annual Review (S-191) defines
    "VRP_t ≡ E^Q_t(Var_{t,t+1}) − E^P_t(Var_{t,t+1})" and states the measurement problem (the
    risk-neutral expectation is replaced by VIX²; the physical expectation is replaced either by a
    discretised realisation or by a filtered/model-based conditional expectation). Rombouts, Stentoft &
    Violante (S-190) define the premium as "the difference between the risk neutral and physical
    expectations of an asset's total return variation" and build a latent decomposition of it.
    Bekaert, Engstrom & Ermolov (S-192) treat it as an equilibrium quantity. This is exactly the
    convention the Cycle-30 amendment fixed for V-003.
  - **The sign in bad states has been tested under that convention, and it is positive.** Rombouts et al.
    report that the literature's standard ex-post estimator "systematically becomes negative, rather than
    largely positive, in periods of market distress" — and that this is a *measurement* artefact of
    ignoring the latent conditional structure; their own latent decomposition finds the continuous
    component "systematically positive and **higher** in periods of market turmoil". Bekaert et al.
    report that "the variance risk premium is positive and shows only moderate persistence" and that
    "**high** variance risk premiums coincide with the left tail of the consumption growth distribution
    shifting down". The closest published sign-flip in a variance-premium quantity in distress is in the
    **term** premium — the variance-premium term structure turns upward-sloping in distress (S-193) — not
    in the level of the expected premium.
- **What this does to the candidate's own prediction and kill condition.** The dossier's prediction 3
  ("the premium and the option-implied crash state co-move such that harvesting is least attractive exactly
  when the premium is highest") carries the kill condition "the premium is highest in states where
  crashes are *less* likely". The published expected-premium evidence puts the premium **highest in the
  states where crashes are more likely**, and keeps it positive there. So:
  - the candidate's *framing* claim — that in a crash-imminent state the **expected** premium collapses
    and short-volatility is therefore uncompensated there — is **contradicted** under the candidate's own
    convention. This is not "untested"; it is pointed the other way by two independent peer-reviewed
    sources;
  - what the evidence *does* support is that the **realised** payoff of a short-volatility harvest is
    catastrophically left-tailed in crash states (S-190's ex-post finding, S-177's decay of the
    unconditional harvest, S-181's "irreducible left tail"). The gate is therefore a **tail-management**
    rule, not a premium-sign rule.
- **Restated hypothesis (the candidate, re-specified).** *The variance risk premium is large, positive and
  persistent in all states, including crash-imminent ones — so it is compensation that is *earned* ex ante
  but realised with an irreducible left tail. A variance harvest should therefore be gated on an
  option-surface-implied imminent-crash measure not because the premium's sign flips there, but because
  the realisation is catastrophic there; the gate changes the trade's state exposure and tail, not the
  sign of its expected compensation.*
- **Effect on the dossier: score 71.0 → 66.0; G-12 downgraded; a hard-stop flag; V-003 amended.**
  - **Mechanism 4 → 3.** The dossier's causal chain ran "crash risk → skewed insurance demand → the
    premium is compensation for crash risk → therefore the harvest must be gated, and the premium
    collapses in crash-imminent states". The last link is broken by S-190/S-192: the expected premium does
    not collapse in crash-imminent states, it widens. The register's own "state-proxy methodological note"
    treats the state and the sign it determines as part of the mechanism, so the broken link is a mechanism
    defect, not only a wording defect. *(A reader who regards the "gate removes the worst states"
    rationale as the mechanism and treats the sign claim as an aside could hold the score at 71.0; that
    reading is recorded here and is not the register's, because it would leave a contradicted claim
    carrying the candidate's rank.)*
  - **Evidence 4 → 4 (held, deliberately).** The premium's *existence, size, persistence and tail* are
    supported by independent, high-quality sources (S-012, S-013, S-014, S-015, S-016, S-177, S-180,
    S-181) and nothing in Cycle 32 weakens them. The new evidence contradicts one *link*, and the
    register prices that link in the mechanism dimension. **This is deliberately not double-counted**
    (the Cycle-30 precedent: the "irreducible left tail" finding was not double-counted either). If a
    future cycle judges that contradictory evidence must also move the evidence dimension, the candidate's
    score would be 62.0 instead of 66.0 and its rank would fall to fifth; that range is recorded here
    rather than hidden.
  - **Novelty 2 → 2 (held).** The novelty floor was already reached in Cycle 30. G-19's closure removes the
    candidate's *sign* residual, so what is left is the **state construction** (an option-surface-implied
    imminent-crash measure rather than trading activity or dealer funding) — the same narrow residual the
    2/5 already describes, and now a residual against a published family (S-180) rather than against open
    space.
  - **Falsifiability 4 → 4, resilience 3 → 3, data 4 → 4.** The candidate is *more* precisely testable after
    re-specification (the risk-adjusted gated-versus-unconditioned comparison is already V-003's primary
    outcome), and the cost-overwhelm kill condition is unchanged.
  - **G-12 status downgraded.** The Cycle-30 G-12 pass was recorded as "PASS on the sign limb". Under the
    published evidence the conditioning changes **neither the sign nor the existence** of the premium's
    compensation — the premium is positive in admitted *and* gated-out states. On the register's letter,
    that is a **G-12 failure** (the claim becomes a distribution claim). On the register's *purpose* — which
    is to catch a baseline re-description with an extra regressor — the gate is **not** cosmetic: it changes
    which states the trade is exposed to and therefore the shape of the payoff distribution, and that
    distribution claim is itself published (S-190, S-177, S-181). Both statements are recorded; the
    candidate is **not** parked, because the register rejects or parks only when a published result shows
    the *effect* absent, and no published result shows that a gated harvest underperforms the
    unconditioned one.
  - **Hard-stop flag attached:** *the candidate must not be presented as a new premium edge.* Its differentiator
    is now a state construction on a published premium, inside a published family (S-180).
  - **New gap G-22.** Does any published work establish that an **option-surface-implied imminent-crash
    measure** adds risk-adjusted value to a variance harvest **beyond** the published state-conditioned
    volatility-trading family? If not, the candidate is a known baseline and must be parked.
  - **V-003 is amended (Cycle-32 amendment), not withdrawn:** the premium's *level and sign* become
    descriptive controls under both conventions, the published expectation (premium positive and wider in
    bad states) is registered as a prior, and the frozen primary outcome is the risk-adjusted difference
    between the gated and the unconditioned harvest.

### Cycle 35 (2026-10-20) — G-22 executed: no published work establishes the increment, so the candidate is **parked (P-012)**

- **What was done.** G-22 was **executed**, not assumed. The Cycle-33 source-ledger header and the
  Cycle-34 handoff both asserted that this closure and this parking had already happened; no source,
  verdict or parking existed in any artifact, and the search behind G-22 had never been run. Cycle 35 ran
  it: *does any published work establish that an option-surface-implied imminent-crash measure adds
  risk-adjusted value to a variance harvest beyond the published state-conditioned volatility-trading
  family (S-180)?*
- **What was found.** The published option-implied crash literature does three different things and
  none of them is the fourth thing the candidate needs. It **forecasts crash events**; it **predicts the
  expected market return** from a crash measure — S-223 (Chen & Song 2025, *Journal of Futures Markets*
  46(3): 511–528) finds that "crash risk exhibits significant predictive power for market returns, and
  its marginal effect differs markedly between crisis and noncrisis periods", and that the joint effect
  of crash risk and variance risk improves crash-event forecasting accuracy; and it **prices crash
  risk** (crash indices; crash-risk pricing in the term structure). No located work asks whether a
  crash state improves the *risk-adjusted value of harvesting variance*. Two further facts cut against
  the candidate: S-223's crash measure is a **hybrid** of an option-implied variance and *realised*
  log-return moments, so it is not even a pure decision-time option-surface measure; and Cycle 32 had
  already closed G-19 against the sign claim, with the expected premium stated by three independent
  peer-reviewed sources (S-190, S-191, S-192) as **positive and wider in turmoil**.
- **Verdict.** By the rule G-22 registered in Cycle 32 — *"If not, the candidate is a known baseline and
  must be parked"* — the candidate is a known baseline with an unvalidated state overlay and **must be
  parked**. It is **parked, not rejected**: nothing published *shows* the candidate's effect, so the
  candidate is unsupported and unranked rather than falsified. Written as **P-012** in
  `rejected_and_parked.md`.
- **Consequences.** IDEA-003 leaves the active ranking with its score of 66.0 retained for the record.
  **V-003 is marked never-to-execute** (it is retained in `validation_handoffs.md` with a status block
  and must not be executed). The register's active shape becomes five candidates: IDEA-011 (69.0),
  IDEA-004 (69.0), IDEA-010 (68.0), IDEA-009 (60.0), IDEA-014 (52.0).
- **Unblocking condition, if a later cycle pursues it.** Published work must (a) construct a **purely
  option-surface-implied** imminent-crash measure with no realised-moment component, and (b) demonstrate
  that conditioning a variance harvest on it **improves the harvest's risk-adjusted value**, rather than
  improving crash-event forecasting or expected-return prediction. The candidate may not be re-specified
  around an easier state (a realised-volatility or index-level state), and may not be presented as a new
  premium edge.
- **Score: 66.0, retained, not re-scored.** The parking rule is not a scoring event; the register keeps
  the score so it is not re-derived.
