## IDEA-010 — CREDIT-ETF: corporate-bond ETF flow against dealer balance-sheet capacity

- **ID / short name:** IDEA-010 / CREDIT-ETF
- **Date added:** 2026-10-19 · **Cycle:** 4 · **Status:** shortlisted · **Version:** 1.1
- **Change notes:** v1.0 — initial dossier. New mechanism family not previously in the register.
  **Cycle 6 — re-scored 68.0 → 65.0:** the mechanism's core (AP inventory and balance-sheet
  constraints driving ETF–NAV dislocations) is published institutionally (S-123), so novelty falls
  3 → 2; G-13 passes only partially, because the *differentiating* state is commercial and the
  published substitutes are unvalidated (gap G-16). Retained, not shortlisted; no validation
  specification until G-16 is closed.
  **Cycle 32 — re-scored 65.0 → 68.0 and V-010 written:** G-16 closed in the candidate's favour on a
  reformulated validation target (S-194's AP regulatory capital ratios, disclosed in SEC regulatory
  filings), with three published rivals (S-196, S-197, S-199).
  **v1.1 (Cycle 35) — G-23 closed in the candidate's favour:** the capital state is quarterly and
  lagged (S-225, S-226), but the claim never required the state to change inside the holding window,
  so the identification is re-anchored to a cross-sectional contrast, the 1–10 session horizon is
  preserved, the signedness becomes the first pre-registered killable test, and G-27 (effective sample
  and per-AP filing comparability) is opened. V-010 amended; no score change (68.0).
  **Cycle 52 — no score change (68.0), rank 3 unchanged.** G-30's status was **re-derived** rather than
  carried forward: all four sub-routes remain closed (regulatory — Cycle 41; sponsor-published lists —
  Cycles 42–43; non-US/UCITS issuers — Cycle 42; exchange listing rules — Cycle 43), no new route was
  identified in Cycles 44–51 or in Cycle 52, **no statutory filing was re-run**, and **no crowding proxy
  was substituted**. G-30 is therefore **exhausted**, not open, and **V-010 remains non-executable**
  against the corporate-bond ETF universe. The Cycle-52 R-032 screen added a sixth hard-stop condition:
  **the state used must not be a public real-time by-product of the flow it is supposed to predict**
  (S-260, S-261). The state remains **annual** (9–14 months stale at any decision date).

**One-sentence hypothesis.** When corporate-bond ETF redemptions force authorised participants (who are also bond dealers) to absorb illiquid bonds onto already-constrained balance sheets, the ETF–NAV discount widens and the underlying bonds cheapen; the state (AP balance-sheet stress and ETF flow) is published, and the trade supplies the liquidity the AP cannot.

**Plain-language description.** Corporate-bond ETFs create and redeem shares through authorised participants — typically big bond dealers. When investors redeem ETF shares, the AP has to take the underlying bonds onto its own balance sheet temporarily. If the AP's balance sheet is already tight (dealers are constrained), the AP will demand a bigger discount to absorb those bonds, so the ETF trades at a wider discount to NAV and the underlying bonds cheapen. A participant who supplies the liquidity the AP cannot — buying the cheapened bonds or the discounted ETF — earns the concession when the AP's balance-sheet stress eases and the discount narrows.

**Mechanism.** APs use creations/redemptions to manage balance sheets; when balance-sheet space is scarce and bonds are costly to intermediate, APs become liquidity seekers in the ETF, widening the ETF–NAV gap and cheapening the underlying bonds (Pan & Zeng 2019). Dealer balance-sheet costs and interdealer capacity are published/measurable proxies (Eisfeldt, Herskovic & Liu 2024). The decision-time state is observable: ETF creation/redemption baskets and AP-level balance-sheet proxies are reported with known lags; the flow itself (ETF shares outstanding change) is published daily.

**Expected return source.** Compensation for bearing intermediary balance-sheet risk in the bond–ETF basis, harvestable when the gap exceeds the cost floor; the basis mean-reverts as AP capacity recovers.

**Originality.** Closest work: Pan & Zeng 2019 (AP balance-sheet constraints limit ETF arbitrage); Eisfeldt et al. 2024 (interdealer price dispersion / dealer capacity). Differentiator: a *state-conditioned, two-legged* specification keyed to published AP-stress proxies and a pre-scheduled exit, rather than a static basis trade. Honest classification: informed synthesis; the joint conditioning is an unresolved conjecture. Confidence: moderate.

**Target scope.** US investment-grade and high-yield corporate bond ETFs vs their creation baskets; daily state; holding 1–10 sessions; regime conditions: stressed dealer capacity, normal-to-high redemption flow.

**Observables (decision time).** ETF shares outstanding change (daily, published); ETF–NAV discount; AP balance-sheet proxies (dealer positions, interdealer price dispersion, SRF take-up); bond liquidity proxies (TRACE volume, dealer inventory); macro release calendar.

**Falsifiable predictions.** (1) ETF–NAV discount widens when AP-stress proxies are high and ETF flow is negative. (2) The discount mean-reverts within 1–10 sessions. (3) The effect is absent when AP capacity is ample (control). (4) Gross discount exceeds round-trip cost in selected ETFs. Kill: no state-dependence, no reversion, or effect below cost.

**Confounders.** Fundamental bond re-pricing during stress; index-rebalance flows; redemption-driven forced selling that is permanent, not temporary; dealer inventory accounting changes.

**Data requirements.** ETF prices/NAV, creation/redemption baskets, TRACE bond data, dealer position proxies, AP balance-sheet data. Feasibility: medium (TRACE is public; AP-level data is commercial).

**Theory/evidence status.** Conceptual rationale supported (Pan & Zeng 2019; Eisfeldt et al. 2024). Joint relation unresolved conjecture. Priority score 68/100; rank TBD.

**Constraints.** Cost/liquidity: corporate bond ETFs and their underlying bonds are less liquid than equities; the spread is crossed twice (ETF and bond); the discount itself is the edge, so transaction costs are first-order. Capacity: bounded by the size of the ETF's creation/redemption flow and by the liquidity of the underlying bonds; high-yield ETFs have the widest discounts but the thinnest underlying markets. Financing/margin: bond positions require margin; a leveraged basis trade consumes balance sheet exactly when dealer capacity is tight — the same constraint that generates the signal. Venue/operational: TRACE is public but with a lag; creation/redemption baskets are published daily by the ETF sponsor; AP-level balance-sheet data is commercial or inferred from dealer disclosures. Shorting constraints: not a borrow-constrained trade, but shorting corporate bonds is costly and recall-risk exposed.

**Regime sensitivity, tail exposure, crash behavior, hidden beta.** Regime: the candidate predicts an edge only when dealer balance-sheet capacity is constrained; in normal times the ETF–NAV basis is narrow and the edge is absent. Tail: a credit stress event widens spreads permanently (fundamental re-pricing, not temporary discount), and the position is long illiquid bonds into a falling market. Crash behaviour: in a dealer balance-sheet crisis (e.g. March 2020), the basis can blow out and not mean-revert within the holding window; the AP may stop making markets entirely. Hidden beta: the position is long corporate credit and short ETF shares — it loads on credit spread duration and on the dollar (for USD-denominated bonds), and can carry event risk from issuer-specific news. Correlated risks: all bond ETFs in the same credit tier share the same dealer-capacity state, so the book is a single credit-cycle bet.

**Remaining uncertainty / confidence.** Confidence that AP balance-sheet constraints widen ETF–NAV discounts: **moderate** (Pan & Zeng 2019 documents the mechanism; dealer capacity is documented by Eisfeldt et al. 2024). Confidence that a tradeable two-legged rule can be built on published AP-stress proxies: **low-to-moderate** — AP-level data is commercial, the discount mean-reversion is unproven at the daily frequency, and the cost floor in thin bond markets is binding. Priority score **68/100**; rank TBD. **Concrete next question:** does the ETF–NAV discount in corporate-bond ETFs widen predictably when published AP-stress proxies are high and ETF flows are negative, and does it mean-revert within 1–10 sessions after costs?

**Cycle 6 screen (G-13 publication timing; G-12 sign or existence).**
- **G-13 — the state is published before the decision: PARTIAL PASS, and the partial pass is
  decisive for the score.** The *flow* leg is clean: ETF shares outstanding are published daily by
  the sponsor, and creation/redemption baskets are published daily by the sponsor, so the flow
  state is a decision-time published quantity. The *differentiating* leg — **AP balance-sheet
  stress** — is not published by any institution in the form the dossier specifies (AP-level
  balance-sheet data is commercial). The dossier's own Observables line already substitutes
  *proxies* for the AP state (dealer positions, interdealer price dispersion, SRF take-up); those
  proxies are computable from published data (TRACE-based measures; the Federal Reserve's standing
  repo facility usage), but they are **different constructs** and have not been validated against
  any dealer- or AP-balance-sheet measure. Under the register's own rule, re-specifying around an
  unvalidated proxy is not permitted, so the candidate is retained only with an explicit
  precondition — gap **G-16**: validate the published proxy set against a dealer-balance-sheet
  measure *before* any specification is written.
- **G-12 — the conditioning changes the sign or the existence of the effect: PASS on the existence
  limb, but the mechanism's core is already published.** Prediction (3) is an existence claim —
  "the effect is absent when AP capacity is ample" — and prediction (1) is a joint conditioning
  claim, so the candidate passes G-12 in form. But the *mechanism* it conditions is not new:
  the FCA's own study documents the connection between ETF mispricing and AP inventory (S-123),
  which is already in the register's **baseline** table, and the baseline table's own note records
  that the FCA had to use a regulator's MiFID II database to see AP inventory at all. So the
  candidate's residual is the *joint state plus a trade specification*, not a new mechanism.
- **Score: 68.0 → 65.0.** Novelty **3 → 2** — the mechanism's core (AP inventory and balance-sheet
  constraints driving ETF–NAV dislocations) is published institutionally (S-123), which is what a
  novelty score of 3 was implicitly denying. Mechanism 4, evidence 3, falsifiability 4, resilience 3
  and data 3 unchanged. The unchanged hard-stop flags are the **cost-overwhelm condition** (the
  spread is crossed twice, in thin bond markets, and the discount *is* the edge) and the
  **AP-data-commercial condition**.
- **Effect on the dossier: retained as a candidate at rank 5, with two preconditions.** No
  validation specification is to be written until G-16 is closed; and the "Originality" line is
  re-stated as a refinement of a published institutional baseline rather than as a new mechanism.

**Cycle 13 screen (G-13 publication timing; G-12 sign or existence) — no score change; G-16
narrowed.**
- **G-13 — PARTIAL PASS, unchanged.** The flow leg (ETF shares outstanding and baskets, published
  daily by the sponsor) remains clean; the AP-balance-sheet leg remains commercial.
- **G-12 — PASS on the existence limb, unchanged.** The "effect is absent when AP capacity is
  ample" prediction remains an existence claim.
- **G-16 is narrowed, not closed.** Cycle 13 established that *dealer balance-sheet cost* is now a
  published, modelled channel with a public, decision-time measurement set — the Federal Reserve's
  own notes name the SOMA, the ON RRP facility and the TGA as the channels and use the public
  **TGCR − IORB** spread as the funding-stress measure (S-162), with a second note quantifying the
  sensitivity of repo rates to Treasury issuance as the Fed's balance sheet shrinks (S-161) and an
  IMF working paper identifying TGA flows as a driver of repo-spread swings (S-160). **What still
  does not exist is a validated proxy for *credit*-dealer balance-sheet stress**; the published
  proxies are Treasury-repo funding measures, which are a different construct. G-16 therefore stays
  open with a sharper statement: *validate a public proxy for credit-dealer balance-sheet stress,
  or show that Treasury-repo funding measures are a valid substitute in the credit ETF–NAV
  discount.*
- **Effect on the dossier: no score change (65.0); rank 6 → 4** (rank change is an artefact of
  IDEA-001's rejection and IDEA-004's re-score). No validation specification is written, exactly as
  G-16 requires.

### Cycle 32 (2026-10-20) — G-16 closed in favour on a reformulated validation target; the candidate is
re-scored to 68.0, enters the top three, and receives V-010

- **The gap closes, and it closes better than the register framed it.** G-16 asked whether the public
  dealer-stress proxy set could be *validated against disclosed dealer funding value adjustments*. Cycle
  32 shows that the register had picked the **wrong validation target**, and that the validation it
  wanted has in fact already been done — by independent researchers, on a better target, on exactly the
  link the candidate claims.
  - **The FVA target is unusable, for reasons the register's own ledger already contains.** S-155 records
    that a dealer's funding value adjustment is a peer-reviewed construct but that "the paper is a theory
    of FVAs; it does not publish a continuous, public FVA series, and no such series exists". Disclosed
    dealer FVAs are OTC-derivatives-specific, disclosed quarterly or annually, and frequently immaterial
    at the firms that disclose them. A *daily* public proxy cannot meaningfully be validated against a
    quantity that is published once a year and measures something else. **G-16's original framing was
    wrong; the register says so rather than leaving a gap open on a bad premise.**
  - **The right target is already published and it is public.** Raddatz (S-194) links each US corporate-bond
    ETF to its authorized participants "using U.S. SEC regulatory filings", connects the APs to their
    financial statements, and measures **APs' regulatory capital ratios**. The finding is that the AP
    capital ratio *is* the operative state: ETFs linked to APs at the 25th percentile of regulatory capital
    ratios saw a **0.69 percentage-point decline in arbitrage intensity** during the March 2020
    dash-for-cash, against **0.39 percentage points** for 75th-percentile APs, and "other AP
    characteristics, such as equity-to-assets ratios, cash-to-assets ratios, total assets, or AP activity
    concentration, **do not play a significant role**". This is a *published, independent validation of a
    disclosed state against the ETF-premium/arbitrage relation* — i.e. exactly the exercise G-16 demanded,
    performed with a better construct than the register proposed, and it exonerates the candidate's
    proposed proxy set by showing which AP characteristics do and do not matter.
  - **The register's own proxy set is narrowed by two further published facts.** The dispersion proxy the
    register's dossier leans on (Eisfeldt, Herskovic & Liu 2024 = S-200) is a *monthly* measure —
    "given the infrequent trading of bonds, calculating interdealer price dispersion daily is not
    feasible" — which conflicts with a 1–10 session holding rule; and the same paper finds that
    "equity-weighted averages of dealer-level financial soundness **fail** to capture effects from
    interdealer frictions", so an aggregate dealer-balance-sheet (FR Y-9C-style) leverage proxy is
    published but *invalid* for this purpose. A public aggregate dealer-inventory proxy does exist and has
    been used to infer AP inventories: FR 2004 primary dealer activity disclosures (S-198).
  - **Three published rival explanations now stand against the candidate's interpretation**, and they are
    a direct consequence of G-16 closing: (i) **fractional baskets** — an ETF–NAV gap can arise from
    basket composition with no AP balance-sheet constraint involved (S-196); (ii) **funding segmentation** —
    arbitrage spreads respond to idiosyncratic balance-sheet shocks and intermediaries specialise, so the
    funding source rather than the AP's bond-inventory capacity may drive the spread (S-197); and
    (iii) **client-sourced liquidity discounts** — dealers pay relationship discounts to investors who
    supply liquidity, which is a published market structure of the same "concession for liquidity"
    shape the candidate claims (S-199).
  - **A citation correction.** The register's "Pan & Zeng 2019" is the SSRN revision of a **working
    paper** (S-195: ESRB Working Paper No. 59, December 2017, SSRN 2895478). Its empirical datasets are
    **proprietary** — proprietary AP lists from two ETF sponsors covering ~83% of passive corporate-bond
    ETF assets, a proprietary TRACE build with dealer identifiers, and proprietary creation/redemption
    baskets. That is the empirical provenance of the register's belief that "AP-level data is commercial":
    the *published evidence* is proprietary-based even though the *state construct* is publicly
    reconstructable (S-194).
- **Effect on the dossier: score 65.0 → 68.0; rank 4 → 3; G-16 CLOSED; V-010 written.**
  - **G-16 — CLOSED IN FAVOUR.** The precondition "a validated, public, decision-time proxy for dealer
    balance-sheet stress" is met by published work; the validation target is the AP's filing-disclosed
    regulatory capital ratio. The register's own rule ("no validation specification is to be written
    until G-16 is closed") is therefore satisfied, and **V-010 is written in this cycle**.
  - **Novelty 2 → 1.** The state-to-premium relation that constitutes the candidate's differentiator is
    now published (S-194 identifies the state; S-195 supplies the mechanism), the mechanism's core was
    already published in Cycle 6, and the family is published three more times over (S-196, S-197, S-199).
    The candidate's residual is the *trade specification and the cost floor* — the same "incremental
    variant" classification the register already applies to IDEA-009 and IDEA-003. A novelty score of 1/5
    is the floor and is recorded as such.
  - **Evidence 3 → 4.** Five independent sources now bear on the mechanism and the state (S-194, S-195,
    S-196, S-197, S-198, S-199, S-200), including one peer-reviewed identification of the state (S-194).
  - **Data 3 → 4.** The register's recorded blocker was that "AP-level data is commercial". The *state* is
    not commercial: it is disclosed in SEC regulatory filings. What remains is a **bespoke ETF↔AP mapping**
    from filings, a **filing lag** (quarterly reports against a 1–10 session rule), and a mapping that
    covers only the APs that file. The dimension is raised, not maximised, and the residual costs are
    recorded here and in G-23.
  - **Mechanism 4 → 4, falsifiability 4 → 4, resilience 3 → 3 (unchanged).** The mechanism is unchanged
    and is now *published*, which raises the bar for the candidate's claim rather than lowering it; the
    cost-overwhelm kill condition (thin bond markets, two legs, ETF and bond) is unchanged and is now
    harder to beat, because the published family already trades the same spread.
  - **New gap G-23.** (i) Is the filing-disclosed AP regulatory capital ratio usable at the cadence the
    candidate's rule requires, and (ii) is it a **signed** state rather than only a moderator, given the
    three published rival explanations and the monthly frequency of the published dispersion proxy?

### Cycle 35 (2026-10-20) — G-23 closed in the candidate's favour: the cadence is resolved by re-anchoring the identification, the horizon is preserved, the signedness becomes the first killable test, and the effective sample is registered as the residual (G-27)

- **The cadence facts, established this cycle.** The state is refreshed only when an AP files.
  **S-225** (SEC Form 10-Q General Instruction A.1, with 17 CFR 240.13a-13 and 240.13a-11): a Form 10-Q
  is due for each of the first three quarters within the period set by General Instruction A.1 — 40 days
  after quarter end for large accelerated and accelerated filers, 45 days for all other registrants —
  and the annual report is due 60 / 75 / 90 days after fiscal year end by filer status; 240.13a-13 also
  exempts investment companies, foreign private issuers and asset-backed issuers. **S-226** (Federal
  Reserve FR Y-9C): quarterly, due 40 calendar days after the March, June and September as-of dates and
  45 after December, with the public data released later and revisable until the deadline. So the state
  is **at best quarterly and lagged** — exactly the constraint G-23 was opened for.
- **Why the cadence is *not* fatal, and what it costs instead.** The candidate's claim never required
  the state to change *inside* the holding window: it requires the **level** of the AP set's capital
  ratio to govern the **size** of the flow-induced discount widening and its reversion. A state that is
  constant between filings is fully usable for a claim of that form — and the published relation itself
  (S-194) identifies the state at filing frequency, so the published evidence was never a
  session-frequency state either. Two consequences are now binding on V-010:
  1. **The primary identification is cross-sectional** — weak-capital ETFs versus strong-capital ETFs on
     redemption days, with the state read as of the last filing date and held constant between filings.
     The within-ETF time-series reading is demoted to a **low-power ablation**, to be reported as such
     with its own effective-sample count.
  2. **The effective sample must be reported and "untested" remains a permitted verdict.** The
     informative units are (ETF, filing-date) state observations and the redemption days inside them;
     the counts are small and the March 2020 dash-for-cash episode is the episode in which the published
     state bound (S-194). A null in a small sample must be reported as *untested*, not upgraded into a
     kill.
- **The horizon is preserved and the candidate is not parked.** The 1–10 session exit governs the
  **reversion of the flow-induced widening**; the state's refresh cadence does not enter the exit. No
  horizon extension is authorised and no parking follows from the cadence. The V-010 Cycle-34
  amendment's conditional instruction — "if the 1–10 session rule proves infeasible due to cadence, the
  horizon must be extended or the candidate reconsidered for parking" — is therefore **resolved in the
  candidate's favour** and superseded by the Cycle-35 amendment.
- **Signedness: the literature half closed, the specification half closed.** S-194 establishes a
  **signed, monotone** relation between the AP-set capital state and arbitrage intensity (a 0.69pp
  versus 0.39pp decline in arbitrage intensity for bottom- versus top-quartile capital ratios), so the
  state is signed in the mechanism's direction and not merely a moderator. The residual was never a
  literature question but a specification obligation, and it is closed as one: the signed prediction —
  *on redemption days, weak-capital ETFs' discounts widen more than strong-capital ETFs' discounts* — is
  now the candidate's **first pre-registered, killable test**, measured on the *change* in the ETF–NAV
  discount with ETF fixed effects absorbing the level, and the three rivals are now specified as controls
  on the **interaction** rather than on the level: S-196 (fractional baskets) as a level explanation
  absorbed by fixed effects; S-197 (funding segmentation) controlled for with public funding-state
  variables; S-199 (client liquidity discounts) controlled for by requiring the bond-side cheapening to
  be specific to the ETF's own AP set and redemption days. S-200's monthly dispersion proxy remains a
  robustness check, and aggregate dealer financial-weakness averages remain a documented inferiority.
- **Hard-stop flag added.** The candidate must not be presented as a discovery of the state-to-premium
  relation (published, S-194, S-195), and must not be presented as evidence that a *session-refreshed*
  capital state is tradeable: the state is a **slow filter** and the rule is a cross-sectional contrast.
- **New gap G-27.** Two residual data questions: the **effective-sample counts** above, and the
  **per-AP filing frequency and cross-regime comparability** of the capital ratio (broker-dealer versus
  bank holding company versus asset manager — the firm-level public availability of a broker-dealer's
  regulatory capital ratio was not resolved this cycle). The register must not assume a single
  quarterly cadence for a heterogeneous AP set.
- **Score: 68.0, unchanged.** Mechanism 4 → 4, evidence 4 → 4 (unchanged — no new source bears on the
  state-to-premium relation itself; the new sources establish the *cadence* and the *signedness*), novelty
  1 → 1 (unchanged — the published relation is not the candidate's discovery), falsifiability 4 → 4
  (unchanged — the re-anchoring sharpens the test but does not create a new one), resilience 3 → 3
  (unchanged — the cost-overwhelm kill condition is unchanged), data 4 → 4 (unchanged — the state is
  public in principle; the residual costs are now registered in G-27 rather than in G-23). **Rank 3,
  unchanged.**

#### Cycle 37 block — IDEA-010 · the state-availability residual is CLOSED IN THE CANDIDATE'S FAVOUR

- **What Cycle 37 did.** It executed the check that Cycle 36 declined to execute, and found that
  **Cycle 36's central negative claim was wrong**. Cycle 36 recorded the firm-level public
  availability of broker-dealer regulatory capital as untested, on the stated ground that FOCUS
  reports "are not generally disseminated as regular public datasets in the same way that FR Y-9C or
  call-report data are disseminated". That is a statement about a *dataset*, and it was used as a
  statement about the *data*. It is false: **Form X-17A-5 is a public EDGAR form type, and the
  net-capital figures of broker-dealers are in publicly indexed documents** (S-231, fetched directly;
  6,131 public X-17A-5 documents contain "excess net capital").
- **The route, precisely.** (i) **Private, non-listed APs** — Form X-17A-5 annual filings on EDGAR:
  Citadel Securities (three CIKs; the FY2025 Institutional filing `CSIN_BS_Only_FS_2025.pdf` is
  publicly indexed and matches "excess net capital"), Virtu Americas (FY2017–FY2025, file
  descriptions "PUBLIC REPORT" / "PUBLIC ANNUAL REPORTS"), KCG Americas, AllianceBernstein
  Investments (whose FY2018 document has file type **"COMP NET CAP"**), RFQ-Hub Americas, IEX
  Services (S-231, S-232). (ii) **Listed broker-dealers** — quarterly, XBRL-tagged disclosure of net
  capital, required net capital and excess net capital in Exchange Act periodic reports (S-233:
  LPL Financial's Form 10-Q exhibit R27, as of 31 December 2024). (iii) **Bank APs** — FR Y-9C and
  call reports, already in the register (S-226). (iv) **Routes that are closed**: FINRA's BrokerCheck
  does not release firm-level net capital and FINRA publishes excess net capital only in aggregate by
  firm-size bucket (S-234) — so a specification must use the filings, not FINRA's tables.
- **G-27 closed in the candidate's favour on availability, with three qualifications that are part of
  the verdict.** (a) **Cadence is firm-dependent**: annual for private broker-dealers (fiscal
  year-end, filed February–March, so 40–90 days stale at any decision date), quarterly for listed
  broker-dealers and bank holding companies, and quarterly for at least some private filers (S-231
  records a period ending 30 September 2024) — the register must *measure* it per firm rather than
  assume it. (b) **Format is document-level**: the route requires parsing per firm per year from
  EDGAR, using the public full-text index to locate the documents; this is why the data score does
  not move (available in principle, but not a tidy dataset). (c) **Comparability**: FOCUS net capital
  is a liquidity-style quantity and is not commensurable with a bank holding company's risk-based
  ratios, nor does an asset-manager AP have an equivalent — so the cross-sectional contrast must be
  built within a comparable capital regime, or the mapping must be justified explicitly.
- **G-29 opened — the ETF → AP-set mapping and the coverage count that now bounds the candidate.**
  IDEA-010's identification is a *cross-sectional* contrast, so the state must be attached to an ETF
  through the identity of the firms that arbitrage it. Two facts are needed and only one is
  established: **which corporate-bond ETFs name their authorised participants in a public filing**
  (trade press reported in April 2024 that the iShares Bitcoin Trust prospectus names its APs —
  a lead, not a verified source, and deliberately not entered in the ledger as one; the
  corporate-bond ETF summary prospectuses read in Cycle 37 disclose authorised-participant *risk*
  generically and do **not** name them), and **how many (ETF, AP) cells have both a published AP-set
  membership and a public capital ratio**. The register must not re-specify around a weaker proxy
  (S-026's Days-ADV crowding, S-157's comomentum, S-222's crowding factor): those measure all 13F
  institutions, i.e. directional positioning, not the arbitrage capital the candidate's state is.
- **V-010 amended** (see `validation_handoffs.md`): the capital state must be reconstructed from
  filings with the per-firm cadence measured, the comparison held within a comparable capital regime,
  and the G-23 effective-sample report extended with the G-29 coverage count.
- **Score: 68.0, unchanged. Rank 3, unchanged.** No score moves: the state was already scored as
  public *in principle* (data 4/5), and the finding changes the route's *specificity*, not the
  candidate's novelty, falsifiability or evidence. What changes is the register's knowledge: the
  candidate is no longer blocked by a false negative, and is now blocked by a **countable** one
  (G-29) plus the effective-sample condition already registered.
- **Hard-stop flags added in Cycle 37.** (i) The state may not be described as a tidy public dataset;
  it must be described as **filings**, with per-firm cadence. (ii) The cross-sectional contrast may
  not mix capital regimes without an explicit mapping argument. (iii) The candidate may not proceed
  until the G-29 coverage count is produced, because the effective-sample count depends on it.

### Cycle 40 block — G-29 EXECUTED as a calibrated document-level search; the route is closed, the
### candidate is not; and the state is **annual**, which the candidate's identification now assumes

- **What was executed.** Cycle 40 did not re-read prospectuses. It built a *detector* and checked it
  against a known true positive first. The detector phrase is the naming sentence used by the ETF
  trusts that do publish their APs — "the Authorized Participants that are able to conduct …" — and
  the true positive is the **iShares Bitcoin Trust ETF** (CIK 1980994), whose Form 424B3 prospectuses
  name their APs verbatim: the 9 January 2024 filing names **five** (ABN AMRO Clearing USA LLC, Jane
  Street Capital LLC, JP Morgan Securities LLC, Macquarie Capital (USA) Inc., Virtu Americas LLC), the
  11 April 2024 filing names **nine**, the 5 August 2024 filing names **twelve**, and the 31 July 2025
  filing names **twelve cash APs** (adding BMO Capital Markets Corp., BofA Securities Inc., Citadel
  Securities LLC, Citigroup Global Markets Inc., Jefferies LLC, JP Morgan Securities LLC, Macquarie
  Capital (USA) Inc. and UBS Securities LLC) and **four in-kind APs** (Jane Street Capital, Virtu
  Americas, JP Morgan Securities, Marex Capital Markets Inc.) — **S-239**, now in the ledger, so the
  Cycle-37 trade-press lead is a verified source rather than a lead.
- **What the detector found.** Searching that naming sentence across every prospectus form type in
  the EDGAR full-text index returns **five documents in four filers** — iShares Bitcoin Trust ETF,
  iShares Ethereum Trust ETF, iShares Staked Ethereum Trust ETF and iShares Bitcoin Premium Income
  ETF — and **no corporate-bond ETF**. The complement of the detector was run as well: (i) the
  *phrase-first* route over 591 bond-worded filings containing an AP-naming sentence, in which every
  document that actually names APs in the naming position is a **commodity or crypto trust**
  (iShares S&P GSCI Commodity-Indexed Trust, iShares Gold Trust Micro, World Gold Trust, WisdomTree
  Bitcoin Fund, the Invesco Galaxy, 21Shares, Morgan Stanley digital-asset and T. Rowe Price Active
  Crypto trusts); and (ii) the *name-first* route over bond-worded filings containing any of thirty-one
  AP firm names, in which the names occur **only** as securities-lending "percentage of ownership"
  entries and in the standing "an Authorized Participant may hold of record more than 25 % of the
  outstanding shares" disclosure, and as **revenue-sharing and placement payees** (verified: the
  Pershing, RBC, Schwab, TD Ameritrade, MSWM and Fidelity entries in the SPDR Series Trust SAI). Two
  sponsor product pages were probed directly (iShares AGG, Vanguard BND): both use the generic
  formulation and name no AP; the **unit-investment-trust** class was checked as a separate structure
  (First Trust corporate-bond 497K filings of 13 February 2025 and 2 March 2026) and names no AP firm
  either; and a **second, independent detector** — every filing containing an authorised-participant
  naming position followed by corporate entity names, across sixteen filing form types — was read
  document by document: **3,628 of 4,409 documents read at the end of the cycle, of which 434 name APs,
  and every one of the 434 is a commodity or crypto trust**.
- **The coverage counts the gap asked for.** Corporate-bond ETFs with a published AP set: **0**.
  (ETF, AP) cells with both a published AP set and a public capital ratio: **0**. (ETF, AP, episode)
  cells: **0**. **Verdict: not specifiable from public filings — untested, not dead.** The register
  must not substitute a published crowding proxy (S-026's Days-ADV, S-157's comomentum, S-222's
  crowding factor): those measure all 13F institutions, i.e. directional positioning, not the
  arbitrage capital this candidate's state is.
- **The capital state, re-derived at filer level (G-27 closed).** The phrase corpus contains **6,131
  X-17A-5 documents from 2,419 distinct broker-dealer filers** — a **lower bound**, because the
  filer-level index of the same firms shows how far wording-counts and firm-counts diverge (Citadel
  Securities LLC files 24 X-17A-5 documents over 23 periods and contains the phrase in **one** of
  them). Listed broker-dealers are covered by Form 10-Q (**1,151 documents, 66 filers, quarterly**)
  and Form 10-K (**737 documents, 96 filers, annual**). Bank holding companies are covered by Form
  FR Y-9C, but **FR Y-9C is not indexed by EDGAR's full-text search at all** (zero hits for "capital",
  "net capital" and "Federal Reserve" restricted to that form), so that leg must be enumerated from
  the Federal Reserve's own National Information Center rather than from EDGAR — S-226 restated
  accordingly.
- **Correction to the register's own cadence statement (important for this candidate).** The Cycle-37
  statement that the private-broker-dealer capital state is "annual for most private broker-dealers
  … quarterly for at least some private filers" does not survive filer-level checking. Over the 2,419
  filers in the corpus, **2,414 disclose once per year and five disclose twice per year; not one
  discloses three or more times a year**, and the single quarterly document that produced the
  exception (Agentis Capital Markets US, LLC, period ending 2024-09-30) does not appear in the corpus
  at all. Filer-level enumeration of the **thirteen named AP firms** and of **fifteen major private AP
  firms** found **annual cadence in every case** (fiscal year-end, filed February–March). The state
  this candidate uses is therefore **annual**, and at any decision date it is **9–14 months stale**.
  This *reinforces* rather than undermines the Cycle-35 re-anchoring: the state never changes inside a
  holding window, so the identification must be cross-sectional between AP sets and the time-series
  reading stays a low-power ablation.
- **The arithmetic, on the one ETF whose AP set is published.** 13 distinct named AP firms; **13 of
  13** with a public Form X-17A-5 filing; **285 (AP, filing-date) episode cells**; cadence **annual**
  for all thirteen; **none of the thirteen files a Form 10-Q or 10-K**, so Form X-17A-5 is the *only*
  public capital route for the entire published set. This is the register's first worked example of
  the (ETF, AP, episode) count, and it is also the first demonstration that the private-AP capital
  state is a **slow filter** — exactly the register's standing rule.
- **What is now opened: G-30.** The route closed is the **statutory filing** route. It is not closed
  for all primary sources: a sponsor's own published participant list, an exchange or regulatory
  disclosure requirement, or a non-US issuer's prospectus could still disclose a corporate-bond ETF's
  AP set. **G-30 asks for a specific route, not another re-reading of prospectuses.**
- **Effect on V-010.** V-010 is **amended, not withdrawn**: it may not be executed against a
  corporate-bond ETF state, because no such attachable state exists; it remains executable only if a
  specifiable route emerges (G-30), and it must report the effective-sample denominator in the form
  established here (cells per (ETF, AP set) per filing date, with the cadence stated).
- **Score: 68.0, unchanged. Rank 3, unchanged.** No score moves. The Cycle-37 finding was a negative
  about the *route*; Cycle 40 converted it into a **calibrated, population-level** negative and
  corrected two of the register's own supporting statements. What changed is the register's knowledge,
  not the candidate's merit.
- **Hard-stop flags added in Cycle 40.** (i) The candidate may not describe the AP capital ratio as
  attachable to a corporate-bond ETF from public filings — **no such attachment exists**. (ii) The
  candidate may not describe the capital state as quarterly: for the private AP set it is **annual**,
  9–14 months stale, and a quarterly reading of it is false. (iii) The candidate may not present the
  state-to-premium relation as its own (unchanged from Cycle 32). (iv) The candidate may not
  substitute any published crowding proxy for the missing ETF → AP mapping. (v) The candidate may not
  use a short-interest-based measure of AP activity as a substitute for the missing mapping (S-241,
  S-242, S-243).

#### Cycles 41–51 consolidated note — IDEA-010 · G-29 closed, G-30 opened and then **exhausted without a re-probe**
- Across Cycles 41–51 the register worked this candidate's route ladder explicitly and, in order: **G-29**
  was executed as a calibrated document-level search (Cycle 40) and the **statutory-filing route closed**;
  **G-30** was opened to ask for a *specific* remaining route — a sponsor's own published participant list,
  an exchange or regulatory disclosure requirement, or a non-US issuer's prospectus. Cycles 41–43 then
  exhausted those three legs: **regulatory — closed** (no SEC, exchange or other rule obliges an issuer to
  publish its ETF's AP set); **sponsor-published lists — not found**; **non-US / UCITS issuers — not
  found**; **exchange listing rules — no public disclosure requirement**. Cycles 44–51 added the family
  screens **R-022 … R-031** and the register-wide evidence-currency passes, and **no new route to a
  corporate-bond ETF's AP set was identified in any of them**. The standing instruction from Cycle 40
  therefore holds: *do not re-run statutory filings, and do not substitute a published crowding proxy*
  (S-026, S-157, S-222 measure different crowds).
- **The state remains annual** (Cycle 40's filer-level finding: 2,414 of 2,419 filers disclose once a year,
  five twice a year, none three or more; all thirteen named AP firms and all fifteen major private AP
  firms are annual, filed February–March), so at any decision date it is 9–14 months stale, and the
  identification is cross-sectional between AP sets.
- **Score: 68.0, unchanged. Rank 3, unchanged.**

#### Cycle 52 block — IDEA-010 · G-30 **exhaustion confirmed, no re-probe**, and V-010 remains non-executable against the corporate-bond ETF universe
- **What Cycle 52 did.** It **re-derived** G-30's status rather than carrying it forward in prose: all four
  legs remain closed (regulatory: Cycle 41; sponsor-published lists: Cycles 42–43; non-US/UCITS issuers:
  Cycle 42; exchange listing rules: Cycle 43), and **no new route was identified in Cycles 44–51 or in
  Cycle 52**. **No statutory filing was re-run in Cycle 52**, and no crowding proxy was substituted.
  **G-30 is exhausted; V-010 remains non-executable against the corporate-bond ETF universe.** V-010 stays
  executable only against a specifiable route, and when it is eventually executed it must report the
  effective-sample denominator in the Cycle-40 form (cells per (ETF, AP set) per filing date, with the
  annual cadence stated).
- **What Cycle 52 added that is *not* about G-30, and it is a rediscovery-guard point for this candidate.**
  The R-032 family screen established the register's general **"published state ⇒ no informational edge"**
  principle on a second, independent body of evidence: the LULD bands are republished to the public every
  30 seconds (S-260) and the EU regime *requires* publication of trading halts (S-261), so the state is a
  broadcast and not a discovery. Applied to IDEA-010 this is a **guard on a substitution the candidate has
  already been warned against**: a hypothetical ETF → AP mapping built from a **published, real-time
  by-product** — for example a venue's published AP identifier codes on executed ETF creations — would not
  be a state that produces an edge; it would be a public record of what has already happened. The
  candidate's edge is claimed to come from **AP balance-sheet capacity**, not from *knowing* the AP set, so
  this does not close the candidate. But it does add a sixth hard-stop condition: **the state used must not
  be a public real-time by-product of the flow it is supposed to predict.**
- **Score: 68.0, unchanged. Rank 3, unchanged.** No source bears on any score component; G-30's status
  changed from "open" to "**exhausted without a re-probe**", which is a change in the register's knowledge
  about the *route*, not about the candidate's merit.
