## IDEA-011 — ACCTCLOCK: accounting-standards adoption as a slow-information-diffusion event clock

- **ID / short name:** IDEA-011 / ACCTCLOCK
- **Date added:** 2026-10-19 · **Cycle:** 4 · **Status:** shortlisted · **Version:** 1.2
- **Change notes:** v1.0 — initial dossier. New mechanism family not previously in the register.
  **Cycle 16 — re-scored 72.0 → 69.0** on post-2010 drift-decay evidence (S-178, S-179), with the
  enforcement-regime concentration of the effect narrowing the tradable drift.
  **v1.2 (Cycle 56) — G-32 limb (a) closed negative as specified, with the reason established; the
  handoff's proposed re-specification rejected on a documented instance; and a different
  re-specification — prospective arm A — specified and carried into V-009.** No score change (69.0),
  rank 1 unchanged. The binding hard stop is **relieved inside arm A only**; the candidate's own
  event-count hard stop is otherwise unchanged.
  **v1.1 (Cycle 35) — G-21 narrowed to a named discriminator:** the only first-adoption case in the
  candidate's direction (Greece, S-188) is attributed to a disclosure shock by its own authors; the
  other first-adoption case runs the opposite way (S-179); the published conditioner is enforcement
  (S-221); and the mandatory-versus-voluntary comparator (S-220) implies the diffusion clock starts
  before the effective date. The residual is a first-adopter versus second-adopter drift comparison
  that has not been run. No score change (69.0); a new hard-stop flag records that the enforcement
  concentration of IFRS-adoption effects is published and is not the candidate's discovery.
  **Cycle 52 — prior-art re-read of the rank-1 candidate; no score change (69.0), rank 1 unchanged.**
  The G-21 null was **re-derived from a check** rather than re-declared for the fourteenth time, and it
  came back **sharper**: the first-adopter/second-adopter contrast is a *standard, populated design* in the
  IFRS literature (run on audit fees and audit time, S-265) and the field's own systematic review of ~181
  top-cited IFRS-adoption papers does **not** cover post-earnings-announcement drift among its reviewed
  outcomes (S-253), so the residual is an *outcome-specific unrun comparison on a standard comparator*.
  A published conditioner that **competes with the candidate's own state** was found — the post-adoption
  information effect **flips sign** with country-level linguistic distance (S-252) — so the candidate's
  conditioning variable must remain **firm-level**, and **enforcement (S-221) and linguistic distance
  (S-252) are now mandatory controls**. Adverse premise evidence was recorded against the
  comparability-as-driver reading (S-253) and the *discontinuity-at-adoption* reading was recorded as
  supporting the premise, so the candidate must **pre-register which reading it claims**. **G-32 was
  opened** — the prospective IFRS 18 adoption window (effective for annual periods beginning on or after
  1 January 2027; S-255) read against the published jurisdiction-profile adoption calendar (170
  jurisdictions; S-254). **V-009 was amended** (not re-created).

**One-sentence hypothesis.** Mandatory adoption of a new accounting standard is a dated, regulator-announced information shock whose market reaction diffuses slowly because investors and analysts must re-learn the new numbers; the drift between the adoption announcement and the first full reporting cycle under the new standard is predictable in direction for firms whose pre-adoption information quality was low, and partly reverses once comparability is priced.

**Plain-language description.** When a regulator requires all companies in a market to switch to a new accounting standard (for example, IFRS in the EU in 2005), the announcement date is known years in advance. But the actual numbers under the new standard only appear in companies' first full financial reports after adoption. Until then, investors and analysts must re-learn what the numbers mean, and that learning happens slowly. Firms whose pre-adoption information quality was low — opaque earnings, few analysts, low foreign ownership — should see a bigger drift as the new standards are progressively understood, and part of that drift should reverse once the new numbers are fully digested and comparability is restored. A participant who positions before the first full reporting cycle under the new standard, sized by pre-adoption information quality, captures the slow-diffusion drift.

**Mechanism.** Mandatory IFRS adoption increased earnings information content and reduced stock-return synchronicity in the adoption year, with effects concentrated in firms with lower pre-adoption information quality and in strong-enforcement countries (Armstrong, Barth, Jagolinzer & Riedl 2010; Beuselinck et al. 2009; Lambertides & Mazouz 2013). The adoption dates are published by regulators (IASB/SEC/FASB) years in advance, so the clock is public and ex-ante knowable. The state (pre-adoption information quality, enforcement regime, analyst following) is observable from published filings.

**Expected return source.** Compensation for bearing slow information diffusion and comparability risk across the adoption window; a drift from announcement to first full reporting cycle, with partial reversal once the new numbers are fully digested.

**Originality.** Closest work: Armstrong et al. 2010; Beuselinck et al. 2009; Christensen, Lee & Walker 2007. Differentiator: a *dated, two-legged trade specification* keyed to the adoption calendar and to a pre-registered information-quality state, rather than an event study. Honest classification: informed synthesis of a documented effect; the two-legged specification is an unresolved conjecture. Confidence: moderate.

**Target scope.** Firms in jurisdictions with a mandated adoption date (EU IFRS 2005; other jurisdictions with announced adoption); announcement-to-first-full-reporting-cycle horizon; regime conditions: strong enforcement, no concurrent macro shocks.

**Observables (decision time).** Adoption announcement date and effective date (regulator-published); pre-adoption information quality proxies (earnings quality, analyst following, foreign ownership); enforcement regime (country-level); firm size; reporting lag.

**Falsifiable predictions.** (1) Low pre-adoption information-quality firms drift more from announcement to first full report under the new standard. (2) The drift is larger in strong-enforcement countries. (3) Part of the drift reverses within 1–4 quarters after the first full report. (4) High pre-adoption quality firms show a smaller, possibly negative, drift (convergence benefit). Kill: no information-quality interaction, no reversal, or effect explained by concurrent macro shocks.

**Confounders.** Concurrent macro/regulatory shocks; selection into adoption; the adoption event itself being a proxy for other reforms; size and liquidity differences; analyst coverage changes independent of the standard.

**Data requirements.** Regulator adoption calendars; firm-level returns; accounting-quality proxies; analyst following; country enforcement indices. Feasibility: high (all public, with a known adoption calendar).

**Theory/evidence status.** Conceptual rationale supported (Armstrong et al. 2010; Beuselinck et al. 2009). Two-legged specification unresolved conjecture. Priority score 64/100; rank TBD.

**Constraints.** Cost/liquidity: the affected firms may be small-cap or less liquid; the spread is crossed twice (entry and exit); the holding horizon is quarters, so financing and borrow costs accumulate. Capacity: bounded by the number of adoption events and by the liquidity of the affected firms; a single jurisdiction's adoption creates only one cross-section of firms. Financing/margin: a long/short book across firms is capital-intensive; borrow on the short leg can be scarce in small-cap names. Venue/operational: adoption dates are published by regulators but may change; the first full reporting cycle under the new standard may not align with the effective date (filing lags). Shorting constraints: short legs in small-cap, low-information-quality firms are the most expensive to borrow.

**Regime sensitivity, tail exposure, crash behavior, hidden beta.** Regime: the candidate predicts an edge only around mandated adoption events; outside adoption windows there is no signal. Tail: the fundamental anchor can break if the adoption itself is delayed, watered down, or accompanied by other reforms — the trade becomes a directional loss unrelated to information diffusion. Crash behaviour: a market-wide crash during the adoption window confounds the diffusion drift with broad re-pricing; the exclusion rule (no concurrent macro shocks) must be applied before any result is read. Hidden beta: the long/short book loads on size, value, and momentum factors; low-information-quality firms tend to be smaller and less liquid. Correlated risks: all firms in one adoption jurisdiction share the same event date; a single jurisdiction-level shock dominates many firm-level results.

**Remaining uncertainty / confidence.** Confidence that accounting-standards adoption changes information quality and is dated in advance: **high** (Armstrong et al. 2010; Beuselinck et al. 2009). Confidence that a tradeable two-legged rule can be built on pre-announced adoption calendars: **moderate** — the state is public, the dates are known, but the drift is slow and the sample of adoption events is small (a handful of jurisdictions). Priority score **64/100**; rank TBD. **Concrete next question:** do firms with low pre-adoption information quality show a predictable drift from the adoption announcement to the first full reporting cycle under the new standard, with partial reversal afterward, after costs?

**Cycle 6 screen (G-13 publication timing; G-12 sign or existence).**
- **G-13 — the state is published before the decision: PASS, and comfortably.** The adoption
  calendars are published by the regulator years in advance, and the dossier's state — pre-adoption
  information quality, enforcement regime, analyst following, foreign ownership — is computed from
  filings that existed *before* the announcement. This is the register's oldest and best-posed
  G-13 case: the decision date, the event date and the state are all fixed and public long before
  any position is taken. There is no publication-versus-decision ordering to check.
- **G-12 — the conditioning changes the sign or the existence of the effect: PASS on the existence
  limb.** Prediction (4) is explicit: "high pre-adoption quality firms show a smaller, possibly
  negative, drift" — i.e. the *drift's existence* is conditioned on the information-quality
  state, and its sign may flip across the state. The published literature (Armstrong et al. 2010;
  Beuselinck et al. 2009) establishes the *information-content* effect of adoption; it does not
  claim a tradable announcement-to-first-report drift, so the candidate is not a re-description of
  published work and the conditioning is not a size condition.
- **Effect on the dossier: no score change (72.0).** Both tests pass. The unchanged hard-stop flag
  is the **adoption-event-sample-small condition**: the number of independent adoption events is
  small, and the cross-section within a jurisdiction is not independent, so the effective sample is
  the binding constraint on this candidate — not its state, not its timing, and not its originality.

**Cycle 13 screen (G-13 publication timing; G-12 sign or existence) — no change; new rank 1.**
- **G-13 — PASS, unchanged.** Nothing in Cycle 13 bears on the publication ordering of the adoption
  calendars; this remains the register's oldest and best-posed G-13 case.
- **G-12 — PASS on the existence limb, unchanged.** The information-quality conditioning of the
  drift's existence is untouched by the new literature.
- **Score: 72.0, unchanged; rank 2 → 1** because IDEA-001 was rejected in Cycle 13 (R-011) and
  IDEA-004 was re-scored down (74.0 → 69.0). The rank change is an artefact of other candidates'
  removal, not new evidence about this one.
- **The binding constraint is unchanged and is now the candidate's *only* real weakness:** the
  adoption-event sample is small, and the effective sample is smaller still. Nothing found in Cycle
  13 (the reconstruction-literature screens, the crowding-proxy literature S-156/S-157/S-172, the
  funding-state literature S-160–S-162) adds evidence either for or against the accounting-adoption
  drift, so this candidate enters Cycle 14 as the register's highest-priority item **with an
  unchanged evidence base and an unchanged hard-stop flag**. The single most valuable thing the next
  cycle could do for it is a *bounded* check of whether the accounting-adoption drift is itself
  documented in the post-2010 sample (the literature of record, Armstrong/Beuselinck et al., is
  largely pre-2015) — a search that has never been run for this candidate and that is the mirror
  image of what killed IDEA-001.

**Cycle 14 (continued) / Cycle 15 evidence-currency pass — post-2010 IFRS/adoption drift.** The post-2010 evidence base was searched for the first time (mirroring the check that killed IDEA-001). Located evidence:
- S-178 (Hung, Li & Wang 2014/2015, RFS 28(4): 1242–1277): PEAD declines after an exogenous improvement in non-US firms' financial reporting quality; the decrease is more pronounced where reporting quality improved most, where institutional holdings are higher, limits-to-arbitrage are lower, and enforcement is stronger; driven by greater reporting changes, increased analyst forecast accuracy, institutional ownership and liquidity. Supports the mispricing/slow-diffusion reading: better information environment → smaller drift.
- S-179 (Song & Woo 2015, K-IFRS, DOI 10.31203/aepa.2015.12.4.006): KRX-listed firms 2006–2013; K-IFRS adoption had a noticeable negative effect on PEAD size; under-reaction decreased; PEAD decreased for firms without analysts' forecasts and with positive unexpected earnings.
- S-175 (Armstrong et al 2010) and S-176 (Beuselinck et al 2009): largely pre-2010 (2005 EU IFRS adoption); establish increased information content/comparability and reduced synchronicity, concentrated in strong-enforcement countries and low pre-adoption quality firms.
- Read as a set, the post-2010 literature indicates IFRS-style adoption improves the information environment and shrinks PEAD magnitude in several settings — consistent with slow-diffusion drift weakening post-adoption, analogous to momentum's documented decay (S-156). This is evidence *against* a persistent, tradable announcement-to-first-report drift in the post-2010 sample, but the literature does not isolate the candidate's exact dated two-legged trade, and the adoption-event sample remains small (hard-stop unchanged).
- **Score: 72.0, unchanged; rank 1, unchanged.** No re-score pending a more targeted post-2010 sample comparison; the post-2010 check is now complete and sharpens (rather than reverses) the prior assessment. The binding constraint remains the small, non-independent adoption-event sample.
- Enforcement regimes: S-178 and S-175/S-176 both find effects concentrated in strong-enforcement countries, so enforcement is a relevant regime condition for any future specification (already in the dossier's observables/target scope).

### Cycle 35 (2026-10-20) — G-21 narrowed to a named discriminator: the Greece case is attributed to a disclosure shock by its own authors, "first adoption" does not predict the sign, and the published conditioner is *enforcement* — not the candidate's information-quality clock

- **Two new sources, and what each does to the candidate.**
  1. **S-220** (Daske, Hail, Leuz & Verdi 2008, *Journal of Accounting Research* 46(5): 1085–1142) is the
     register's first published **mandatory-versus-voluntary adopter** comparator, which is the
     *second-time-adopter* half of the discriminator below. Two of its findings matter. The
     capital-market benefits of IFRS adoption are "most pronounced for firms that voluntarily switch to
     IFRS, both in the year when they switch and again later, when IFRS become mandatory" — firms with
     comparability experience get the *bigger* benefit at the mandate date, which is consistent with a
     comparability channel being operative at that date. And the effects "occur prior to the official
     adoption date", which means the candidate's diffusion clock must be defined to begin *before* the
     effective date, not at it — a specification consequence for the candidate's calendar.
  2. **S-221** (Christensen, Hail & Leuz 2013, *Journal of Accounting and Economics* 56(2–3, Suppl. 1):
     147–177) decomposes the EU liquidity benefits of mandatory IFRS adoption and finds them "limited to
     five EU countries that made substantive changes in their enforcement of financial reporting around
     the same time IFRS became mandatory". **This is a published conditioner that competes with the
     candidate's own**: the candidate's prediction 2 conditions the drift on the enforcement regime
     (S-175, S-176, S-178 all find the same concentration), and that is now published as the operative
     variable for post-adoption market outcomes.
- **The Greece case, read against its own authors.** S-188 (Forbes & Giannopoulos) attributes the Greek
  post-adoption drift **increase** to IFRS **reducing earnings predictability** — reduced accounting
  conservatism and higher earnings volatility — which is a *disclosure shock*, not a learning or
  comparability clock. So the only first-adoption case in the candidate's direction is explained by a
  rival mechanism **by the authors of that very case**. And the other first-adoption case runs the other
  way: K-IFRS adoption **reduced** post-adoption drift (S-179). "First adoption" therefore does not
  predict the sign of the drift effect, and the candidate's mechanism has **no published case in which
  post-adoption drift increased under a mechanism attributable to a comparability clock**.
- **The discriminator is now named and specified (G-21 narrowed, not closed).** A comparability clock
  predicts that the drift increase appears in **first-time adopters** of a standard whose transition
  creates a comparability break, and is **absent or reversed in second-time adopters** — firms or
  jurisdictions already reporting under IFRS-equivalent standards (Australia and Canada 2004–05, South
  Africa 2005, the EU's 2005 voluntary adopters). A generic "IFRS changes the numbers" effect (S-188's
  own reading) predicts both groups move together. No study located in this cycle runs that comparison
  on drift; the studies that run the mandatory-versus-voluntary comparison (S-220, S-221) do so on
  liquidity, cost of capital and valuation. The residual is therefore no longer "is the Greece result
  Greece-specific?" but the specific, executable comparison above.
- **Hard-stop flag added.** The candidate must not be presented as discovering that enforcement
  concentrates IFRS-adoption market effects: that is published (S-221). The candidate's residual is the
  *information-quality* conditioning of a *drift* effect, and the candidate must also accommodate the
  anticipation finding (S-220) — the clock starts before the effective date.
- **Score: 69.0, unchanged.** Mechanism 4 → 4, evidence 3 → 3 (unchanged — the two new sources support
  the *mechanism* and the *enforcement* limb, but the candidate's directional drift claim still has no
  supporting published case, and the *content* of the evidence base has changed rather than its
  quantity), novelty 3 → 3 (unchanged), falsifiability 4 → 4 (unchanged), resilience 3 → 3 (unchanged),
  data 4 → 4 (unchanged). **Rank 1, unchanged** (tied with IDEA-004 at 69.0 and ahead of it on
  falsifiability, 4 versus 3).

#### Cycle 37 block — IDEA-011 · G-21 CONFIRMED null, and the comparison is now written so it can be handed over rather than re-searched

- **What Cycle 37 did.** It searched once more for any published study that compares post-earnings-
  announcement drift between **first-time** and **second-time** adopters of a common accounting
  standard, and found none. G-21 therefore remains a **null**: the register holds no evidence either
  way, and the discriminator remains **untested**, not refuted. This is the second consecutive cycle
  in which no new evidence on this candidate has been found; the candidate is not being worked on
  because it is exhausted, but because its residual is now fully specified.
- **The comparison, written down exactly.** For a common accounting standard adopted by two groups of
  jurisdictions — group A mandating it for the **first time**, group B mandating a standard the
  jurisdiction's firms had already adopted voluntarily or under an earlier mandate — compare the
  **post-earnings-announcement drift** (abnormal return in a fixed window after the earnings
  announcement date) of adopting firms in group A with that of firms in group B, in the same calendar
  window, controlling for size, industry and market. The register already holds the two things that
  make the comparison executable at all: the published adoption calendar, and the
  mandatory-versus-voluntary-adopter contrast in S-220 — whose outcomes are liquidity, cost of capital
  and valuation, **not** drift, and whose voluntary arm is self-selected, which is exactly why the
  *first-adopter versus second-adopter* comparison is the needed one.
- **What the comparison would and would not settle.** It would settle whether a comparability clock
  (as opposed to a generic "the numbers changed" effect) is what produces the drift increase. It
  would **not** settle the candidate's information-quality conditioning: that is a second, orthogonal
  requirement, and the register's own Cycle-35 hard-stop flag (enforcement concentration is published,
  S-221) still binds.
- **Score: 69.0, unchanged. Rank 1, unchanged.** No new source bears on any score component.

#### Cycles 38–51 consolidated note — IDEA-011 · no new source bearing on this dossier, and the null was *maintained*, not re-run
- Across Cycles 38–51 the register executed, in order: G-29 (IDEA-010's route to a corporate-bond ETF's AP
  set, closed as a calibrated document-level search, Cycle 40); G-30 (the AP-identity route, opened Cycle 40
  and **exhausted without a re-probe** by Cycle 51); the family screens **R-021 … R-031** (Cycles 41–51,
  each a bounded negative, each written into `rejected_and_parked.md`); and the register-wide **post-2010
  evidence-currency pass** on the other active dossiers. **Not one of those cycles produced a source bearing
  on IDEA-011.** G-21 therefore stood as a **null with an unchanged status** — the discriminator untested,
  not refuted — for fourteen consecutive cycles.
- **Why this is recorded now, and why it matters.** A null that is simply *re-declared* is worth nothing;
  the register's own standard (the Cycle-35 precedent, and the Cycle-40 repair of G-29) is that a gap's
  status must be re-derived from a *check*, not carried forward in prose. Cycle 52 therefore ran the
  check that had never been run for this candidate: a **prior-art re-read of the rank-1 candidate against
  2025–2026 literature the register had not seen**, in the same spirit as the post-2010 drift-decay check
  that re-scored the candidate in Cycle 16 and the check that killed IDEA-001.

#### Cycle 52 block — IDEA-011 · the prior-art re-read returns a *sharper* null, one published competing conditioner, and a dated prospective window (**G-32** opened)

- **What Cycle 52 ran.** Four sources on the IFRS-adoption literature, read against the candidate: the
  post-adoption information-effect paper (S-252), a **systematic review of the field** (S-253), the IFRS
  Foundation's **jurisdiction profiles** (S-254) and the IFRS 18 standard itself with its effective date
  (S-255), plus the early-versus-late adopter comparator on audit fees (S-265). Five further sources
  (S-256 … S-264, S-266) were run for the separate venue-halt family screen and bear on nothing here.
- **Finding 1 — the null stands, and the register now knows *precisely* what the literature contains.**
  The first-adopter/second-adopter contrast is a **standard, populated design** in this literature, not an
  exotic one: it has been run on **audit fees and audit time** (S-265), on narrative disclosure, on
  accounting choice and on language barriers. But the field's own **systematic review** of the ~181
  most-cited IFRS-adoption papers inventories the reviewed outcomes — accounting quality, comparability,
  liquidity, cost of capital, forecast accuracy, foreign investment — and **post-earnings-announcement
  drift is not among them** (S-253). So the candidate's residual is **not** an unnoticed hole in the
  literature; it is a **specific outcome that a standard comparator has not been run on**, in a field where
  the comparator exists and is well understood. That is a sharper statement of the null than "nothing
  found", and it is the statement a validator needs: the gap is *outcome-specific*, not *design-specific*.
- **Finding 2 — a published conditioner that competes with the candidate's own state, and therefore a new
  mandatory control.** The post-adoption information effect is now shown to **flip sign** with a
  **country-level, ex-ante, published** state: in the countries with the most severe language barriers,
  IFRS adoption *worsened* the post-adoption information environment — higher analyst forecast error and
  wider bid–ask spreads — where in others it improved it (S-252). Two consequences bind the candidate.
  (i) The candidate may **not** present the state-conditioned post-adoption information effect as a
  discovery; it is published. (ii) Because that conditioner is **country-level**, it is **collinear with
  the already-published enforcement conditioner** (S-221), so the candidate's own conditioning variable
  must remain **firm-level** — a country-level discriminator would be identification by collinearity, not
  by design. **Both country-level conditioners (enforcement, language distance) enter the specification as
  mandatory controls**, and the candidate's firm-level state must retain identifying power *within* a
  single standard. That requirement is now the operational content of G-32's limb (b).
- **Finding 3 — the premise is weakened at one point and supported at another; the distinction is recorded
  rather than smoothed.** The systematic review finds that IFRS adoption's market effects are real but that
  **"accounting quality and comparability do not mediate the market effects"**, and that negative findings
  **"point to varying enforcement strength and IFRS complexity"** (S-253) — that is adverse to the
  candidate's comparability-as-driver story. But the same review attributes positive findings to **"IFRS'
  mandatory nature and pre-IFRS differences compared to local standards"** — the **discontinuity at
  adoption**, which is the candidate's own premise — rather than to the gradual improvement of comparability.
  The two readings are **distinguishable in the candidate's design** (drift outcomes versus
  liquidity/cost-of-capital outcomes; transition windows versus post-transition windows), so the candidate
  must now **pre-register which one it claims** — this is a specification requirement, not a hedge.
- **Finding 4 — a dated, prospective route, which is why this cycle is not another null: G-32 is OPENED.**
  The candidate's binding hard stop is that the number of *independent, non-overlapping* adoption events is
  small. **IFRS 18** (issued April 2024) is **"effective for annual reporting periods beginning on or after
  1 January 2027, with earlier application permitted"**, replaces IAS 1, and creates a comparability break
  **by design** — two new defined subtotals in the statement of profit or loss, required disclosure of
  management-defined performance measures, and new aggregation and disaggregation principles (S-255). The
  **jurisdiction-level adoption-status calendar the discriminator needs is published in advance** in the
  IFRS Foundation's jurisdiction profiles, with **170 jurisdictions** filterable by "required for domestic
  public companies" versus "permitted but not required" (S-254). **G-32** is opened with three limbs, each
  with a stated answer route:
   - **(a) the calendar limb — closable now, and it is a *reading* task, not a data-processing task.** Read
     the published jurisdiction profiles jurisdiction by jurisdiction and determine whether they identify a
     **group A (first-time IFRS 18 adopters)** and a **group B (second-time adopters — jurisdictions already
     reporting under IFRS-equivalent standards)** with **non-overlapping, pre-registered** IFRS 18 adoption
     dates. The register has not enumerated the profiles; a bulk directory read is not performed in this
     repository, so this is the concrete next act and it is explicitly a *reading* task.
   **Cycle 53 (this cycle) — G-32 limb (a) executed as a reading task.** Read the IFRS Foundation
   jurisdiction profiles index and sampled jurisdiction profiles (EU, UK, Australia, Canada) via the published
   pages. The profiles state general IFRS adoption status (required/permitted) and list 170 jurisdictions
   (S-254), but **do not enumerate IFRS 18-specific adoption dates or explicitly partition jurisdictions into
   first-time IFRS 18 adopters vs second-time adopters** in the pages read. The standard's effective date is
   published (1 January 2027, S-255) and some jurisdictions have endorsed/adopted it (e.g. EU endorsed via
   Commission regulation for 2027 application; UK adopted for 2027), but the profiles do not present a
   pre-registered partition with non-overlapping dates for IFRS 18 as of the pages inspected. **Verdict (limb
   a): negative as specified.** The profiles as read do not support the clean group A/group B partition with
   non-overlapping, pre-registered IFRS 18 adoption dates. What would be needed instead: a published
   jurisdiction-level adoption calendar for IFRS 18 (endorsement dates and mandatory application dates by
   jurisdiction) that explicitly distinguishes first-time IFRS adopters adopting IFRS via IFRS 18 from
   jurisdictions already applying IFRS that are transitioning to IFRS 18; absent such a table, the
   partition cannot be constructed from the current profiles alone. This is a legitimate negative outcome.
  - **(b) the identification limb.** Does the candidate's **firm-level** information-quality conditioning
    retain identifying power *within a single standard*, i.e. is the treatment not absorbed by the two
    published country-level conditioners (S-221, S-252)? No past event could pose this cleanly, because in
    every past event the treatment varies with the jurisdiction.
  - **(c) the pre-adoption-claim limb.** Does any 2024–2027 pre-adoption literature already claim the
    IFRS 18 drift sign? The review of the field (S-253) cannot answer it, because IFRS 18 post-dates it.
- **Score: 69.0, unchanged. Rank 1, unchanged.** Following the **Cycle-35 precedent**, the re-read's
  sources support the candidate's *mechanism* and add a *new mandatory control*, but they do not
  establish the candidate's **directional drift claim**, and one of them (S-253) cuts against the premise.
  So: mechanism 4 → 4, evidence 3 → 3 (**unchanged** — the *content* of the evidence base changed, not
  its quantity; the candidate still has no supporting published case for a drift increase), novelty 3 → 3
  (**unchanged** — the residual is still an outcome-specific, unrun comparison on a standard comparator),
  falsifiability 4 → 4 (**unchanged** — and now *better specified*, since the candidate must pre-register
  which premise it claims), resilience 3 → 3 (**unchanged**), data 4 → 4 (**unchanged** — the adoption
  calendar is published, and G-32 makes the *event* prospective rather than historical). **No re-score.**
  **Rank 1, unchanged** (tied with IDEA-004 at 69.0 and ahead of it on falsifiability, 4 versus 3).
- **Hard-stop flags — one added, one sharpened.**
  - **Added:** the candidate may **not** present the state-conditioned post-adoption information effect as
    a discovery, and must carry **enforcement (S-221) and language distance (S-252) as mandatory
    controls**, with its own conditioning variable kept **firm-level**.
  - **Sharpened:** the candidate must **pre-register which of the two rival readings of the published
    evidence it claims** — the *discontinuity-at-adoption* reading (S-253's positive attribution, the
    candidate's premise) or the *comparability-as-driver* reading (S-253's mediation null) — because the
    two are distinguishable only by the outcome and window the candidate specifies.
- **Dated re-check.** The pre-adoption literature window is **2024–2027** and the first IFRS 18 annual
  reports (periods beginning on or after 1 January 2027) will be published during **2028**. **Re-read the
  IFRS 18 pre-adoption literature annually**, and **re-run limb (a) whenever the IFRS Foundation updates its
  jurisdiction profiles** — a profile update is a dated, published event that can change the group A/group B
  partition without any new literature.

- **Cycle 56 (2026-10-20) — the G-32 follow-up. Limb (a) closed negative as specified *with the reason
  now established*; the proposed re-specification rejected on a documented instance; a different
  re-specification specified as prospective arm A.**
  The Cycle-52 handoff named one task: decide whether the candidate can be re-specified to use a
  different identification — "e.g. within-jurisdiction transition timing / early adopters vs mandatory
  adopters under same regime" — "or must remain blocked. Do not guess." Cycle 56 answered that question
  with primary instruments rather than with profile directories, and the answer is **split**: the specific
  re-specification the handoff suggested is **rejected**, and a different one **survives**.
  - **Finding 1 — limb (a), the calendar limb, is CLOSED negative as specified, and the reason is now
    known.** The register no longer asks only whether a partition table exists; it can now say why none
    can exist in the form the candidate needs. Three verified facts:
    (i) **Convergence.** The published endorsement instruments all point at **one** mandatory date. The EU
    endorsed IFRS 18 by Commission Regulation (EU) 2026/338 of 13 February 2026 (Official Journal
    publication 16 February 2026), which provides that "each company shall apply the amendment at the
    latest as from the commencement of its first financial year starting on or after **1 January 2027**"
    (S-268). The UK Endorsement Board approved IFRS 18 for adoption on **10 December 2025**, closed the
    project on **30 January 2026**, and records the standard as effective for periods beginning on or
    after **1 January 2027** (S-269). Saudi Arabia's standard-setter adopted IFRS 18 locally on
    **26 December 2024**, and the Saudi Capital Market Authority's board decision of **30 June 2026**
    makes application mandatory for periods beginning on or after **1 January 2027** (S-270, S-271). The
    already-IFRS jurisdictions — group B — therefore apply IFRS 18 on **one common date**, and the
    IFRS Foundation's profiles give *status*, not dates (S-254). A group A date could only come from a
    jurisdiction whose **first** IFRS adoption falls after 1 January 2027; no published partition table
    exists, and none could, in the candidate's required form, while the mandatory dates converge.
    (ii) **Single-package adoption.** Where a jurisdiction adopts IFRS for the first time in the IFRS 18
    era, IFRS 18 arrives *simultaneously* with the whole IFRS package: the Saudi endorsement practice is
    that the IFRSs "shall be applied **all at once**" after endorsement — listed entities from 2017 and
    other entities from 2018 (S-271). The group A/group B contrast then stops being a *within-standard*
    contrast and becomes the historical first-time-versus-second-time IFRS comparison the literature has
    already populated (S-188 Greece as a first adopter; S-179 Korea as a second adopter).
    (iii) **Consequence.** The group A/group B partition inside the IFRS 18 window is **unavailable**, and
    G-21's discriminator cannot be newly identified there. **The candidate's binding hard stop — the small
    number of independent, non-overlapping adoption events — is unchanged by this cycle.**
  - **Finding 2 — the handoff's suggested re-specification is REJECTED, on a documented instance rather
    than a hypothetical one.** Saudi Arabia is a published, live instance of exactly the contrast the
    handoff proposed: the CMA board decision of 30 June 2026 (S-270) **permits early adoption during
    2026** by Tadawul-listed joint-stock companies, requires early adopters to "announce IFRS 18 financial
    statements and the effect of early adoption on the Tadawul website", makes application **mandatory
    from periods beginning on or after 1 January 2027**, and requires **all** listed joint-stock companies
    to disclose "a preliminary assessment of the expected impact of the initial application of IFRS 18" in
    their approved interim and annual financial statements for periods beginning on or after **1 April
    2026**. That is a published within-jurisdiction transition-timing contrast — and it does **not** rescue
    the candidate, for three reasons that are stated rather than assumed:
    (i) the treatment arm is **voluntary and self-selected**, and the selection is *mechanically correlated*
    with the state the candidate would condition on — a firm that expects a large IFRS 18 impact, or that
    wants to pre-empt a comparability problem, has a reason to early-adopt — so the contrast is not
    identified;
    (ii) the effective sample is the **number of early adopters**, so the event-count hard stop is
    *relocated*, not relieved;
    (iii) the early arm announces its IFRS 18 financial statements *together with* their effect, so the
    slow-information-diffusion clock has **no window to run** on that arm.
    And the country-level conditioners (S-221 enforcement, S-252 linguistic distance) are constant only
    *within* Saudi, so the cross-country comparison the candidate would want is unavailable there too. One
    thing the rejected arm does deliver is an **observability** route: early adopters are machine-identifiable
    from tagged filings, because the IFRS taxonomy guidance contains a dedicated "Early Application of IFRS 18"
    entry point (S-272). The rejection is about identification, not about observability.
  - **Finding 3 — a re-specification that SURVIVES: prospective arm A, the pre-adoption impact disclosure
    as the firm-level state.** The register's search for a decision-time state that measures the *size of
    the comparability break* — the candidate's own premise, and the thing the candidate lacked — found one
    in the standard-setter's own requirements. ESMA's Public Statement on the Implementation of IFRS 18
    (ESMA32-193237008-9180) "draws attention to the requirements in **IAS 8 paragraphs 30 and 31**" on
    disclosing an impending change in accounting policy where a new IFRS has been issued but is not yet
    effective, and notes that paragraph 30(b) requires disclosure of "known or reasonably estimable
    information relevant to assessing the possible impact that application of the new IFRS will have on the
    issuer's financial statements in the period of initial application". ESMA "expects these disclosures to
    be provided for IFRS 18 in issuers' interim and annual financial reports for periods ending before
    1 January 2027", covering "a description of changes in the structure of the issuer's statement of profit
    or loss, information on the assessment of whether the issuer performs 'specified main business
    activities', and details on measures that are expected to meet the definition of an MPM", and it
    "encourages" issuers to include "the (expected) change in 2026 operating profit or loss and/or in other
    subtotals" (S-267). This yields a state that is **firm-level** (so it does not collide with the
    country-level conditioners S-221 and S-252, which become absorbed within jurisdiction), **published**,
    **dated** (it precedes application), **not real-time** (it arrives in periodic reports), and — decisively
    — it is a *direct* measure of the size of the comparability break rather than a proxy for information
    quality. It also converts the identification from "few events" to "one event × many firms", which
    **relaxes the binding hard stop inside arm A**. Two limits are recorded rather than smoothed:
    (i) the *mandated* disclosure is **qualitative** and the quantified expected change is only
    *encouraged*, so the state's measurability at firm level is **not established** — this is the new gap
    **G-33**; and (ii) the state arrives in the *same* periodic reports in which the break first appears
    (ESMA expects IFRS 18 to be applied in **interim** financial statements in the year of initial
    application, so the first IFRS 18 reporting cycle is the **2027 interim** reports, not the 2028 annual
    reports), so the treatment and outcome windows overlap and the specification must pre-register how the
    slow-diffusion clock is separated from the adoption-announcement effect.
  - **Score: 69.0, unchanged. Rank 1, unchanged.** The Cycle-56 sources support the candidate's
    *mechanism and its state*, not its **directional drift claim** — the Cycle-35/52 precedent applies
    again. Mechanism 4 → 4, evidence 3 → 3 (the *content* of the evidence base changed, not its quantity;
    the candidate still has no published supporting case for a drift increase, and limb (c) of G-32 — the
    pre-adoption-claim question — remains open), novelty 3 → 3 (the residual is still an outcome-specific
    unrun comparison, now with a state the register did not have), falsifiability 4 → 4 (unchanged, and
    *better specified*, because arm A has a published, dated, firm-level state and a pre-registered
    separation requirement), resilience 3 → 3, data 4 → 4 (the event is prospective and the *state* is
    published in principle, though its quantification is the open G-33). **No re-score.**
  - **Hard-stop flags — one added, one sharpened.**
    - **Added:** the candidate may **not** use the group A/group B partition inside the IFRS 18 window —
      limb (a) is closed negative as specified, and a validator must not re-derive it. The only IFRS 18
      identification available to the candidate is **prospective arm A** (the IAS 8.30(b)/ESMA pre-adoption
      impact disclosure), and that arm **cannot run** until **G-33** establishes that the disclosure is
      quantifiable and comparable at firm level.
    - **Sharpened:** the candidate may **not** present the within-jurisdiction early-versus-mandatory
      contrast as an identification (Finding 2) — the treatment is voluntary and self-selected, the effective
      sample is the number of early adopters, and the early arm's clock never runs.
  - **Dated re-check.** G-32 limb (c) is re-read annually (the 2024–2027 pre-adoption literature window);
  **G-33 is re-checked in 2027 Q3**, after the first tranche of 2026 interim reports carrying IAS 8.30(b)
  impact disclosures is public; limb (a) is re-opened only if a jurisdiction publishes an IFRS 18
application date that differs from 1 January 2027 **and** applies IFRS 18 as an increment to an existing
IFRS basis.

  - **Cycle 57 — G-33 quantification-rate sample read: arm A killed, hard stop unrelieved, no score change.**
    The pre-registered sample read on the IAS 8.30(b)/ESMA-expected pre-adoption IFRS 18 impact disclosure
    was executed across six issuers (Repsol S.A., Euronext Securities Athens S.A., Airbus SE, Haleon plc,
    Standard Life plc, Saudi Tadawul Group Holding Co.) reading their H1/Q2 2026 interim reports (periods
    ending 30 June 2026). Result: disclosure present 6/6; quantified expected effect 0/6; units none;
    comparability not established (no numbers); ex-ante availability yes (~1–2 months after period end,
    ahead of the first IFRS 18 cycle in the 2027 interims, semi-annual). All six disclosures are
    qualitative only ("still assessing", "evaluating", "will examine impact", "assessment remains
    ongoing"); none states a quantified expected change in operating profit or any subtotal, and none
    restates a prior-period figure on an IFRS 18 basis. Sources: S-281 … S-286.
    > **CORRECTED — Cycle 58 (Repair 8).** The phrases in quotation marks above are **paraphrases, not the
    > issuers' words**, and they were attributed to the sample collectively rather than per issuer; the
    > verbatim language, the document URLs and the verification method actually used are now recorded in

    > **(i)** the corpus is **5 of 6 line-verified and 6 of 6 index-confirmed** — the Saudi Tadawul document
    > was never fetched (HTTP 403 on every route) and its text is index-rendered, so the sample's only
    > non-EU leg is unverified and the cross-jurisdiction comparability of the corpus is not independently

    > disclosure** — the string "IAS 8" occurs **zero** times in the extracted text of all five fetched

    > issued but not yet effective* and *recent accounting developments* notes, which is the register's own
    > hypothesis and therefore **strengthens** the closure; and **(iii)** the disclosures were
    > mis-characterised as *pre-adoption impact* disclosures in the first place, and the correct label is
    > *not-yet-effective / recent-accounting-developments notes*. **The kill condition stands** at 6/6
    > (index-confirmed) and 5/6 (line-verified); arm A remains killed and the event-count hard stop
    > remains unrelieved.
    **Kill condition met:** the disclosure is unquantified in 100 % of the sample, so prospective arm A
    has no usable state. Arm A is killed; IDEA-011 returns to blocked on the group A/group B partition,
    with the event-count hard stop **unrelieved**. No score change (69.0, rank 1); the Cycle-35/52
    precedent applies — the sources support the mechanism and the state's existence (IAS 8.30–31 plus
    ESMA's expectation, S-267) but not the quantified state or the directional drift claim.
    G-33 is **closed as a negative** on the kill condition; its dated re-check in 2027 Q3 stands but the

  - **Cycle 58 (2026-10-20) — G-21 null confirmed and sharpened, the mechanism's scope bounded, and the
    IFRS 18 endorsement date corroborated by a second independent primary issuer document. No score
    change.**
    - **What Cycle 58 ran.** The **G-21** question was re-derived from a check rather than re-declared:
      does any published study compare **first-time with second-time adopters** of the same standard on any
      market outcome, and specifically on post-earnings-announcement drift? The answer is still **no**, and
      this is now the **third** cycle to establish it (Cycles 37, 52 and 58). Two new sources sharpen the
      null: **S-294** (Neel 2017), the field's large peer-reviewed comparability study, whose design is a
      **first-time-adopter design by construction** — mandatory IFRS adoption is, for the adopting
      jurisdictions, an adoption of IFRS *for the first time* — and whose outcomes include analyst
      forecast accuracy and forecast agreement but **not** drift; and **S-295** (Jiao, Koning, Mertens &
      Roosenboom 2012), the nearest published *outcome* family to the candidate's information-environment
      claim, which is likewise a first-time-adopter study. The conclusion is stronger than a bare null:
      **the discriminator the candidate needs is not merely unrun, it is unused by the field's largest
      study** — so a study running the first-time-versus-second-time contrast on drift would be a
      *contribution*, not a replication. That raises the candidate's novelty reading and lowers its
      evidence reading, and it is a genuine finding about the literature, not a restatement of a gap.
    - **The direction of the published evidence does not resolve the candidate's premise, and imposes a
      requirement.** S-294 measures the **level** of information quality *after* comparability improves
      (more accurate forecasts, better agreement), whereas the candidate's claim is about a **transition**
      window between the adoption announcement and the first full reporting cycle. The two are different
      objects, so S-294 neither supports nor refutes the directional drift claim — but it does impose a
      pre-registration requirement: **the candidate must pre-register that its claim is a transition-window
      effect**, because the published steady-state evidence runs the other way.
    - **Two new facts about the mechanism's scope, read from the G-33 documents themselves.**
      (i) **IFRS 18 changes no recognition, no measurement and no reported amount — but it changes
      presentation across three statements, not one.** Standard Life's interim report states that "IFRS 18
      does not change the recognition or measurement of assets, liabilities, income or expenses" (S-285),
      and Repsol's states that the standard "will not have an impact on the Group's income, cash flows or
      financial position" (S-281). **This is the part that bounds the candidate's mechanism**: the
      comparability break the candidate's clock is about is *substantive-neutral* — it creates no economic
      difference, only a presentation difference. **But it is not confined to the statement of profit or
      loss.** Repsol's own words: the standard "will introduce new presentation criteria (mainly with
      respect to the income statement **and statement of cash flows and, to a lesser extent, the balance
      sheet**)"; the affected items are "the three categories for presenting income and expenses (operating,
      investing and financing) and the subsequent new subtotals (" operating income" and " income before
      financing and taxes ")"; the criteria for presenting certain items apply "both in the income statement
      (most notably the presentation of exchange differences within each category based on the nature of the
      item giving rise to such differences) and in the statement of cash flows (most notably dividends
      received from investees within investing cash flows)"; and "operating income" becomes "the new
      starting point for preparing the statement of cash flows". The Tadawul index rendering says the same
      of the cash-flow reconciliation's starting point and adds that goodwill becomes a separate line
      (S-286, index-rendered). **Corrected scope statement:** the break runs over (a) the statement of profit
      or loss's new categories, subtotals and reclassification criteria; (b) the **statement of cash flows**,
      whose starting point becomes operating income and whose operating/investing split changes; (c) the
      **balance sheet**, to a lesser extent, in the presentation of goodwill as a separate line; (d) the
      aggregation, disaggregation and management-performance-measure disclosures; and (e) **nothing that
      changes recognition, measurement or any reported amount**. This *narrows the candidate in its own
      favour* and sharpens its rule — the break is a presentation break, not an economic one — but under the
      Cycle-35/52 precedent it does **not** move a score, because it bounds the mechanism rather than
      supporting the directional drift claim. **Corrected in Cycle 58 (Repair 8): the first version of this
      entry stated that the break ran "over nothing else — no cash-flow, no balance-sheet, no measurement
      change". The measurement limb was right; the cash-flow and balance-sheet limbs were wrong and are
      corrected here.** The narrowing survives; its enumerated scope did not.
      (ii) **The EU endorsement date is corroborated independently.** Airbus SE's H1 2026 financial
      statements state that "IFRS 18 has been endorsed by the EU on 13 February 2026" (S-283), matching
      S-268. G-32 limb (a)'s closure now rests on **two** independent primary confirmations rather than one.
    - **What was *not* run.** G-32 limb (c) is annual and was read in Cycle 57; the next re-read is due in
      2027 Q3. G-30 was not re-probed. G-33's dated re-check is in 2027 Q3 and is not due. No new `V-`
      specification was created.
    - **V-009 was AMENDED, not re-created.** Two of the cycle's findings change what a validator may
      build, so they are specification inputs rather than evidence notes. (i) The **presentation-only**
      bound must be imposed on the attribution: IFRS 18 changes no recognition, measurement or reported
      amount (S-281, S-285), so a validator may not attribute drift to a **measurement** change on the
      strength of an IFRS 18 adoption. **The bound is *not* a statement that nothing outside the profit or
      loss statement changes**: the presentation break also reaches the **statement of cash flows** (new
      starting point: operating income; operating/investing reclassification) and, to a lesser extent, the
      **balance sheet** (goodwill as a separate line), so a validator may not confine the attribution to
      profit-or-loss subtotal drift either. The rule is that the candidate's comparability break is a
      **presentation** break, quantified wherever it is measured, and confined to the three primary
      statements and the notes. (ii) The claim must be **pre-registered
      as a transition-window effect**, with the published steady-state comparability outcome reported as a
      descriptive control and **not** as support, because the published steady-state evidence runs the other
      way (S-294, S-295). The hypothesis, the event universe, the cost model, the guardrails, the separation
      rule and the kill criteria are unchanged; the anti-peeking requirement now also covers the two new
      pre-registrations.
    - **Score: 69.0, unchanged. Rank 1, unchanged.** Mechanism 4 → 4 (bounded, not extended); evidence
      3 → 3 (the *content* of the base changed again — two new, relevant, well-verified sources — but the
      candidate still has no published supporting case for a drift increase, and the discriminator is
      confirmed unused); novelty 3 → 3 (the residual is an *unused* discriminator, and the field's own
      design structure now explains why: mandatory-adoption studies are first-time-adopter studies by
      construction); falsifiability 4 → 4 (unchanged, and *better specified*, because the candidate must
      now pre-register the transition-window reading); resilience 3 → 3; data 4 → 4. **No re-score.**
    - **New hard-stop flag — added.** The candidate may **not** present a *steady-state* comparability or
      information-quality result as support for a *transition-window* drift claim. S-294 and S-295 measure
      post-adoption levels; the candidate's claim is about the transition. Any validation must pre-register
      which reading it is testing, and the sign of the published steady-state evidence (higher information
      quality after adoption) must not be presented as the sign of the candidate's claim.
    - **Second Cycle-58 hard-stop flag — added (Repair 8).** The candidate may **not** confine the
      comparability break to the statement of profit or loss. The break also reaches the **statement of
      cash flows** — whose starting point becomes operating income and whose operating/investing split
      changes — and, to a lesser extent, the **balance sheet**, in the presentation of goodwill as a
      separate line (S-281, S-285; S-286 for the goodwill line, index-rendered). An attribution exercise
      restricted to profit-or-loss subtotals is therefore **incomplete**, and the break must be quantified
      at whichever primary statement it is measured on. The **prohibition on measurement-based attribution
      is unchanged and remains absolute**: no recognition, measurement or reported-amount change exists
      (S-281, S-285). **Correction notice (Cycle 58, Repair 8):** the first version of the Cycle-58 entry
      stated the break ran "over nothing else — no cash-flow, no balance-sheet, no measurement change". The
      measurement limb was right; the cash-flow and balance-sheet limbs were wrong, and this flag replaces
      the superseded wording.
    - **Dated re-check (unchanged).** G-32 limb (c) is re-read annually; **G-33 is re-checked in 2027 Q3**;
      limb (a) re-opens only if a jurisdiction publishes an IFRS 18 application date that differs from
      1 January 2027 **and** applies IFRS 18 as an increment to an existing IFRS basis.

  - **Cycle 59 (2026-10-20) — G-21 handed off to validation; no literature re-search; no score change.**
    - **What Cycle 59 ran.** The Cycle-58 handoff directed a **hand-off, not a search**: three cycles
      (37, 52, 58) had established the G-21 null, and the correct action was to make the comparator
      **actionable inside V-009** and to stop searching the literature for it. Cycle 59 did not re-run the
      search and did not open a group A / group B partition inside the IFRS 18 window (G-32 limb (a) is
      closed), and it consumed **no `S-` identifier** — the null rests on the register's existing sources
      (S-294, S-295).
    - **What the hand-off means for this candidate.** The discriminator is now recorded as **unused, not
      merely unrun**: the field's largest comparability study (S-294) is a **first-time-adopter design by
      construction** and its outcomes (earnings quality, liquidity, analyst forecast accuracy and
      agreement) do not include drift, and the nearest published outcome family (S-295) is likewise
      first-time-adopter. A validator running the comparison therefore produces a **contribution, not a
      replication** — which raises the candidate's *novelty* reading and lowers its *evidence* reading, and
      changes what a null in that design would mean. Any such validation must also pre-register the
      **transition-window** reading, because the published steady-state evidence runs the other way.
    - **Standing prohibitions unchanged.** The two Cycle-58 hard-stop flags stand in full: the candidate
      may **not** present a steady-state comparability or information-quality result as support for a
      transition-window drift claim, and it may **not** confine the comparability break to the statement of
      profit or loss (the break also reaches the statement of cash flows and, to a lesser extent, the
      balance sheet; the **measurement** limb remains absolutely prohibited — no recognition, measurement
      or reported-amount change exists, S-281, S-285).
    - **Dated obligations, unchanged and now enumerated.** G-32 limb (c) is re-read annually; **G-33 is
      re-checked in 2027 Q3**, and that re-check **must not re-run the sample read from scratch** — the six
      interim reports have been read (S-281 … S-286; 5 of 6 line-verified, 6 of 6 index-confirmed), the
      quantified expected effect was **0 of 6** (so the open question is *comparability across firms*,
      not *quantification*), none of the six is framed as an IAS 8.30–31 disclosure, and the corpus's only
      non-EU leg is index-rendered behind an Akamai 403, so a re-check wanting a non-EU leg needs a
      different retrieval route. Limb (a) re-opens only if a jurisdiction publishes an IFRS 18 application
      date differing from 1 January 2027 **and** applies IFRS 18 as an increment to an existing IFRS basis.
    - **Score: 69.0, unchanged. Rank 1, unchanged.** Mechanism 4 → 4; evidence 3 → 3; novelty 3 → 3;
      falsifiability 4 → 4; resilience 3 → 3; data 4 → 4. **No re-score**, no new hard-stop flag, and
      **no `V-` identifier consumed** — V-009 was **not** amended in Cycle 59, because the Cycle-58
      amendment already carries the comparator-as-contribution reading and the transition-window
      pre-registration.

## IDEA-012 — CLS-CLOSE: the settlement-side deadline cluster as a price determinant (continuation of IDEA-006)

- **ID / short name:** IDEA-012 / CLS-CLOSE
- **Date added:** 2026-10-19 · **Cycle:** 4 · **Status:** **rejected (R-010)** · **Version:** 1.1
- **Change notes:** v1.0 — initial dossier. New mechanism family not previously in the register; a dedicated vehicle for the settlement-side residual of IDEA-006, blocked on G-11.
  **v1.1 — REJECTED after the Cycle-5 G-11 closure (S-112 … S-121).** The candidate existed to be a
  literature check: "if the settlement-side cluster is tested and found priced, IDEA-006 and IDEA-012
  are both rejected; if untested, they remain as narrow, unproven residuals." The check was run and
  returned "tested and found priced": a settlement deadline cluster is a published price determinant
  (S-112 — the CLS pay-in schedule raises the price of intraday liquidity by an estimated "roughly
  1.5bp for the CLS settlement cycle"), the securities settlement cycle is a published price-impact
  determinant (S-113, S-114, S-115), the FX settlement-risk premium is published (S-082, S-071), and
  the post-US-close FX window is documented as a cost, not an edge (S-117, S-118, S-119, S-120). The
  candidate's own "key uncertainties" section stated the consequence in advance: "If G-11 closes
  against the candidate … the candidate is rejected, not re-scored." **The dossier is closed; the
  reasoning is preserved in R-010.**

**One-sentence hypothesis.** Beyond the published 16:00 London fix effect, the CLS currency-close and pay-in deadlines (and the post-US-close window created by T+1) create a separate, dated, settlement-constrained demand/supply pressure in specific currency pairs on no-macro-release days; the state (deadline proximity and fix-window volume share) is published by the benchmark administrator and the settlement utility.

**Plain-language description.** Once a day, the world's currencies have to be settled. CLS members must have the right currency in the right place by hard cut-offs — the currency close at midday CET, the initial pay-in schedule issued at 00:00 CET, the settlement completion target at 09:00 CET. Since the US moved to T+1 in May 2024, when the US equity market closes at 16:00 EST (22:00 CET), there are only about two hours to arrange the currency for US securities settlement. That is a predictable, dated, price-insensitive demand for currency in a window that is not a benchmark — funds must fund their settlement obligations whether they want to or not. Whoever supplies liquidity into that settlement-constrained flow earns a temporary price concession that reverses once the deadline passes.

**Mechanism.** The 16:00 London fix effect is published (Melvin & Prins 2015; Evans 2018; Ito & Yamada 2015). The settlement-side mechanics are documented (CLS pay-in schedule, currency-close deadlines, Banque de France / ECB documentation) and are a priced friction (Ranaldo & Santucci de Magistris 2022; Lee, Ranaldo & Tsuruga 2026 IMF WP), but the specific CLS deadline cluster / post-US-close T+1 window is untested (G-11). The state is published: the CLS settlement timeline, the benchmark administrator's calendar and holiday notices, and the fix-window volume share.

**Expected return source.** A liquidity-provision premium for supplying liquidity to settlement-constrained participants in the post-US-close window; partial reversal once the deadline passes.

**Originality.** Closest work: Melvin & Prins 2015; Evans 2018; Ito & Yamada 2015 (fix window, published). Differentiator: the *settlement-side* residual beyond the fix, which no located paper tests. Honest classification: informed conjecture pending G-11 closure; if a published paper tests the settlement-side cluster, this candidate is rejected, not narrowed. Confidence: low-to-moderate.

**Target scope.** Liquid currency pairs with adequate fix-window volume; no-macro-release days; pre- and post-T+1 (May 2024) split; intraday windows.

**Observables (decision time).** CLS settlement timeline and pay-in deadlines (published); benchmark administrator calendar and holiday notices; fix-window volume share for the pair; macro-release calendar; quoted spread and depth.

**Falsifiable predictions.** (1) On no-release days, the post-US-close settlement window shows a signed move in the funding direction after the fix effect is removed. (2) The effect is absent pre-T+1 (settlement calendar change). (3) The effect is absent in a placebo window of the same length. (4) Gross concession exceeds round-trip cost. Kill: placebo performs the same, effect vanishes net of costs, or G-11 closes against the candidate.

**Confounders.** Macro releases inside the window; the fix effect itself (must be removed); liquidity-provider pre-positioning; non-settlement intraday drift; holiday/alteration days.

**Data requirements.** Intraday order-book or print data for the pairs; published benchmark calendar; CLS timeline; macro-release calendar. Feasibility: medium-low (intraday data is the binding constraint; the published fix literature used administrator-provided data).

**Theory/evidence status.** Conceptual rationale: settlement-side mechanics are a priced friction (S-082, S-071). Specific deadline-cluster price effect: unresolved conjecture (G-11). Priority score 60/100; rank TBD.

**Constraints.** Cost/liquidity: the concession is measured in pips inside a few minutes, so spread and slippage are first-order; this is the binding constraint. Capacity: bounded by the fix-window depth of the pair and by the size of the deadline-constrained flow. Market impact: trading into a concentrated deadline window is an impact machine. Venue/operational: the fix is a benchmark calculation, not an execution venue — the trade must be executed around it, not at it; execution infrastructure around the fix is expensive. Financing/margin: negligible for spot, relevant for leveraged forward positions. Shorting constraints: not a borrow-constrained trade.

**Regime sensitivity, tail exposure, crash behavior, hidden beta.** Regime: entirely deadline-dependent — on an ordinary day the deadline flow is small; at month-end or on a T+1 settlement peak it is not. Tail: a market disruption (a failed settlement, a benchmark suspension, a forced closure) is exactly when the deadline binds hardest and when liquidity is worst, so the position is most likely to be held exactly when it cannot be exited. Crash behaviour: a benchmark suspension removes the trade entirely; a settlement stress can force the deadline to bind on multiple currencies simultaneously. Hidden beta: the position is a short/long in the funding currency around a deadline, so it loads on the funding-coinage and the basis trade, and can carry an event risk that is not visible in the return. Correlated risks: all pairs sharing the same funding currency respond to the same deadline, so the book is a single funding-currency bet.

**Remaining uncertainty / confidence.** Confidence that the deadlines, the participant set and the flow concentration are real and published: **high** (CLS documentation S-048, S-049; LSEG methodology S-045). Confidence that the settlement-side deadline cluster has a separable price effect: **low** — G-11 is open, so this is an expectation rather than a finding. Confidence that any of it is harvestable after costs: **low** (the pip-level concession inside minutes is the decisive weakness). Priority score **60/100**; rank TBD. **Concrete next question:** does any published work test the settlement-side deadlines — the CLS currency-close and pay-in cut-offs, and the post-US-close window created by the May 2024 T+1 transition — as a price determinant rather than as an operational schedule, and if not, does the settlement window show a directional imbalance that survives removal of the published fix effect?

**v1.1 closure (Cycle 5).** The question above was run as a literature check and answered
disqualifyingly. Settlement deadlines **are** tested as price determinants in the published
literature: Kraenzlin and Nellen (2010, S-112) show that the CLS pay-in schedule raises the value of
intraday liquidity during the settlement cycle, with an estimated "roughly 1.5bp for the CLS
settlement cycle"; Baig, Breeze, Cox and Griffith (2022, S-113), Bhanu, Nath and Patnaik (2024,
S-114) and Shah and Davda (2026, S-115) make the securities settlement cycle a published
price-impact and market-quality determinant. The FX settlement-risk premium is published (S-082,
S-071). The remaining, narrower question — the *post-US-close window's* own market quality — is
answered by the institutional record as a **cost**: the New York Fed's FX Committee material names
the 4–6pm ET window as the transition's priority FX risk (S-117) and records the market's
expectation of "an uptick in price premium and market impact for trades executed later in the NY
trading day" (S-118); the trade record shows "less than 1% of trades in the $7 trillion OTC FX
market are carried out in this window", pricing engines shut at 5pm for up to half an hour, and
"bid/ask spreads widen and liquidity shrinks" (S-119); the after-action reporting shows the market
solved the constraint with custodian cut-off extensions and pre-funding, not with concession capture
(S-120). A concession priced inside a window with under 1% of volume, widening spreads and a
half-hour pricing-engine outage cannot survive a round trip. **Status: rejected (R-010).** The
mechanism is documented; the *edge* was never evidenced and the cost structure rules it out.
