## IDEA-013 — EU-ETSCOMP: the surrender-deadline concession in the EU ETS, and the published banking position that decides whether it exists

- **ID / short name:** IDEA-013 / EU-ETSCOMP
- **Date added:** 2026-10-19 · **Cycle:** 5 · **Status:** **parked (P-008)** · **Version:** 1.1
- **Change notes:** v1.0 — first dossier. New mechanism family for the register: a **statutory,
  date-bound purchase obligation** with a monetary penalty, in a market whose decision-time state is
  published by a regulator. The *effect* (a surrender-month premium) is published (S-101); the
  candidate claims only the two things the published work does not cover — the **regime change**
  (the deadline moved from 30 April to 30 September from 2024, outside the published sample) and the
  **existence-flipping state** (a pre-deadline published aggregate compliance balance). The full
  text of the published work (NBER WP 35356 / arXiv 2606.03767) **must be read before this candidate
  is retained**; see "Remaining uncertainty".
  **v1.1 — the required full-text check was performed, and the candidate is parked (P-008).** Three
  findings, all verified in this cycle. (i) **The check is resolved in the candidate's favour and
  against it at once:** S-101 does **not** condition the premium on the aggregate surplus (there is
  no occurrence of "TNAC" in the paper; the aggregate balance appears only as a state variable
  *inside the model*, and the paper's "cumulative surplus" measure is used to *construct*
  operator-level shortfalls, not to explain premium variation) — so the candidate is not a
  re-description of published work. But the published model already makes the benchmark price affine
  in the aggregate bank, so the state-conditional structure is **implied by the published model**; what
  the candidate claims is an *empirical* conditional structure that the published work does not
  estimate. (ii) **The published sample does not support the existence-flipping hypothesis and mildly
  cuts against it:** the paper states that "This delayed and frictional trading pattern persists into
  the most recent phase of the EU ETS, indicating limited improvement over time", and its most recent
  phase is Phase IV (2021) — a period of very large aggregate surplus (TNAC 1 134 794 738 for 2022
  and 1 111 736 535 for 2023, S-121). The paper does not report the April premium's magnitude by
  year, so the hypothesis is neither supported nor refuted by the published sample; what the sample
  shows is that a large surplus did **not** switch the effect off. (iii) **The decision-time state
  was, until the regime change, published *after* the deadline it was supposed to inform** — the
  Commission publishes TNAC for emissions year Y in late May or June of year Y+1 (S-121), so under
  the pre-2023 regime (surrender deadline for emissions of year Y: 30 April of year Y+1) the aggregate
  state for the compliance year was published **after** that deadline. Decision-time availability
  begins only with the 2023 compliance year (deadline 30 September 2024), which leaves **three
  decision-time observations to date** (2023, 2024 and 2025 compliance years) and **no observation in
  a deficit year**. This is recorded as the new gap **G-13** and as a new screening condition in the
  state-proxy note: *a published state is usable as a decision-time variable only if it is published
  before the decision.*
  **Why parked rather than retained or rejected.** The effect is published (S-101), so this is not a
  discovery; the claimed residual is not refuted, but the candidate cannot be *validated* in any
  decision-time form: the state switches sign too rarely for a conditional claim to be testable, the
  published model implies the conditional structure rather than leaving it open, and the published
  sample contains a high-surplus year in which the effect persisted. Re-specifying around a weaker
  state (operator-level bank-adjusted shortfalls, which are public only *ex post* in the EUTL) would
  convert the idea into a disguised baseline. The honest position is a **park** with a dated
  unblocking question, not a retention and not a rejection.

**One-sentence hypothesis.** The EU ETS obliges every covered installation, aircraft operator and
shipping company to surrender allowances for the previous year's verified emissions by a statutory
date, penalising failure at €100 per tonne — an obligation that is therefore almost perfectly
price-inelastic — and the resulting deadline-bound demand creates a surrender-month premium paid by
delayed compliance officers to their counterparties; the candidate's claim is that this premium is
**not a standing feature** but an **existence-conditional** one, appearing only in years when the
aggregate compliance balance is short, a balance that is knowable *before* the deadline from
regulator-published data — though, as v1.1 records, that property holds only in the current regime,
from the 2023 compliance year onwards — and that the published result covers only the *old* deadline
regime.

**Plain-language description.** Every year, an installation that emitted a tonne of carbon last year
must hand over one allowance to the government by a fixed date, or pay €100 for every tonne it
fails to cover — roughly the price of the allowance itself, so the obligation is effectively hard.
A firm that has been sitting on its allowance budget all year cannot wait for a better price; it
must buy in the deadline month, and it buys from whoever is willing to sell — usually a bank or a
fund that has been holding the allowance. The buyers pay up. But this only happens when the system
as a whole is *short*: if firms are sitting on a large surplus of banked allowances, nobody is
forced to buy anything in the deadline month and the premium does not exist. Whether the system is
short is not a secret — the regulator publishes, every May or June, the total number of allowances
in circulation, which is a direct read on the surplus. **But** (v1.1) that publication arrives in
late May or June of the year *after* the emissions year, so before the 2023 compliance year it
arrived *after* the surrender deadline it was meant to inform, and the "knowable before the
deadline" property of this state is a property of the **current regime only**.

**Mechanism (causal chain).**
1. **The obligation is statutory and hard.** Under Article 12(3) of Directive 2003/87/EC, "by
   30 September each year" the operator of each installation, each aircraft operator and each
   shipping company must surrender allowances equal to its verified emissions for the preceding
   calendar year (S-102). Failure to surrender on time attracts a penalty of **€100 per tonne** for
   the missing allowances, and the **names of penalised operators are made public** (S-101). A
   penalty of that order relative to the allowance price makes the demand curve almost perfectly
   inelastic in the deadline window — the textbook condition for a concession.
2. **The calendar is published and the timing is a policy variable.** Emissions must be verified by
   an accredited verifier by **31 March**; the surrender deadline was **30 April** under the
   original regime and was moved to **30 September** by Directive (EU) 2023/959, applying from the
   2024 compliance year onwards (S-102, S-103). The market monitors have already observed that the
   shift "spread out demand" and that "December saw the highest volumes, as is typical" (S-104) —
   so the *aggregate* relocation of the pressure is documented; what is not documented is the
   *conditional* structure below.
3. **The published effect.** Borri, Liu, Tsyvinski and Wu find that "purchases concentrate in April
   when returns are systematically high, and operator flow predicts future returns"; that "this
   concentration of compliance demand is associated with systematically high April returns, averaging
   about 10%"; and they estimate "the implied April premium paid by delayed buyers from this
   surrender pattern at about €5 billion" — while being careful that the premium "should be
   interpreted as a financial premium paid in April rather than as a direct measure of aggregate
   resource costs, because part of the higher April price is paid to counterparties" (S-101). Their
   model makes the mechanism explicit: "access determines residual compliance demand, intermediary
   constraints translate residual demand into a surrender-month premium, and the premium feeds back
   into access incentives."
4. **The published sample predates the regime change.** The empirical work covers **2005–2021**
   (S-101). The deadline moved to 30 September for the 2024 compliance year. The published effect
   therefore has **not** been re-established in the regime that is now law, and the market
   structure around it has changed: the MSR has been withdrawing allowances into the reserve since
   September 2024, and the 2027 auction calendars were published on 1 October 2026 with the
   2027 volumes already adjusted for the reserve and for the Social Climate Fund allocation
   (S-105, S-106).
5. **The state is published before the deadline — but only in the current regime — and it flips the
   sign of the effect.** The
   Commission publishes, every year, "the total number of allowances in circulation (TNAC) for the
   previous year" under Article 1(4) of the MSR Decision; the market monitors describe TNAC as "an
   indicator for the surplus" (S-107). The Commission published TNAC for 2023 — 1,111,736,535 — on
   **3 June 2024**, which is after the March verification deadline and before the September
   surrender deadline, and thereby determined that 266,816,768 allowances would be withheld from
   the auction volumes from 1 September 2024 to 31 August 2025 (S-107). Verified emissions, free
   allocation and the cross-sectoral correction factor are also published (S-108, S-109). TNAC,
   the auction calendar and the verified-emissions data therefore allow the **aggregate compliance
   balance for the year whose deadline is approaching** to be estimated before that deadline —
   **in the current regime only** (v1.1: the TNAC publication calendar is late May or June of the
   *following* year, so under the pre-2023 April deadline the balance for the compliance year was
   published after that deadline; see the timing finding below and gap G-13).
6. **The sign discipline.** If the aggregate balance is short, the deadline creates a forced,
   price-inelastic *buyer* and the allowance price should be pushed up into the deadline, with the
   concession partly unwound afterwards as the forced demand disappears. If the aggregate balance is
   long, there is no forced buyer, the deadline effect should be **absent**, and the residual
   pressure in the year should appear elsewhere (the December volume pattern the market monitor
   already reports, S-104). The candidate's prediction is therefore *two-sided*, and the negative
   case is as informative as the positive one: **a deadline premium that appears in every year is
   evidence against the candidate**, because it means the effect is not state-conditional.
7. **The counterparty side is not a compliance entity.** The seller into the deadline is a
   non-compliance holder — a bank, a fund or another non-compliance entity — and the market
   monitor's own data show the "number of entities invested in EUA futures and not regulated under
   the EU ETS Directive" rising to around 900 on average in 2024, a jump of about 15% year on year
   (S-104). This is the "who is mispriced" leg the prompt requires: the compliance officer is
   *forced* to transact and pays a premium; the non-compliance holder *chooses* to transact and
   collects it.

**Expected return source.** A compensation for supplying allowances to a date-bound, penalty-backed
mandated buyer — a *liquidity-provision premium*, not a risk premium for systematic exposure, and
not the carbon-price trend itself. The premium exists because the obligated buyer's demand is
insensitive to price near the deadline; the provider is compensated for the price risk of holding
the allowance into the window and for the concentration of the exit.

**Originality.**
- *Closest known work:* Borri, Liu, Tsyvinski and Wu (S-101) — a NBER working paper with a formal
  model and 2.7 million registry transactions, and the direct statement of both the effect and the
  mechanism; the earlier versions of the same project (S-111) are the same work, not separate
  evidence. The market-structure and pricing background is in the European Commission's own
  auctioning documentation (S-105), the Commission's TNAC communications (S-107), the national
  authority's auction-calendar notices (S-106) and the annual independent market report (S-104).
  The venue and rule documentation is S-102, S-103.
- *What is already known:* that purchases concentrate in the surrender month; that returns in that
  month are systematically high (about 10% on average); that the implied premium is of the order of
  €5 billion; that operator flow predicts future returns; that roughly 40% of operators do not
  trade in a given year; and that the premium is a payment to counterparties rather than a resource
  cost.
- *Substantive differentiator claimed here:* (i) **the regime** — the published sample ends in 2021
  and the deadline that is now law is a different deadline, so the published result is not a
  statement about the current regime; (ii) **the state** — the premium is predicted to be
  *existence-conditional* on the aggregate compliance balance, which is knowable before the
  deadline from regulator-published data (TNAC, verified emissions, the auction calendar and the
  MSR decision), so the condition is decision-time observable rather than an ex-post
  classification; (iii) **the falsification structure** — the candidate can be killed by a
  deadline premium that appears in surplus years as well as deficit years, which is the outcome
  that the published framing would lead one to expect.
- *Honest classification:* **published effect, with an unclaimed conditional structure and an
  unexamined regime.** This dossier must never be described as a discovery of the surrender-month
  premium — that is S-101's result. What is claimed is that the premium is conditional and that the
  condition is published in advance. The condition is a *conjecture*; nothing here establishes that
  the premium is state-conditional, and the kill conditions below are written so that it can fail.
- *Confidence in the comparison:* **moderate, and now checked.** The full text of S-101 (arXiv HTML
  rendering, v2) **has been read** in Cycle 5. The verdict: the paper does **not** condition the
  surrender-month premium on the aggregate surplus, so the candidate's central claim is not
  pre-empted; but the paper's *model* already makes the equilibrium price affine in the aggregate
  bank of allowances, so the state-conditional structure is an implication of the published model
  rather than an unclaimed idea about the mechanism; and the paper's own robustness construction
  works from **operator-level, bank-adjusted** shortfalls, which implies the aggregate balance is
  not the object that determines residual demand. The full text also supplies two facts that the
  v1.0 dossier did not have: the statement that the delayed-trading pattern "persists into the most
  recent phase of the EU ETS" (Phase IV, 2021 — a high-surplus year), and the fact that the paper's
  own counterfactual analysis of **staggered surrender deadlines** already reduces the premium by
  about 59%, which is directly relevant to the regime change the candidate relies on. **What remains
  unclaimed** is an *empirical* estimate of how the premium varies with the published aggregate
  balance across the regime change.

**Target scope.** EU allowances (EUA) on the ICE futures and options venue and, at lower frequency,
the EEX auction clearing price; the compliance window from the verification deadline (31 March) to
the surrender deadline (30 September from 2024; 30 April in the earlier regime); annual frequency
with one observation per year, and a pre-2024 sub-sample with one per year at the April deadline.
Regime conditions: exclude the ETS2 sector expansion (buildings and road transport, surrender
deadline 31 May from 2028) from any early sample; exclude years in which the *policy* cycle
dominates (the October-November annual review, the MSR decision and the free-allocation
adjustment) from the estimation window, and treat them as controls rather than as signal.

**Observables (knowable at decision time, conceptually).**
- The statutory calendar: verification deadline 31 March, surrender deadline 30 September from 2024
  (S-102, S-103) — published, statutory, not a forecast.
- The aggregate compliance balance: TNAC for the compliance year (published by the Commission in
  late May or June of the year of the deadline — **available before the deadline only from the 2023
  compliance year**), the verified-emissions dataset, the free-allocation and
  cross-sectoral-correction-factor decisions, and the EEX auction calendar with its volumes
  (S-105, S-106, S-107, S-108, S-109, S-121).
- The MSR's effect on the auction supply in the compliance window (the reserve's withholding
  schedule, which the Commission and the national authority both publish) (S-106, S-107).
- The published market data used as the mechanism check: EEX daily auction results and quarterly
  auction reports including participant counts (S-105).
- *Timing hazards to flag for the validator:* the surrender and compliance data for year Y are
  published by 6 October of Y+1 (S-110) — that is **after** the deadline, so they can only be used
  for an ex-post check of the state, never for the decision; the auction calendar and the MSR
  adjustment are published in advance but their *interpretation* is not; and the sample has one
  observation per year, so the effective sample is very small and every additional control
  (year fixed effects, the policy cycle) costs degrees of freedom.
- **The decisive timing finding of Cycle 5 (G-13):** the aggregate state itself has a publication
  calendar that must be checked against the decision date. The Commission publishes TNAC for
  emissions year Y in **late May or June of year Y+1** — TNAC for 2022 on 15 May 2023, for 2023 on
  3 June 2024 (S-121). Under the pre-2023 regime the surrender deadline for emissions of year Y was
  **30 April of year Y+1**, i.e. the aggregate balance for the compliance year was published
  **after** the deadline it was meant to inform, and the state a rule could have used before that
  deadline was the balance for year Y−2. Under the post-2023 regime (deadline 30 September of year
  Y+1) the balance for year Y is published **before** the deadline. **Consequence:** the
  *decision-time* form of this candidate can only be run from the **2023 compliance year** onwards —
  three observations so far (deadlines of 30 September 2024, 2025 and 2026), all in a period of large
  aggregate surplus, with **no deficit observation**. The *ex-post* form can be run over the whole
  published sample, but an ex-post state is a decomposition, not a rule.

**Rule sketch (descriptive, non-executable).** Once a year, after the Commission has published the
TNAC figure for the compliance year (which, in the current regime, happens in late May or June of
the year of the deadline) and the auction calendar for that year, and before the surrender
deadline: estimate the aggregate compliance balance for the year whose deadline is approaching as
(verified emissions for that year, net of the free allocation and the CSCF adjustment) minus the
allowances the system is holding net of what it has already surrendered — a quantity the published
TNAC series and the surrender data jointly bound. If the balance is short, form a long position in
the front-of-deadline allowance future, held to the deadline; if the balance is long, form no
position. Size the position by the *estimated shortfall* relative to the *published auction volume*
for the compliance window, and cap it at a pre-specified fraction of the contract's average daily
volume. Exit on the settlement of the deadline-week contract. Exclude any year in which the
October-November policy cycle or a link to the Swiss ETS produced a repricing, and record those
years as excluded rather than as failures. **Feasibility note (v1.1):** this rule can only be run
from the **2023 compliance year** onwards, because before that the TNAC for the compliance year was
published after its deadline (G-13) — three observations to date, none in a deficit year, so the
rule as written cannot yet be evaluated on a state switch.

**Persistence thesis.** The obligation is statutory and the penalty is a multiple of the allowance
price, so the deadline demand cannot be avoided; the compliance calendar is published a year ahead
in the directive; and the market's structure around it (a small number of non-compliance sellers
and a fragmented set of obligors) changes slowly. A premium that is paid annually to the party
that provides the allowance in the window is a structural transfer, not an anomaly.

**Constraints.** Cost/liquidity: the EUA futures market is liquid enough for institutional size but
is an order of magnitude smaller than rates or equity futures, and the spot market is thin; the
*effect* is large (about 10% in the published April sample) but so is the *noise* from the policy
cycle, so a small net edge after costs is possible and must be checked rather than assumed.
Capacity: a non-compliance participant is needed on the *supply* side of the trade, and a
participant that is not a compliance entity can be on either side; a pure compliance officer cannot
short. Market impact: in a one-year sample, a single position can move the price it is predicting.
Venue/operational: EUA futures are cash-settled on ICE with a published contract specification
whose expiry list changed when the deadline moved (S-102) — the contract calendar must be
reconstructed for the pre-2024 sample, because the April deadline and the September deadline imply
different expiries. Shorting constraints: shorting an allowance future is available but the
position carries the risk of an unscheduled policy announcement (the "carbon price crash" risk).

**Regime sensitivity, tail exposure, crash behavior, hidden beta.** Regime: the candidate is
*defined* by the compliance regime and by the annual policy cycle. Tail: the EUA market has
repeatedly had policy-driven 30–60% drawdowns, and a position held into the deadline is exposed
to them. Crash behaviour: the position is *long* the allowance into a window in which the policy
calendar is quiet, so it is exposed to a negative policy shock arriving without warning (a
re-based cap, a lower auction volume, a link to a cheap foreign market). Hidden beta: the long
allowance position is a long *carbon-transition* position, so it loads on the regulatory-trend
factor and on the energy complex, and the "premium" may be compensation for exactly that exposure
rather than for liquidity provision — a confound that the state-conditioning does not remove.

**Falsifiable predictions and rejection conditions.**
1. *Existence-conditionality (the primary test):* the deadline premium is present in years when
   the pre-deadline published aggregate balance is short and absent (or of the opposite sign) in
   years when it is long. **Kill:** the premium is of similar size in surplus and deficit years —
   in which case the effect is unconditional, the state adds nothing, and the candidate is a
   re-description of S-101. **Cycle-5 status: untestable in decision-time form, and partly
   pre-answered.** The decision-time test has at most three observations, none in a deficit year
   (G-13); and the published sample contains a high-surplus year (Phase IV, 2021) in which the
   delayed-trading pattern persisted, which is evidence against the sign flip. Both facts are
   recorded here rather than buried, and they are the reason the candidate is parked.
2. *Regime discontinuity:* the effect's month moves with the statutory deadline — April in the
   pre-2024 regime, September thereafter — and is not a fixed calendar month. **Kill:** the premium
   remains concentrated in April after the legal change, which would mean the April pattern is
   driven by something other than the surrender obligation.
3. *Mechanism check with published data:* the EEX auction volumes and participant counts rise in the
   compliance window of deficit years and not in surplus years (S-105). **Kill:** the published
   auction data show no such difference, which would undercut the demand-concentration mechanism.
4. *Reversal:* after the deadline, part of the pre-deadline concession unwinds (the price gives
   back part of the deadline move) in deficit years. **Kill:** there is no post-deadline reversal,
   which would mean the move is a permanent repricing rather than a concession, and there is no
   liquidity-provision premium to capture.
5. *Cost floor:* the estimated pre-deadline premium minus the round-turn cost in the EUA future
   (spread, commission, financing) is positive. **Kill:** it is not, in which case the candidate is
   a description and not a trade.
6. *Pre-registration:* the *rule* — long the allowance future if and only if the published balance
   is short — must be fixed before the outcome data are examined, and it must be evaluated on the
   pre-2024 April regime and the post-2023 September regime as two separate samples, with the
   sign-flip prediction stated in advance for both.

**Main confounders and rival explanations.**
- *The policy cycle* — the October-November review, the MSR decision, the free-allocation
  adjustment and the cap decisions move the allowance price far more than a deadline month
  normally does; the deadline is inside the calendar year but the *effect* may be an artefact of
  the surrounding announcements.
- *The energy complex* — EUA prices co-move with gas and power prices, so a "deadline premium"
  may be a power-sector hedging demand story: the fuel-switching channel is identified in the
  literature as a major driver of allowance prices.
- *Speculative positioning* — the ECB's own economic bulletin documents the role of non-compliance
  participants and of speculative flows in allowance price moves, which cuts both ways: it is an
  alternative explanation for the deadline premium and a supply-side explanation for who collects it.
- *Free allocation and the CSCF* — the aggregate balance depends on free allocation, which is
  adjusted ex post and may dominate the surrender obligation itself.
- *The banking constraint* — operators that bank a surplus in one year are the ones that can afford
  to miss the deadline in another; the "40% of operators do not trade" finding (S-101) is a
  reminder that the *marginal* obligor is not the average one.
- *Small sample* — one observation per year is a very weak basis for a conditional claim, and this
  is the most serious design weakness in the candidate.

**Data requirements and feasibility for a future independent validation.** (Specification only —
nothing is acquired or processed here.) Public: the statutory calendar (S-102, S-103); the TNAC
communications (S-107); the verified-emissions and free-allocation data and the Union Registry
public data and the EEA data viewer (S-108, S-109); the EEX auction calendars, daily auction
results and quarterly auction reports (S-105); the national authority's notices (S-106); the annual
market report (S-104). Commercial or venue-priced: the EUA futures price history (ICE settlement
data) and the EUTL transaction-level files (public but bulk, and the transaction data are
commissioned-research-grade). Feasibility: **medium-high** for the state and the calendar, and
**medium-low** for the price series and the sample size. The binding constraint is not data
availability but the **number of independent observations**: one per year against a state that
must be estimated, not observed — and, after the Cycle-5 timing finding (G-13), **only three
years in which the state was published before the decision at all**.

**Theory/evidence status.**
- *Conceptual rationale:* supported — the mechanism, including the "intermediary constraints
  translate residual demand into a surrender-month premium" step, is formalised in S-101, and the
  penalty-backed near-inelasticity of the obligated demand is a direct reading of the statute and
  the penalty schedule.
- *Mechanism supported by sources:* **yes** — the obligation, the penalty, the calendar, the
  auction calendar and the TNAC publication are all documented (S-102 … S-109).
- *Prior empirical support reported by others:* the surrender-month premium, the April
  concentration, the flow-return predictability and the €5 billion estimate are the authors' own
  (S-101) and are **not** reproduced here.
- *Unresolved:* whether the premium is state-conditional; whether it survives the deadline change;
  whether any part of it is a policy-cycle or fuel-switching artefact; and whether the published
  work already answers the state question.

**Remaining uncertainty / confidence.** Confidence that the obligation, the penalty and the
published state exist: **high**. Confidence that the deadline premium exists in the current
regime: **moderate** — the published result is strong but its sample ends in 2021, and the market
monitors' own reading of the shift is that demand was "spread out", which is the opposite of a
sharp deadline premium. Confidence that the premium is *state-conditional*: **low** — this is the
candidate's conjecture; the full text of S-101 (read in Cycle 5) neither tests it nor supports it,
and its Phase IV persistence statement shows that a very large surplus did not switch the effect
off. Confidence that it is tradeable after costs: **low-to-moderate** — thin market, small sample,
large policy-event risk. **Feasibility of the decision-time rule: low** — the state is published
before the decision only from the 2023 compliance year, leaving three observations, none of them in
a deficit year (G-13). Priority score **58/100** (down from 70: the first check was resolved
favourably, but the model-implied conditional structure, the contradicting persistence statement
and the decision-time sample cut the other way); rank 10 of 10 retained-or-parked candidates at the
time of writing. **Status: parked (P-008).** **Concrete next question (dated, and it is a
data-accumulation question, not a research question):** the unblocking event is the accumulation of
enough post-2023 compliance years for the *ex-post* balance to move across a sign change with at
least one observation on each side. The next published TNAC is for emissions year 2026, expected in
late May or June 2027 (S-121's calendar); the corresponding surrender deadline is 30 September
2027. **Before 2029, the candidate cannot be validated in any decision-time form, and the register
should not spend further effort on it.** A secondary question, worth one search only: whether the
published literature has yet replicated the April premium in the post-2023 September regime — if a
replication exists, the *regime* residual is closed too and the candidate should be rejected rather
than parked.

---

## IDEA-014 — RIN-DEADLINE: the US Renewable Fuel Standard's compliance-deadline obligation, and the published net position that decides its sign

- **ID / short name:** IDEA-014 / RIN-DEADLINE
- **Date added:** 2026-10-20 · **Cycle:** 6 · **Status:** candidate · **Version:** 1.1
- **Change notes:** v1.0 — initial dossier. New mechanism family for the register: a statutory,
  date-bound purchase obligation on a *credit* in a US regulatory market, screened as frontier
  family 1 ("statutory, date-bound obligations outside the EU ETS"). The candidate is the
  **state-conditioned sign** of the deadline effect; the EU ETS version of the same template is
  published (S-101) and is a baseline, not a discovery.
  **v1.1 (Cycle 35) — G-24 and G-25 closed as bounded negatives for the conditional version:** the
  claimed RGGI replacement state and candidate IDEA-016 were never written in any artifact, so the
  RGGI lead is an unwritten lead rather than a candidate; the conditional version stays retired and
  the state-free existence limb is retained. No score change (52.0).
  **Cycle 52 — no score change (52.0), rank 5 unchanged.** The state-free existence limb was checked for
  any 2025–2026 source bearing on the RFS compliance deadline, the RIN price and its pass-through, or a
  pre-deadline RIN state: **nothing was found**, so the limb **remains inconclusive** — neither a
  positive finding nor a refutation — and **P-011 stands**; no gap was opened on it. One point was
  sharpened for a future reopening: a pre-deadline state must satisfy **both** halves of the requirement
  independently — **published before the deadline** *and* **sign-determining rather than merely
  informative** — and the RIN analogue fails the second half because the published projection is set
  against the agency's own supply projection, so the projected net position is near zero by construction
  (S-184, S-201).

**One-sentence hypothesis.** Obligated parties under the US Renewable Fuel Standard must retire
Renewable Identification Numbers (RINs) against their annual renewable volume obligations by a
statutory, published compliance deadline; because the obligation is not price-sensitive, because
its size is fixed in advance by published regulation, and because the industry is either a net
buyer or a net seller of RINs depending on published generation against published obligations, the
*sign* of the deadline effect is determined by a state that is published **before** the decision —
so the concession is paid by the obligated party into the deadline and reverts after it.

**Plain-language description.** The US Renewable Fuel Standard forces refiners and importers to
blend a specified volume of renewable fuel each year, and they meet that obligation by retiring
"RINs" — tradable credits issued by the EPA for each gallon of qualifying renewable fuel. EPA
sets each year's volumes in a rule published months ahead, and the annual compliance report (and
the RIN retirement it evidences) is due on a deadline that the regulation itself fixes and
publishes. In the last weeks before that deadline, any obligated party that has not yet covered
its obligation must either buy RINs (if it generated too few) or sell them (if it generated too
many) — and it must do so on a schedule that is not price-sensitive. If the published generation
data say the industry is short, the deadline creates forced *buying*, the RIN price is pushed up
into the deadline, and the forced buyer pays a premium that is expected to unwind afterwards. If
the published data say the industry is long, the sign reverses. Which of those two worlds you are
in is **published before you have to take a position** — which is the property this candidate is
built on.

**Mechanism.** A price-insensitive, date-bound flow (here: a regulated party's obligation to
retire credits by a statutory deadline) meets a credit whose price is set by a small, specialist
market. The template is published in another venue — the EU ETS surrender-month premium
(S-101: surrender-month returns "averaging more than 10% in Aprils") — so the mechanism is borrowed
and is not claimed as new. The RIN price itself is a studied object in the peer-reviewed
literature (S-134 on RIN prices and the ex-post cost of the programme; S-135 on RIN-price
pass-through into refined fuels). What makes this instance different is not the mechanism but the
**calendar and the state**: the obligation's size is codified in advance (S-128), the deadline is
published by regulation (S-129), and the direction of the obligated flow is computable from
published standards and published generation data.

**Expected return source.** Compensation for supplying (or, in the net-long state, demanding)
liquidity to a forced, dated, price-insensitive participant at the compliance deadline; the
concession is expected to revert after the deadline as the obligation is satisfied and the
forced flow ends.

**Originality.** The EU ETS instance of this template is published (S-101) and is in the register's
baseline set. Within the US RIN market, **no located work states the compliance-deadline effect
with its sign determined by the published net obligated position**. What is published is: the price
and its pass-through (S-134, S-135); the market's quality, liquidity and transaction costs, and
the documented tendency to price near-expiry vintages *above* longer-lived ones (S-133); and EPA's
own published acknowledgement that market participants need time "to adjust their compliance
strategies … and for the RIN market to react" to exemption decisions before the deadline (S-131).
The last is an institutional statement that the market reacts to compliance news — with no sign
and no magnitude. Differentiator: a **two-sided, state-conditioned deadline claim** in a market
where the sign is an empirical question, plus a tradeable futures venue (S-136) and a published
price series (S-132). Honest classification: informed synthesis of a published mechanism template
in an unclaimed instance, with the sign untested.

**Target scope.** US RFS D3–D6 RIN vintages; compliance years 2010–2025 (with 2026 and 2027
partially observable); annual decision windows centred on the published compliance deadline;
cross-section across RIN types and across the net-position state; holding 1–10 sessions into and
out of the deadline.

**Observables (decision time).** The annual percentage standards, codified and including two
future years (40 CFR 80.1405 — S-128); the annual rule setting the applicable volumes and its
effective date (S-130); the **published** annual compliance reporting deadline (40 CFR 80.1451(f)
— S-129) and any published extension of it (S-131); EPA's published RIN generation data by fuel
type and D code (weekly, lagged); EPA's published weekly volume-weighted-average RIN prices by
transfer date (S-132); small-refinery-exemption decisions and reallocations (published; S-131
shows they move deadlines); ICE D6 RIN vintage futures and the CME D4/D6 (OPIS) futures (S-136).

**Falsifiable predictions and rejection conditions.**
1. *The sign follows the published net position.* In compliance years where published generation
   falls short of the published obligation net of carryover and exemptions, the obligated party is
   a net buyer: the relevant vintage's price rises into the deadline and falls back after it. In
   years where it is long, the sign reverses. **Kill:** the pre-deadline/post-deadline difference
   has the same sign in both kinds of year — i.e. the published state does not determine the sign.
   This is the candidate's core test and it is two-sided.
2. *It is a deadline effect, not a level effect.* The pre/post-deadline difference is larger for
   vintages that expire at the deadline than for vintages that do not, in the same week.
   **Kill:** no difference by expiry vintage.
3. *Magnitude follows the obligation, not the market.* The size of the move scales with the
   published obligation shortfall relative to published generation, not with RIN market turnover.
   **Kill:** no relation to the shortfall.
4. *The moved deadline is a test, not an exception.* The 2025 compliance deadline moved from
   1 September 2026 to 1 October 2026 by published rule (S-131); the 2026 deadline is 31 March 2027
   (S-129, S-131). A date that moves at short notice is a *stronger* test than a fixed one, and a
   **no-response result around either date is informative in both directions** — it would weaken
   the deadline mechanism rather than merely fail to support it.
5. *Cost floor.* The gross pre/post-deadline difference must exceed the round-trip cost in the venue
   actually used. **Kill:** the difference is smaller than the cost. Given the published
   market-quality evidence (S-133), this is the most likely kill.

**Confounders.** The "blend wall" and vintage-specific scarcity (a D6 can be scarce for reasons
unrelated to the deadline); EPA rule changes and waivers, including the partial waiver of the 2025
cellulosic requirement in the same rule that set the 2026–27 standards (S-130); small-refinery
exemptions and reallocations, which move the deadline and the obligation (S-131); the carryover
mechanism, which changes the net position; the market's documented opacity and squeeze history
(2013), which can dominate any deadline effect; seasonal ethanol and diesel price moves; and the
filters and truncation in the published price series itself (S-132), which can manufacture
artefactual level shifts in the very series the test would use.

**Data requirements.** (Specification only — nothing is acquired or processed here.) EPA's
published weekly RIN price series by D code and transfer date (S-132); EPA's published RIN
generation data by fuel type (weekly, lagged); the codified standards (S-128); the annual rules and
their effective dates (S-130); the published compliance deadlines and any extensions (S-129,
S-131); ICE and CME futures prices and open interest for the vintages (S-136). Feasibility:
**medium** — every *input* is published, but the published price series is a lagged weekly
volume-weighted average from EPA's own moderation system with published filters and truncation,
the generation data's lag leaves the last weeks of the compliance year unpublished before the
deadline, and the venue's liquidity is not documented in the public record.

**Theory/evidence status.**
- *Conceptual rationale:* supported by analogy to a published mechanism in another venue (S-101)
  and to the general literature on price-insensitive, dated flows; the RIN credit has a studied,
  economically measurable price (S-134, S-135).
- *Mechanism supported by sources:* yes for the participant, the constraint, the dated obligation
  and — decisively — the **publication calendar** (S-128, S-129, S-130, S-131). The
  state-conditioned sign claim is an unresolved conjecture.
- *Prior empirical support reported by others:* for the components only. **No located work states
  the deadline effect with the sign determined by the published net obligated position.**
- *Unresolved:* the sign and size of the deadline effect (gap **G-15**) and the cost floor (S-133).

**Constraints.** Cost/liquidity: the published market-quality evidence runs against the candidate —
roughly one-tenth the liquidity of comparable commodity futures and transaction costs peaking at
five times theirs (S-133) — and the futures venue's own liquidity is undocumented publicly
(S-136), so the cost model must be justified independently rather than assumed. Capacity: bounded
by the size of the obligated party's net requirement and by venue depth, and the other participants
who supply the same liquidity see the same published state. Financing/margin: in the net-short
industry state the trade is *short* a credit with a documented squeeze history (2013), and margin
is set on a thin market. Venue/operational: the futures are physically delivered into EPA's
Moderated Transaction System (S-136), so exit depends on EMTS processing and on the delivery
calendar. Shorting constraints: the position's sign is short precisely in the states most likely
to squeeze. Model risk: the state requires a *net* obligated position, reconstructed from published
generation, published obligations, carryover and exemptions; an error in that reconstruction flips
the sign of the trade.

**Regime sensitivity, tail exposure, crash behavior, hidden beta.** Regime: the effect exists only
near compliance deadlines, and the net-position state that carries it changes slowly. Tail: the
2013 RIN squeeze is the tail case, and deadline concentration multiplies it. Crash behaviour: a
regulatory shock — a waiver, an exemption wave, a reallocation — can remove the obligation after
the fact, and EPA has moved deadlines at short notice (S-131). Hidden beta: a long RIN position is
a long biofuel-policy position; a policy change that reduces the obligation removes the state and
the signal together. Correlated risks: all RIN types share one obligation and one deadline, so the
book is a single policy bet.

**Remaining uncertainty / confidence.** Confidence that the obligation, its size, the deadline and
the state inputs are all **published before the decision**: **high** (S-128, S-129, S-130, S-131)
— this is the candidate's defining property and the exact property IDEA-013 lacked. Confidence that
a harvestable concession survives the cost floor: **low-to-moderate**, because the published
market-quality evidence is directly against it (S-133). Confidence that the sign is state-determined:
**unverified — this is the candidate's first empirical test** (G-15). Priority score **60/100**;
rank 7 of 7. **Cycle 13: score unchanged at 60.0; rank 7 → 6.** **Concrete next question:** does the published weekly RIN price series show a
systematic pre-deadline/post-deadline difference whose sign follows the published net obligated
position, and is that difference larger than the venue's round-trip cost?

**Cycle 6 screen (G-13 publication timing; G-12 sign or existence).**
- **G-13 — the state is published before the decision: PASS, and this is the candidate's defining
  property.** Three independent regulatory facts carry it: the obligation's **size** is codified in
  the CFR for two future years (40 CFR 80.1405: 2026 total renewable fuel standard 15.50%, 2027
  15.78%; amended 91 FR 16487, 1 April 2026 — S-128); the **deadline** is not merely knowable but
  *published by regulation* — "The annual compliance reporting deadline will be calculated in
  accordance with paragraph (f)(1)(i) of this section and published on EPA's website" (40 CFR
  80.1451(f)(1)(iii) — S-129); and where the deadline has moved, it moved **by published rule**
  (the 2025 deadline moved from 1 September 2026 to 1 October 2026 — S-131), with the next deadline
  stated in the same instrument (31 March 2027). This is the candidate IDEA-013 could not be.
- **G-12 — the conditioning changes the sign or the existence of the effect: PASS on the sign
  limb.** The published EU ETS instance of this template (S-101) has no such state: the candidate's
  claim is that the deadline effect is **two-sided** — a net-buyer state produces a rise into the
  deadline and a fall after it, a net-seller state the reverse — so the published state determines
  the sign of the trade, and the claim is falsifiable in both directions. That is a sign claim, not
  a size claim, and it is what makes the candidate more than a re-description of S-101.
- **Effect on the dossier: new candidate at 60.0, status `candidate` (retained, not shortlisted).**
  The two conditions it must clear before it can be validated are already named: **G-15** (does the
  published price series show the state-determined pre/post-deadline difference, and is it larger
  than the cost) and the cost floor from S-133. No validation specification is written in Cycle 6.

**Cycle 13 screen (G-13 publication timing; G-12 sign or existence) — no score change.**
- **G-13 — PASS, unchanged.** The obligation's size (codified), the deadline (published by
  regulation) and the deadline's own history of moving by published rule are untouched by Cycle 13.
- **G-12 — PASS on the sign limb, unchanged.** The two-sided, state-determined sign claim is
  untouched; no published work found in Cycle 13 claims the state-determined sign.
- **Cycle-13 frontier screen result relevant to this candidate.** The register screened the sibling
  cap-and-trade family — **California Cap-and-Invest** — for a second instance of the deadline
  template (S-163, S-164, S-165, S-168, S-173, S-174). The verdict was *no candidate*, for a
  reason worth recording next to IDEA-014: California's own price-containment reserve (a
  state-triggered, date-bound supply valve whose trigger is the published preceding auction's
  settlement price against published tier thresholds, and whose schedule sits in the regulation)
  has **never been used** in the modern era (S-163), and the annual surrender obligation is met with
  near-100% compliance, so the price-insensitive demand at the deadline is *satisfied in advance*.
  The template is therefore not automatically portable: what makes IDEA-014's version interesting is
  the *state* (the published net obligated position) that decides whether the deadline demand is
  tight — and no analogous published state exists in the California program.
- **Effect on the dossier: no score change (60.0); rank 7 → 6** (rank change is an artefact of
  IDEA-001's rejection and the re-scores of IDEA-004 and IDEA-009). **G-15 remains open** and no
  validation specification is written.

**Cycle 30 G-15 advance — the deadline's movement is rule-determined, and the sign-determining state
exists before the deadline only in *projected* form; no score change (60.0); G-15 sharpened into G-20.**
- **What was searched.** G-15 asks whether the published RIN price series shows a state-determined
  pre/post-deadline difference. This cycle did not look for a price pattern — that is a validation
  question and is out of scope here — but for the **state's publication status before the deadline**,
  because that determines whether a two-sided sign test can be run at all.
- **Finding 1 — the deadline's movement is rule-determined (S-184).** The EPA's 16 October 2026 proposed
  rule states the **automatic extension mechanism** at 40 CFR 80.1451(f)(1)(i)(C): if the EPA neither
  finalises nor withdraws the proposed revision to the 2026 RFS standards within 12 months of its
  publication, the next quarterly reporting deadline moves. The candidate's prediction 4 ("a moved
  deadline is a test, not an exception") therefore rests on a **rule** rather than on discretion, which
  strengthens the design, and it creates a **second dated test event** — the 2026 compliance year,
  currently 31 March 2027 — whose trigger is published rather than discretionary.
- **Finding 2 — the sign-determining state is only *projected* before the deadline (S-184, S-185).** For
  the 2025 compliance year the EPA *projected* an exempted Renewable Volume Obligation of **990 million
  RINs**; the proposed rule states that the relief leaves obligated parties with obligations **770 million
  RINs fewer** than the EPA projected in the Set 2 Rule; and the rule states explicitly that **"reported
  2025 exempted fuel volumes will not be available until after the 2025 compliance reporting deadline of
  October 1, 2026"**. The *realised* net obligated position — the state that decides the sign — is
  therefore published **after** the deadline it informs, while the *projected* position is published
  before it. EPA's public data estate does publish the obligation side as a **named data category**
  ("Renewable Volume Obligations"), monthly generation and volume-production files on roughly a
  one-month lag, and a participant-level **RIN Holdings** report by balance year and balance quarter
  (S-185) — so a *projected* state is constructible in principle, but it is an inference from published
  projections and holdings, not a published net-obligated-position series.
- **Effect on the dossier: no score change (60.0); G-15 stays open but is now a precise question; one
  new gap.**
  - **G-15 (sharpened, still open):** does the published RIN price series show a state-determined
    pre/post-deadline difference, *conditional on the projected* net obligated position? The projection
    is the only pre-deadline state, so the two-sided sign test must be run on it, and the realised
    position is a post-hoc control, never an input.
  - **New gap G-20:** is the *projected* net obligated position a sufficient sign-determining state, or
    does projection error swamp it? The projection has already been revised once — from the Set 2 Rule's
    projection to the 770 million RIN relief — and if the revision band exceeds the state that decides
    the sign, the candidate's sign test is not runnable and the candidate must be re-specified or parked.
  - Scores unchanged: mechanism 4/5; evidence 2/5 (no published price-pattern work located; the new
    sources are legal and institutional, not empirical); novelty 3/5; falsifiability 3/5 (the
    rule-determined deadline strengthens the design, but the **cost floor** (S-133) remains the binding
    kill condition and the sign test now carries a projection-error caveat); resilience 2/5 (unchanged —
    squeeze risk on the short side); data 4/5 (unchanged — the obligation side is published in principle,
    but the realised state is post-deadline, which is a constraint the dossier already carried).
  - **No validation specification is written.** IDEA-014 has never had a validation specification, and
    none may be written for it until G-24 is closed. *(Cross-reference corrected in Cycle 32: the Cycle-30
    text of this line read "V-008 stays withdrawn", which was wrong — V-008 belongs to IDEA-009 PERP-FUND,
    which is active and executable. IDEA-014 has no V-number at all.)* The candidate remains at the
    bottom of the ranking, unchanged in score and rank.

### Cycle 32 (2026-10-20) — G-20 closed against the state as specified; the candidate is re-scored to 52.0
and takes a hard-stop flag on its sign-determining state

- **The gap is closed, and the answer is that the projection error does swamp the state.** G-20 asked
  whether the *projected* net obligated position is a sufficient sign-determining state. It is not, and
  the register can now say so quantitatively rather than as a worry.
  - **The projected net position is near zero by construction.** The Set 2 final rule (S-201) sets the
    volume requirement against the agency's own supply projection — its statutory factor analysis works
    from the "Analyzed Volumes" and states that "the total quantity of renewable fuel we project will be
    supplied to the U.S. to meet these volume requirements". So the *projected* net obligated position —
    the quantity the candidate wants to read the sign from — is a small residual of two large projections
    that the agency has already made consistent.
  - **The one signed component of that projection has been measured, and the error is +78%.** The EPA
    projected a 2025 exempted RVO of **990 million RINs** in the Set 2 Rule (S-201); the actual 2025
    exempted RVO, from the 31 August 2026 small-refinery-exemption decisions recorded in S-184, is
    **1,760 million RINs**. **Projection error: +770 million RINs, +78%** (underlying fuel volume: 7.55
    billion gallons projected against 13.38 billion actual, **+5.83 billion gallons, +77%**). The error is
    *structurally* one-sided: the projection is "a three-year average of the actual exempted fuel volumes
    from 2022 through 2024" (S-201) — a lagged average of past decisions, not a forecast of this year's
    exemption wave, which the EPA decided in a single batch on 31 August 2026.
  - **The scale makes the swamp concrete.** The 2026 and 2027 total applicable volumes are **26.81** and
    **27.02 billion RINs**, with SRE reallocation volumes of **0.99** and **1.04 billion RINs** (S-203). A
    770-million-RIN projection error is **78% of the entire annual SRE reallocation volume** that the
    agency itself treated as material enough to revise the standards; and it is roughly **2.9% of the
    total applicable volume**, i.e. of the same order as the net position a projection error of that
    size would have to resolve.
  - **A projected shortfall is institutionally absorbed, not expressed as a binding market obligation.**
    The one category where a projected shortfall actually occurred is instructive: for 2025 cellulosic the
    EPA projected 1.21 billion gallons against a 1.38 billion RIN requirement — "a shortfall of
    approximately 13 percent" — and the response was "implementation of the **cellulosic waiver
    authority** for 2025" (S-201), i.e. the obligation was waived, not bought. The RFS's architecture
    (waiver authority, SRE reallocation, carryover RINs) buffers mismatches between obligation and
    supply; a shortage state is not allowed to express itself as a forced market purchase.
  - **The realised state is published only after the deadline** (S-184: "reported 2025 exempted fuel
    volumes will not be available until after the 2025 compliance reporting deadline of October 1,
    2026"), so the *realised* net position is unusable as a decision-time state, and the *projected* one
    does not determine the sign.
- **Effect on the dossier: score 60.0 → 52.0; G-20 CLOSED against the state; a hard-stop flag; G-24
  opened; the candidate's testable core is restated.**
  - **Mechanism 4 → 3.** The chain "negative net obligated position → forced deadline-bound, price-
    insensitive buying → a concession the deadline rents" has a broken first link: the projected position
    is calibrated to zero, and where a projected shortfall does occur the obligation is waived rather
    than bought. The deadline-cluster *existence* claim (a statutory deadline concentrates a compliance
    flow) is untouched; the *state-determined sign* link is not.
  - **Falsifiability 3 → 2.** Prediction 2 of the dossier ("the published net obligated position
    determines the sign") is now known to be untestable as stated, not merely untested: the projected
    position does not determine the sign, and its only signed component carried a +78% error in the one
    year in which it has been measured. One of three predictions is dead as written.
  - **Evidence 2 → 2 (unchanged), novelty 3 → 3 (unchanged), resilience 2 → 2 (unchanged), data 4 → 4
    (unchanged).** The new sources are regulatory documents, not price-pattern evidence, so the
    evidence dimension does not move — the register records this rather than inflating it with
    institutional material. The *inputs* are public; what fails is the informational content of the
    state, which is priced in the mechanism and falsifiability dimensions.
  - **Hard-stop flag attached:** *the sign-determining state as specified requires look-ahead information
    (the realised position) or is uninformative by construction (the projected position); the candidate
    must not be specified, ranked as a state-conditioned candidate, or presented as a top opportunity
    until a replacement state is found.*
  - **The candidate is retained, not parked**, because its **existence** limb remains testable without any
    state: G-15's first half — does the published weekly RIN price series show a systematic pre-deadline /
    post-deadline difference at all, and is it larger than the venue's round-trip cost (S-133)? — is a
    state-free test of the deadline-cluster's existence and size. What is suspended is the *conditional*
    version. *(A reader who prefers to park the candidate outright can do so without loss: the register's
    own rule would then apply — a parked candidate keeps its score but sits outside the ranking and
    writes no specification. The register does not park it because parking would discard a testable
    existence claim that the register has no other candidate covering.)*
  - **New gap G-24.** Is there a published, **pre-deadline**, **signed** state for the RIN deadline
    cluster that is **not** the outcome itself — for example the carryover-RIN balance, or the
    pre-deadline RIN price and positioning (S-185) — such that the test can be run without look-ahead and
    without circularity? If none exists, the candidate's state-conditioned version is unrunnable and the
    candidate is a state-free existence test only.

### Cycle 35 (2026-10-20) — G-24 and G-25 closed as bounded negatives for the conditional version; the claimed RGGI replacement state turns out never to have existed

- **The claim that has to be corrected.** The Cycle-33 source-ledger header stated that G-24 was closed
  **in IDEA-014's favour** because "a replacement state exists in the RGGI CO₂ allowance market,
  producing new candidate **IDEA-016 RGGI-COMPLIANCE**", and the Cycle-34 handoff repeated it. **No
  such candidate, source or verdict exists in any artifact** — checked file by file and against the full
  git history on 2026-10-20. IDEA-016 was never written; the "Cycle 33 sources" and "Cycle 33 gap
  verdicts" blocks were never in the ledger; the identifier is **reserved and unused**.
- **What survives, and on what basis.** The *Cycle-34 conclusion* — that no **pre-deadline**, **signed**,
  **non-circular** replacement state was found for the RIN cluster, so the conditional version of the
  candidate is retired to a state-free existence test — is independently supportable on its own terms and
  is what Cycle 35 records. **G-24 and G-25 are closed as bounded negatives for the conditional
  version.** The RGGI lead is logged as an **unwritten lead** (no dossier, no source, no verdict) and is
  **not** a candidate: a later cycle may pursue it only by writing it from primary sources under the next
  free IDEA identifier, which is **IDEA-017** (IDEA-016 stays reserved).
- **What is unchanged.** IDEA-014 retains its **state-free existence limb** — the G-15 existence test
  (is there a post-deadline difference at all, and is it larger than the venue's round-trip cost,
  S-133?) — which remains runnable without look-ahead. The score stays at **52.0**, the hard stop on the
  conditional version stays, and the candidate stays **retained, not parked**, because the existence
  claim is a testable residual the register has no other candidate covering. **Rank 5, unchanged.**
- **Score: 52.0, unchanged.** Mechanism 3 → 3, evidence 2 → 2, novelty 3 → 3, falsifiability 2 → 2,
  resilience 2 → 2, data 4 → 4. No new source bears on the candidate this cycle.

#### Cycles 36–51 consolidated note — IDEA-014 · no source bearing on this dossier; the state-free existence limb stays **inconclusive**
- Across Cycles 36–51 the register ran the family screens **R-018 … R-031** (the Rule 201 short-sale price
  test, the ex-dividend family, the closing-price family and the rest, each a bounded negative), the
  **G-29** execution and **G-30** exhaustion on IDEA-010, the **G-21** null maintenance and the Cycle-52

  source bearing on IDEA-014.** Two things remain true and must keep being stated precisely:
  1. The candidate's **state-free existence limb** (the G-15 test — is there *any* post-deadline difference,
     and is it larger than the venue's round-trip cost, S-133?) is **inconclusive**. It is neither a
     positive finding nor a refutation, and it must never be described as either.
  2. The **conditional version is retired**: G-24 and G-25 are closed as bounded negatives (no
     pre-deadline, signed, non-circular replacement state exists), and the RGGI lead remains an **unwritten
     lead** — no dossier, no source, no verdict — with the next free identifier **IDEA-017** (IDEA-016
     stays reserved).
- **Score: 52.0, unchanged. Rank 5, unchanged.** The candidate stays **retained, not parked**, because the
  existence claim is a testable residual the register has no other candidate covering.

#### Cycle 52 block — IDEA-014 · the existence limb stays inconclusive, and Cycle 52 sharpened *what a future pre-deadline state would have to be*
- **What Cycle 52 did.** A bounded check for any 2025–2026 source bearing on the US Renewable Fuel
  Standard's compliance-deadline obligation, on the RIN price and its pass-through, or on the pre-deadline
  RIN state. **Nothing was found.** The Cycle-52 corpus (S-252 … S-266) is entirely IFRS-adoption
  literature and venue-halt / circuit-breaker literature; neither touches the RFS. **The existence limb
  therefore remains inconclusive, and P-011 (IDEA-015) stands.** No new gap is opened on this limb, and
  the conditional version stays retired.
- **What Cycle 52 *did* add — and it is a specification-sharpening point, not a candidate.** The R-032
  family screen produced the register's clearest published example of the **opposite** situation: a
  decision-time state that is **published before the decision, in real time, and continuously** — the LULD
  price bands are "republish[ed] … every 30 seconds" to the public (S-260) — and that example is useful
  here precisely because it *fails* for a reason that mirrors the RFS candidate's problem. For LULD, the
  state is public but carries **no sign**: the band is a symmetric constraint, and the tradeable
  consequence is a *cost* (wider spreads, higher realised volatility, volume concentration in the least
  liquid names) rather than a *direction* (S-259, S-266). For the RFS deadline, the candidate's problem is
  the mirror image: the state that would carry a **sign** (the net obligated position) is published only
  **after** the deadline (S-184), and the published pre-deadline proxy (the agency's own projection)
  carries **no sign**, because it is set against the agency's own projection of supply, so the projected
  net position is near zero by construction (S-201). **Both halves of the register's standing requirement
  are therefore jointly necessary and independently missing**: a pre-deadline state must be (i) published
  before the deadline *and* (ii) sign-determining rather than merely informative. A future G-24 reopening
  must look for a state that satisfies **both**, and any candidate that proposes only a published
  pre-deadline state will fail the second half — which is the RIN analogue of the "no sign" objection that
  closed R-031 and, in the case of a market-wide trigger, the "beta" objection that closed R-032.
- **Score: 52.0, unchanged.** Mechanism 3 → 3, evidence 2 → 2, novelty 3 → 3, falsifiability 2 → 2,
  resilience 2 → 2, data 4 → 4. **Rank 5, unchanged.** No validation specification is written for
  IDEA-014, and none may be written until a pre-deadline, signed state is identified.
