## IDEA-015 — PLAN-SUPPLY: pre-announced, non-discretionary insider supply as a dated, published, price-insensitive shock

- **ID / short name:** IDEA-015 / PLAN-SUPPLY
- **Date added:** 2026-10-20 · **Cycle:** 30 · **Status:** **parked (P-011)** · **Version:** 1.1
- **Change notes:** v1.0 — new dossier, entered as a *screened family* result of Cycle 30. The
  register's first candidate whose decision-time state is published by **corporate disclosure law**
  rather than by a market operator, a regulator or an exchange. Entered parked because the nearest
  published evidence is about a *different event* (plan adoption around earnings, S-183) and the
  effect's size is most likely below the cost floor for most names.

**One-sentence hypothesis.** Officers and directors who sell under a Rule 10b5-1 plan sell on a
schedule they chose months earlier and cannot revise without resetting a regulatory cooling-off
period; because the plan's adoption, duration and maximum aggregate size are disclosed quarterly and
the remaining capacity is reported on Form 4, a security's *future scheduled insider supply* is a
published, decision-time state — and the price pressure that supply creates is a dated concession.

**Plain-language description.** An executive who wants to sell shares signs a plan months in
advance: the plan fixes roughly how much, over what window, and usually at what prices. Since
December 2022 the SEC has required the company to publish the plan's adoption date, its duration and
its maximum aggregate size every quarter, and the insider's Form 4 reports each sale with a
"10b5-1 plan" box and the plan's adoption date — sometimes with the shares the plan still has left.
The seller cannot change course mid-window without restarting a 90-day cooling-off period. So the
market can *see* the supply coming. The candidate's question is whether seeing it is worth anything.

**Mechanism (causal chain).**
1. *Rule and safe harbour.* Rule 10b5-1(c) offers an affirmative defence to a trade made under a
   plan adopted while the insider is not in possession of material non-public information, which
   specifies the amount, the price or price range and the timing, and over which the insider
   exercises no subsequent influence; the 2022 amendments extend the defence, impose a mandatory
   cooling-off period (the later of 90 days after adoption and two business days after the filing of
   the 10-Q/10-K covering the quarter of adoption), bar overlapping plans, and require plans to be
   adopted and operated in good faith (S-182).
2. *The flow is scheduled and non-discretionary.* A plan's amount and timing are fixed in advance and
   the insider cannot accelerate into a price move without invalidating the safe harbour and
   restarting the cooling-off period (S-182). This is the register's recurring "dated,
   price-insensitive, knowable-in-advance" structure, with the difference that the participant's
   constraint is a *legal safe harbour* rather than a regulatory quota or an index rule.
3. *The state is published.* Item 408(a) of Regulation S-K requires quarterly disclosure in the
   10-Q/10-K of trading arrangements, their adoption and termination dates, their duration and the
   **aggregate number of securities to be sold or purchased**; Form 4 reports each plan sale within
   two business days with the plan checkbox and the plan's adoption date, and in at least some
   filings the plan's **remaining** capacity (S-182, S-187).
4. *Expected price pressure.* Scheduled supply arrives against finite liquidity; liquidity providers
   who know the schedule can pre-position, so the concession is paid before or during the execution
   window rather than at a fixed instant.
5. *Reversal.* When the plan's capacity is exhausted or its window closes, the scheduled supply stops
   and the price pressure unwinds — unless a new plan rolls in.
6. *Hence the claim.* Scheduled insider supply is a dated, published, price-insensitive supply shock
   whose price pressure is predictable from the published schedule.

**Expected return source.** A liquidity concession paid by informed liquidity providers to
pre-position ahead of scheduled, non-discretionary supply that the market can see but cannot
accelerate away; the return is the pre-window price pressure (or its absence) net of borrow and
execution costs.

**Originality.**
- *Closest known work:* the insider-trading literature and the buyback/insider-purchase effect
  rejected as a known baseline in R-005; the SEC's own adopting release and economic analysis
  (S-182) and its 2022 proposed release (S-183), whose literature review records that a study of
  Form 144 data on plan trades (January 2016 – May 2020) documents plan-based selling behaviour, and
  that work by Hugon and Lee on plans and strategic trading around earnings announcements finds
  abnormal returns of roughly **−2% to −3%** for plans that execute a sale in the window between a
  plan's adoption and that quarter's earnings announcement, **no price drop** after the earnings
  announcement, and no such effect where the cooling-off period exceeded 60 days.
- *What is already known:* (i) insider transactions predict returns — a known baseline (R-005);
  (ii) plan-based sales dominate the modern insider-selling population (S-187, a vendor statistic,
  unaudited); (iii) **plan timing has published price effects attached to the *adoption* window and
  to information events, not to the mechanical execution of a disclosed schedule** (S-183); (iv) the
  rule, the disclosure regime and the cooling-off period are published law (S-182).
- *Substantive differentiator claimed here:* the **state** — a security's *remaining scheduled
  insider supply*, disclosed quarterly under Item 408(a) and updated on Form 4 — used as a
  decision-time gate on a supply-pressure trade, with the trade's direction determined by whether the
  remaining supply is being executed into strength or weakness. This is not R-005's "insider buys →
  returns" channel (different participant constraint: no discretion, a fixed maximum size, a
  published schedule) and it is not S-183's "plan adoption is strategically timed" channel (different
  event: execution, not adoption).
- *Honest classification:* **an incremental variant of a documented family with a new, published state
  variable.** The family is a known baseline; what is claimed as new is the scheduled-supply state
  and the trade built on it.
- *Confidence in the comparison:* **low-to-moderate.** The plan literature is recent, the located
  price-effect evidence concerns a different event, and no paper was located that constructs a
  "remaining scheduled supply" state.

**Target scope.** US-listed equities with a Section 16 officer or director; small- and mid-cap names
in which scheduled insider sales are a non-trivial share of median daily dollar volume; horizons from
days (pre-window positioning) to weeks (the window itself); exclude sell-to-cover sales tied to
vesting and tax withholding, which are mechanically linked to compensation events rather than being
scheduled discretionary supply.

**Observables.** Item 408(a) quarterly tables (adoption date, termination date, duration, aggregate
maximum shares, plan or not, from 10-Q/10-K); Form 4 filings (plan checkbox, plan adoption date,
transaction date, size, price, and where reported, remaining plan capacity); daily prices and
volumes; short interest and borrow cost for the short leg; a pre-specified liquidity benchmark
(trailing median dollar volume) to scale the supply.

**Rule sketch (descriptive, non-executable).** Each quarter, parse Item 408(a) tables to build, per
security, the set of live plans with adoption date, duration and maximum aggregate size; subtract the
plan sales already reported on Form 4 to obtain remaining scheduled supply; scale by trailing median
dollar volume to obtain a supply-to-liquidity ratio; where remaining scheduled supply exceeds a
pre-registered threshold relative to liquidity and the price has not already moved, take the short
leg in expectation of pressure from the scheduled supply, with a pre-registered exit at the plan's
window end or when remaining capacity is exhausted.

**Persistence argument.** The 10b5-1 plan is the default legal route for insider sales, because the
affirmative defence is available only for pre-arranged trades (S-182); the 2022 amendments make the
plan *more* central, not less. Plan-based sales are a structural feature of US equity supply,
refreshed each quarter as compensation vests and each filing cycle produces new plans, so the
pressure is repeated rather than one-off.

**Constraints and capacity.** Scheduled insider sales are a small fraction of market volume for most
names, so the effect is plausibly above the cost floor only in smaller names — which are also the
most expensive to borrow and the least liquid. The trade consumes exactly the liquidity it prices, and
informed liquidity providers compete for the same concession.

**Regime sensitivity.** The effect should be strongest when the security is small relative to the
plan size, when the plan's window is short and front-loaded, when the tape is rising (plan sales are
usually market or limit orders that execute more readily into strength), and when the window is not
coincident with an information event. It should be weakest around earnings announcements, where plan
sales cluster with information events (S-183) and the sign is confounded.

**Falsifiable predictions and kill conditions.**
1. *Cross-sectional:* securities with the largest remaining scheduled supply relative to liquidity have
   more negative abnormal returns over the pre-window period than matched securities. **Kill:** no
   cross-sectional difference, or the wrong sign.
2. *Within-name:* the abnormal return into the plan's window end is negative and reverses after the
   window closes. **Kill:** no reversal.
3. *Dose-response:* the effect scales with the remaining supply-to-liquidity ratio, not with plan
   existence.
4. *Exogeneity:* the effect survives controls for earnings-announcement dates, index rebalances and
   overlapping plan windows, and is stronger for plans whose windows are *not* near earnings (S-183).
5. *Cost floor:* the gross concession must exceed round-trip cost including borrow on the short leg.
6. *Decay:* if the effect is competed away it should shrink over calendar time; a stable effect over a
   decade would itself be evidence against the mechanism.

**Confounders and rival explanations.** (i) Earnings and information events: plan sales cluster around
earnings, so the price move is information rather than supply. (ii) The adoption-timing channel
(S-183): abnormal returns attach to the *adoption* window, so a rule keyed to execution must exclude
adoption windows. (iii) Insider *buys*, which carry the opposite sign and are discretionary.
(iv) Compensation events: sell-to-cover sales tied to vesting are mechanically linked to the vesting
date and are not evidence of scheduled discretionary supply. (v) Option-expiry and index-rebalance
collisions. (vi) Vendor-constructed datasets with survivorship and plan-classification error.

**Data requirements and feasibility for a future independent validation.** EDGAR filings (10-Q/10-K
Item 408(a) tables; Form 4 XML), daily prices and volumes, short interest. Feasibility:
**medium-low** — every input is public, but a security-level panel with a reliable plan-by-plan
capacity reconciliation must be built from thousands of filings, and which plan a given Form 4 sale
draws on is not always disclosed.

**Theory/evidence status.** Conceptual rationale: supported for the *rule* and the *publication* of the
state (S-182, S-183); **unsupported** for the price-pressure link, for which no published work was
located in this cycle. Mechanism supported by sources: partially — the flow's scheduled,
non-discretionary character is documented; the price effect is not. Prior empirical support reported
by others: for plan-timing price effects around adoption (S-183, as characterised by the SEC); for
insider-trading informativeness generally (a known baseline, R-005). Unresolved: whether the
*execution* window carries a distinct, tradeable price effect once information events are excluded.

**Remaining uncertainty / confidence.** Confidence in the mechanism: moderate (the rule and the
disclosure are certain; the price link is conjectural). Confidence that the state is decision-time:
high (quarterly Item 408(a) plus Form 4 updates). Confidence that the effect survives costs: low.
Priority score **61/100** — mechanism, causal coherence and persistence **4 → 20.0**; evidence
quality and independence **2 → 8.0**; substantive novelty **3 → 9.0**; falsifiability **4 → 12.0**;
resilience to costs, crowding and decay **2 → 6.0**; data availability **3 → 6.0**.
**Status parked (P-011), not ranked** — a parked candidate is not in the ranking because its
precondition is open; if G-21 closes in the candidate's favour it would enter between IDEA-010
(65.0) and IDEA-009 (60.0).

**Cycle 30 screen (G-13 publication timing; G-12 sign or existence) — G-13 PASS, G-12 PASS on the
existence limb; parked (P-011) on evidence.**
- **G-13 — PASS.** The state is published by the issuer (Item 408(a), quarterly, in the 10-Q/10-K)
  and by the insider (Form 4, within two business days of the trade), both before or at the time of
  the supply they describe, so a rule can be run on public information. This is the register's
  cleanest *legal-disclosure* instance of a decision-time state.
- **G-12 — PASS on the existence limb.** The claim is that the effect exists only where scheduled
  supply is large relative to liquidity, so the state conditions the *existence* of a tradeable
  concession rather than merely its size.
- **Why parked rather than retained.** The register rejects only when a published result *shows* the
  candidate's effect, and nothing published shows this effect — so the candidate is not rejected. But
  the register also does not promote a candidate whose nearest published evidence concerns a
  different event (plan *adoption* around earnings, S-183) and whose magnitude is most likely below
  the cost floor for most names. It therefore enters as **parked (P-011)** with one unblocking
  condition, **G-21**: is there published work that (a) constructs a security-level *remaining
  scheduled supply* state from Item 408(a) and Form 4 filings, and (b) documents a price effect
  attached to the *execution* window rather than the adoption window? If yes and the effect is
  positive, the candidate is re-scored and enters the ranking; if the answer is a published null on
  execution-window price pressure, the candidate is rejected; if nothing is published, it stays
  parked with an evidence-first condition. **No validation specification is written**; V-010 is not
  opened.

**Role in the portfolio.** The register's first candidate in a *pre-announced, non-discretionary
insider supply* family, and the first whose state is published by corporate disclosure law rather
than by a market operator, a regulator or an exchange. The economics are ordinary
liquidity-provision economics; the register's claim is only that the *schedule* is unusually well
published — which is exactly the kind of claim that must be earned with a price-effect literature
this cycle did not find.

### Cycle 35 (2026-10-20) — G-21's IDEA-015 limb confirmed: the published 10b5-1 effect is an *adoption*-window effect, so P-011 stands

- **The unblocking condition was re-run and re-answered.** The condition registered in Cycle 30 was:
  is there published work that (a) constructs a security-level *remaining scheduled supply* state from
  Item 408(a) and Form 4 filings, and (b) documents a price effect attached to the **execution**
  window rather than the **adoption** window?
- **What was found.** The published Rule 10b5-1 literature attaches its effect to **participation and
  adoption**. S-224 (Jagolinzer 2008, "SEC Rule 10b5-1 and insiders' strategic trade", *Management
  Science* 55(2): 224–239) finds that "participating sales systematically follow positive and precede
  negative firm performance, generating abnormal forward-looking returns larger than those earned by
  non-participating colleagues", and that "a substantive proportion of randomly drawn initiations are
  associated with pending adverse news disclosures". Read with the register's existing sources on the
  adoption-window effect (S-182, S-183, S-187), the published result is an *adoption-window* result and
  nothing located attaches a price effect to the **execution** of a disclosed schedule.
- **Verdict.** No execution-window result was located, so the condition is **not met** and the parking
  stands. **P-011 is confirmed**; IDEA-015 remains parked at 61.0 with its evidence-first condition
  unchanged; **no validation specification is written**; the candidate may not be re-specified around an
  easier state, and may not use sell-to-cover sales tied to vesting and tax withholding.
- **Score: 61.0, unchanged**, and it stays outside the active ranking.

## State-proxy methodological note (closing section)

**Purpose.** This note answers the question deferred by Cycles 1 and 2: how to choose, build and
validate a state proxy, and when the honest answer is "no public proxy exists — park the
candidate rather than re-specify it." It governs every candidate in the register whose mechanism
depends on a latent state.

**1. The state-proxy problem is largely a venue-selection problem.**
IDEA-002 (equities, funding state) is parked because no public funding-state proxy exists
(G-01); IDEA-007 (FX, dollar funding state) works because the BIS publishes the state on a known
calendar (S-052, S-054). IDEA-004 (equities, crowd depth) is now resolved on *timing* — the
13F-based crowd state is published on EDGAR within 45 days of each quarter end, so a
quarter-anchored rule is decision-time feasible — and its binding constraint is **proxy
validity** (G-04), not staleness; the Cycle-5 characterisation of it as "quarterly-stale" was
wrong about which question it was blocked on. IDEA-005 (futures, passive share) passed on timing
(CFTC publishes Index Trader positions weekly — S-030) but was **parked in Cycle 6 (P-009)** on
G-12, because its own state's closest published test is null (S-099) and the aggregate effect it
would condition is documented as decayed (S-033) — a different failure, and a reminder that a
*published* state is a feasibility property, not a source of novelty. IDEA-008 (securities
lending, borrow cost) **no longer works**: the register's Cycle-3 assessment that "short interest
and lending fees are published" was wrong about the lending-fee leg, and the Cycle-6 screen
established that the lending-rate data are not publicly disseminated before **29 March 2029**
(S-126) while the short-interest file is twice-monthly and published roughly seven to eight
business days after its settlement date (S-127); IDEA-008 is **parked (P-010)** with a dated
unblocking condition (G-14). IDEA-009 (crypto perpetuals, funding rate) works because the
exchange publishes the funding rate every few hours. The pattern is deterministic: *do not hunt for
a proxy; hunt for a venue where the state is already published by an institution* — and, added in
Cycle 6, *check the publication calendar of the state against the decision calendar before
assuming the venue qualifies*.

**2. How to choose a state proxy.**
A state proxy must satisfy all five conditions:
(a) **Observable at decision time:** the value must be knowable before the trade is executed,
   with the true publication lag respected. A quarterly holdings file used on a daily rule is
   look-ahead, not a proxy.
(b) **Published by an institution:** an exchange, regulator, benchmark administrator, settlement
   utility, or official statistics body releases it on a fixed calendar. Vendor-constructed
   series are proxies of proxies and must be labelled as such.
(c) **Causally connected to the mechanism:** the proxy must measure the binding constraint or
   flow that generates the edge, not a correlated variable. The carry channel is a rival
   explanation for the funding channel; the proxy must separate them.
(d) **Changes the sign or existence of the effect:** conditioning on the proxy must change the
   *sign* or the *existence* of the predicted return, not merely its size (per the rediscovery
   guard in `rejected_and_parked.md`).
(e) **Published before the decision, not after it** (added in Cycle 5, gap **G-13**): the state must
   be released *before* the decision date it informs, not merely before the end of the sample. An
   official state published on a calendar that runs *after* the decision it is supposed to inform
   can support an explanatory decomposition but can never support a decision-time rule. This is not
   a technicality: in the EU ETS the aggregate compliance balance is published in late May or June
   of the year *after* the emissions year (S-121), so under the pre-2023 April deadline it was
   published *after* the deadline, and the decision-time form of IDEA-013 begins only with the 2023
compliance year. **Every candidate must now record, for each observation, the publication date of
    the state relative to the decision date.**
    Cycle 6 applied condition (e) as a *screening test* to every active candidate and to the one
    new candidate, and the results are in each dossier's "Cycle 6 screen" block. Two outcomes are
    worth carrying forward as method: (i) **a published state can satisfy (e) even when it is
    assembled rather than published as an index** — IDEA-004's crowd state is built from filings
    that are individually published on a statutory cadence, and IDEA-014's net obligated position
    is an assembly of published standards, published generation data and published exemption
    decisions; what matters is that *every input* is published before the decision and that the
    assembly rule is fixed in advance; and (ii) **a state can fail (e) on a dated, institutional
    ground rather than a judgement** — IDEA-008 fails because the regulation that will publish the
    lending rates does not begin public dissemination until 29 March 2029 (S-126), and the
    register now treats a *dated* future publication calendar as a parking condition with an
    unblocking date rather than as a permanent rejection.

**3. How to build it without look-ahead.**
- Use only vintages available at the decision timestamp; if the series is revised, state whether
  real-time or latest-vintage data are used.
- Respect the true publication lag: a decision on day T may use only reports published on or
  before day T.
- Make the proxy construction point-in-time: no backfilled index membership, no survivorship,
  no future information in the selection of the universe.
- Freeze the proxy definition on the training window; do not re-estimate it after seeing holdout
  results.

**4. How to validate it against an independent measure.**
- Validate the fast proxy against at least one holdings-based or institution-reported measure
  (e.g. IDEA-004's proxy against S-026's holdings-based crowding; IDEA-002's proxy against
  disclosed dealer funding value adjustments). If validation fails, the candidate is untestable
  as specified — report that, do not re-specify on the fly.
- The validation itself must be out of sample relative to the trading test.

**5. When to park rather than re-specify.**
The honest answer is "no public proxy exists" when:
- the binding constraint is not published by any institution (IDEA-002's equity funding state,
  G-01);
- the only available measure is stale relative to the decision frequency (IDEA-004's crowd
  proxy, G-04);
- the fast version of the state must be constructed from paid vendor quotes (IDEA-007's
  cross-currency basis, G-10);
- the validator cannot confirm access to the required proprietary data (IDEA-006's intraday fix
  data);
- **the state will not be publicly published until a known future date** — added in Cycle 6 for
  IDEA-008: the SEC's Rule 10c-1a loan-rate data are not publicly disseminated until **29 March
  2029** at the earliest (S-126), so on the present calendar the binding constraint is not
  published by any institution. This case is **parked with a dated unblocking condition** (P-010,
  G-14), not rejected, because the blocker has a known end date;
- **the candidate's own state variable has a published null and the effect it would condition is
  documented as decayed** — added in Cycle 6 for IDEA-005 (P-009, G-06/S-099/S-033). This case is
  also parked rather than rejected, because the joint claim is untested rather than refuted; but it
  must not be presented as a discovery and it must not be re-specified around a weaker proxy.
In each case, park the candidate. Re-specifying around a weaker proxy converts a good idea into a
disguised baseline. Change venue, not proxy.

**6. Candidates governed by this note.**
IDEA-002 (parked, P-004, G-01), IDEA-004 (passes (e) on a filing-anchored rule; blocked on G-04
proxy validity), IDEA-005 (S-030, public — passes (e); **parked (P-009)** on G-12), IDEA-007
(S-054, public — passes), IDEA-008 (**fails (e)** — lending rates not public before 29 March 2029,
S-126; **parked (P-010)**, G-14), IDEA-009 (funding rate, published by venue — passes), IDEA-010
(partial: the flow leg passes, the AP-stress leg is commercial and unvalidated — G-16), IDEA-013
(published state, but **fails condition (e)** for the pre-2023 regime — parked, P-008), IDEA-014
(passes (e) — the obligation's size is codified (S-128), the deadline is published by regulation
(S-129) and moves only by published rule (S-131)), IDEA-015 (passes (e) — the state is published by
**disclosure law**: Item 408(a) quarterly tables and Form 4 plan disclosures (S-182, S-187);
**parked (P-011)** on evidence, G-21).

**7. A fourth kind of state publisher, added in Cycle 30: securities-law disclosure.**
The register's venue pattern has been "a market operator, a regulator or a settlement utility
publishes the state" (condition (e) above). IDEA-015 is the first candidate whose state is published
by **corporate disclosure law** instead — the issuer must file the plan's material terms quarterly,
and the insider must file each plan sale within two business days (S-182, S-187). This class has
two properties that the other publishers do not: the state is **mandatory and standardised** (no
vendor discretion, no publication discretion) and its **cadence is statutory**. It also has two
limitations: the state is **coarse** (an aggregate maximum size and a window, not an execution
schedule) and the **reconciliation** of individual Form 4 sales to individual plans is not always
disclosed, so the state must be *constructed* rather than read. The register's methodological
conclusion is unchanged — a state must still be published *before* the decision, and it must still be
valid for the quantity being conditioned — but a future cycle looking for a new venue should look
for this class of publisher first, and should expect to pay for the construction step.

## Cycle 13 frontier-family screen log (2026-10-20)

Six families were screened, one search round each, with the register's standing rules applied: write
a dossier only if the mechanism is unclaimed, the state is published *before* the decision, the sign
(or existence) is decidable from the published state, and the venue is feasible. A family that fails
any of these is recorded here as a bounded negative with the reason, so that a later cycle does not
re-screen it from scratch.

1. **Index reconstitution (the IDEA-001 family) — SCREENED, and it produced a rejection rather than
   a candidate.** See R-011. The family is now a *baseline*, not a candidate space: the effect has
   disappeared (S-146), the front-running is published as competitive liquidity provision (S-147),
   the passive-asset sizes are constructible (S-148), the counterparty is the issuer (S-151), and
   the execution convention is documented (S-149, S-150). Two further 2026 papers sit on top of this
   (S-171, a second model of anticipatory trading around reconstitution; S-152, the distinct
   *value-weighted composition* channel, whose published and working-paper versions even carry
   opposite signs — a warning about pre-publication numbers). **Any future proposal in this family
   must start from S-146 and must explain what is left.**
2. **Crypto token unlock cliffs — SCREENED OUT (documented).** Vesting schedules are published
   on-chain and in vendor calendars, and the *effect* is documented publicly: a market maker's study
   of more than 16,000 unlock events reports that roughly 90% were followed by negative price
   pressure, that the decline typically begins about 30 days before the unlock and stabilises within
   about two weeks after, and that team unlocks are the most disruptive (S-170 — an interested
   party, not peer-reviewed, but the sign, the window and the heterogeneity are all stated). There
   is also no sign-determining published state that would satisfy G-12: the schedule is known, but
   *whether the recipients sell* is not published. **No candidate.**
3. **California Cap-and-Invest: the surrender deadline — SCREENED OUT (documented, and the deadline
   demand is satisfied in advance).** This was the most promising second instance of the register's
   cap-and-trade template, and it has excellent published state: a codified 1 November, 5:00 p.m.
   Pacific surrender deadline with a **four-times** untimely-surrender penalty (§ 95856, § 95857 —
   S-174), a published auction settlement price at each quarterly auction, published allowance
   banking reports, and CARB's own market-monitoring reports (S-165). But the *mechanism* requires
   the deadline demand to be tight, and the published compliance record shows near-100% compliance
   with the obligation met in advance (S-165); the program's price history shows prices at or near
   the floor for most of its life (S-163, S-168). There is no published *state* that decides
   whether the deadline demand is tight — which is exactly the state IDEA-013 lacked and IDEA-014
   has. **No candidate; the family is recorded as a boundary case for IDEA-013/IDEA-014.**
4. **California Cap-and-Invest: the Allowance Price Containment Reserve sale — SCREENED OUT
   (documented, and inert in practice).** This is the closest thing to a *state-triggered,
   date-bound, price-insensitive supply event* found in thirteen cycles of screening, and its
   published state is unusually clean: a Reserve sale is offered **only if the preceding quarter's
   auction settled at or above 60% of the lowest Reserve tier price**, except for the sale
   immediately before the 1 November deadline, which happens "without regard to the settlement
   price"; the tier prices and quantities are published annually; notices are posted about 30 days
   ahead; and the schedule sits in Appendix C of the regulation (S-164, S-174). It fails for a
   practical reason that the register records as a general lesson: **the reserve has never been
   called into use** (S-163), and when it is offered, the tier prices sit far above the market
   price, so no obligations are actually taken up. A supply valve that never opens cannot carry a
   price effect. **No candidate — but this is the register's best template for what a *future*
   state-triggered supply valve would have to look like, and it is filed as a baseline.**
5. **Treasury General Account → repo/funding channel — SCREENED OUT (published).** TGA flows are an
   identified driver of short-term funding-market stress: an IMF working paper quantifies
   repo-spread swings around debt-ceiling episodes (S-160), the Federal Reserve's own notes model
   repo-rate sensitivity to Treasury issuance through the SOMA, the ON RRP facility and the TGA, and
   use the public **TGCR − IORB** spread as the decision-time stress measure (S-161, S-162). This
   also **narrows G-01**: the funding-state family is published, and what is missing is specifically
   a *dealer-level funding-value-adjustment* series (the construct is peer-reviewed in S-155; no
   public series exists and none is likely to). **No candidate.**
6. **Accelerated share repurchases — SCREENED OUT (published).** The ASR mechanism, its pricing as an
   Asian/Bermudan option and its announcement effects are all published (S-166, S-167); and the
   decision-time state is weak, because the discount is private-contract data and volumes are
   disclosed quarterly. **No candidate.**

**Cycle 13 summary of the screening log.** Six families, six bounded negatives, zero new dossiers.
The productive part of the cycle was not a new family but a *re-reading of the register's own top
candidate against the newest literature*, which is where the register found both its first-cycle
rejection of a rank-1 idea and the two most useful closures (G-03, G-02/G-07) it has ever had.

## Cycle 32 frontier-family screen log (2026-10-20)

One family was screened, one search round, with the standing rules applied: write a dossier only if the
mechanism is unclaimed, the state is published *before* the decision, the sign (or existence) is
decidable from the published state, and the venue is feasible.

1. **Corporate-debt tender offers and liability-management exercises — SCREENED OUT (bounded negative;
   filed as a baseline).** The family looked like the register's best remaining template fit: a *dated*,
   *price-insensitive* flow into a *published* deadline, with a decision-time state that is literally in
   the offer document (the offer price, the expiry date and time, the amount sought). Five findings kill
   it, and the last two are the register's own criteria rather than new facts.
   - **The flow is published and quantified** (S-204): the tender price averages a **4.75%** premium
     (median 3.40%) to the pre-tender market price, and **82.3%** of the issue (median 94.6%) tenders;
     a 1% rise in the tender premium raises the tendering rate by as much as 10%. The price-insensitive
     flow at a published deadline is a *documented, quantified* object, not an unclaimed mechanism.
   - **The deadline-cluster behaviour is published too** (S-206): **72%** of exercised-clawback issues
     underwent renegotiation by tender, and **73.9%** of them did so *before* the clawback's published
     expiration date. The "many obligations converge on one published deadline" structure is documented.
   - **The price-insensitive flow is the *issuer's*, and it supports the price rather than depressing
     it.** In a tender the issuer commits to buy at a fixed premium; that is price-insensitive
     *demand* at a published price, so the deadline puts a **floor** under the bond and pulls it *toward*
     the offer price as expiry approaches — the opposite shape to the register's cap-and-trade and
     compliance-deadline template (IDEA-013, IDEA-014), which requires the flow to push the price
     *below* fair value into the deadline and revert afterwards. (S-205 makes the structural contrast
     explicit: the tender is the price-known-to-the-seller route; open-market repurchase is not.)
   - **The one tradeable implication is a mechanical arbitrage rather than an edge**: buying a bond below
     a *published* offer price and tendering it is a trade against a known price, subject only to
     completion risk, proration/priority (the "early bird" step-down, S-208) and the guaranteed-delivery
     mechanic (S-207). The only residual after the deadline is the re-pricing of the *remaining* bonds
     as the capital structure changes — a **credit event**, not a mechanical reversion, and therefore not
     the register's object.
   - **There is no cross-issuer deadline cluster to exploit.** The tender deadline is **issuer-set**, with
     regulatory minimum offering periods (S-207, S-208) rather than a single statutory date shared by a
     population of obligors. The register's deadline-cluster template (EU ETS surrender, RFS compliance)
     works precisely because a *regulator* fixes one date for everyone; in credit, the deadlines are
     staggered by construction, so there is no cluster, and the family is the structural opposite of the
     register's best-documented deadline families.
    - **Verdict: bounded negative, no dossier — logged as R-013.** Filed in `rejected_and_parked.md` as
      a baseline so that a later cycle does not re-screen it. The one item left unverified is noted there as well: the specific
      Rule 14e-1 periods used in this screen are index-rendered from practitioner notes (S-208, S-209) and
      were not read against the rule text in this cycle (S-207).

## Cycle 35 frontier-family screen log (2026-10-20)

Two families were screened, with the standing rules applied: write a dossier only if the mechanism is
unclaimed, the state is published *before* the decision, the sign (or existence) is decidable from the
published state, and the venue is feasible. Both screens produced bounded negatives and **no new
candidate**, and neither consumed an IDEA identifier.

1. **Crypto token-unlock cliffs — RE-SCREENED, bounded negative, and the Cycle-33 screen entry written
   for real (R-014).** The Cycle-33 header and the Cycle-34 handoff both asserted that this screen had
   been executed and logged as R-014; no entry, verdict or source existed. Cycle 35 executed it. The
   channel is **already documented** — the register's own corpus holds the Cycle-13 study of more than
   16,000 unlock events, in which roughly 90 % were followed by negative price pressure, prices
   typically begin declining about 30 days *before* the unlock, team unlocks are the most disruptive,
   and the move stabilises within roughly two weeks after (S-170) — so a dated supply whose price
   effect is already published, in a signed direction, with a published pre-event window is a baseline,
   not a candidate space. Two further reasons: the **state is commercial** (third-party unlock
   calendars, not an issuer, exchange or regulator), which trips the register's own Cycle-30 standing
   condition on commercial states; and the **recipient population is anonymous**, so no pre-event
   measure of supply about to be absorbed exists that is not a restatement of the schedule. The family
   also inherits the unresolved crypto-venue risk that IDEA-009 already carries. **Bounded negative,
   R-014**; filed in `rejected_and_parked.md` as a baseline.
2. **The EU CBAM certificate purchase obligation — SCREENED, bounded negative, with a dated re-screen
   condition (R-016).** This was the register's best remaining template fit and the *third* instance
   of its own compliance-deadline template after two that are already exhausted (IDEA-013 parked,
   IDEA-014's conditional version retired) — a **different regulator** (the European Commission, not
   the EPA) and a **different obligor population** (importers of CBAM goods, not fuel producers). The
   family has excellent published state: the certificate price is published on a fixed calendar
   (four quarterly prices for 2026 — €75.36, €75.28, €82.32 and a Q4 price due 4 January 2027 — and
   **weekly from 2027**), the obligation calendar is fixed in the Commission's guidance and Q&A (from
   2027 an authorised declarant must hold certificates for at least 50 % of its embedded emissions at
   each quarter end; the first annual surrender is 30 September 2027; sales open on the central
   platform on 1 February 2027; the annual deadline has already moved once, from 31 May to
   30 September), and the constraint is real (loss of authorised-declarant status for serious or
   repeated infringement). Five findings nevertheless make it a baseline:
   - **The concession is not harvestable.** The certificate price is a *published deterministic formula*
     — the weighted average of EUA auction clearing prices (S-227) — and the certificate can be used
     only to discharge the obligation, is sold only to authorised declarants, and is not an allowance.
     No third party can buy it, and certificate sales do not consume EUAs.
   - **The would-be sign state is a definition.** Any gap between the certificate price and the spot EUA
     price is an artefact of the lagging formula, and declarants can time purchases inside the window,
     so the gap is arbitraged by the obligor rather than being a price.
   - **The obligation is near zero by construction.** The share of embedded emissions generating a
     certificate obligation is the complement of the free-allocation factor: 2.5 % in 2026, 5 % in 2027,
     10 % in 2028, 22.5 % in 2029, 48.5 % in 2030, 100 % only from 2034 (S-229) — the same structure
     the register already closed against IDEA-014's RIN state under G-20. The 2026 obligation is
     immaterial against EU allowance turnover.
   - **The obligor is the beneficiary.** The party who would capture any concession is the authorised
     declarant, not a participant the register could act as.
   - **The macro channel is published.** CBAM's effect on EU carbon prices, trade and competitiveness is
     a large published and policy literature, including the European Parliament's own briefing (S-229).
   **Bounded negative, R-016**, with a **dated re-screen condition**: re-screen when the obligation
   share has grown materially (the 2029–2030 step to 48.5 % of embedded emissions) *and* the weekly
   pricing regime has run long enough for the **pre-deadline hedging behaviour of authorised declarants
   in the EUA market** to be observable — obligor behaviour, not a regulatory-calendar fact, is the
   only residue that could carry a sign. Filed in `rejected_and_parked.md` as a baseline.

**Cycle 35 summary of the screening log.** Two families, two bounded negatives, zero new dossiers — and
a third, larger repair: the two screens above are the ones a prior cycle *claimed* to have run. The
productive part of the cycle was not a new family but the execution of the register's own **owed**
searches (G-22, G-23, G-21) and the correction of a header that had asserted work that was never done.

## Cycle 52 frontier-family screen log (2026-10-20)

One family was screened, with the standing rules applied: write a dossier only if the mechanism is
unclaimed, the state is published *before* the decision, the sign (or existence) is decidable from the
published state, and the venue is feasible. The screen produced a bounded negative and **no new
candidate**, and it did not consume an IDEA identifier. Cycle 52's other half was not a screen at all
but the register's **prior-art re-read of IDEA-011**, recorded in that dossier and in the Cycle-52 gap
verdicts of `source_ledger.md`.

1. **Venue-imposed trading halts, price bands and market-wide circuit breakers — SCREENED, bounded
   negative, six independent reasons, dated re-screen condition (R-032).** This is the register's **first
   screen of a *market-interruption* family**: every earlier screen (R-012 … R-031) covered an
   obligation, a switch, a supply schedule, an expiry-driven flow or a price-formation rule, and none
   covered the mechanism by which the **venue stops the market and restarts it**. Instances run: the US
   market-wide circuit breakers at 7 % / 13 % / 20 % of the prior S&P 500 close and the 15-minute
   level-1 halt (S-256, S-258, S-266); the Limit Up–Limit Down Plan's price bands, the 15-second Limit
   State, the five-minute Trading Pause and the reopening auction (S-259, S-260, S-262); the European
   volatility-interruption regime and the ESMA/MiFID II calibration and **publication-of-trading-halts**
   guidelines (S-261, S-263); and the Korean dynamic and static volatility interruptions (S-264).
   Six independent reasons make it a baseline, any one of them sufficient: **the effects are published
   and sized** (volatility rises drastically near the trigger, returns take on increasing negative
   skewness, 17.5 bps of normalised daily volatility per 1 % closer, roughly double inside 2 % — S-256;
   post-halt returns stabilise and trading costs fall — S-258; LULD curbs short-term volatility without
   delaying price discovery, with a magnet effect and reduced HFT activity after the halt — S-262; and the
   family has a thirty-year survey literature with its own methodological disputes — S-257);
   **the trigger state is public and real-time**, so there is no informational edge in *knowing* it (the
   SIPs republish the current price bands every 30 seconds, S-260, and the EU regime *mandates*
   publication of trading halts, S-261) — G-13 passes and is simultaneously useless here;
   **a market-wide trigger is a state common to every security**, so the only implementable claim is a
   directional bet on the index's own news, which is beta, and the published finding that index
   membership adds no market-quality impact during halts removes the cross-sectional version (S-258);
   **an individual-stock halt is triggered by that stock's own news**, so the identification is
   inseparable from the information event — the R-018 objection, not removed by the state's being public
   (S-259, S-260); **the concession is competed away inside the halt**, because the reopening is an
   auction and the venue's own Designated Sponsors are obliged to quote during the European call phase
   (S-259, S-263); and **the cost floor is hostile** — the tradeable window is seconds wide, in the
   widest-spread names, with 84 % of halted ETPs small-capitalisation and only 8 S&P 500 constituents
   halting on the SEC's own statistics for 24 August 2015 (S-259), realised volatility 194 % higher
   annualised in the first minute after a level-1 halt and elevated for over ten minutes, spreads wider for
   about five minutes (S-266). **The one narrow residual is recorded rather than claimed**: the SEC's own
   staff note that NYSE Arca applied price collars to reopening auctions on 24 August 2015, producing
   "prices that were substantially less dispersed than reopening auctions on other primary listing
   exchanges" and leaving "many unexecuted order imbalances" (S-259) — a venue-rule difference observed
   by the regulator on a single day, in a seconds-scale auction window. Filed in
   `rejected_and_parked.md` as **R-032** and added to the known-baseline table, with a **dated re-screen
   condition** (a persistent, pre-published reopening-auction rule difference; a mandatory participation
   requirement for a non-market-maker class; or a material change to the US thresholds, the LULD
   parameters or the EU publication requirement).

**Cycle 52 summary of the screening log.** One family, one bounded negative, zero new dossiers, zero
scores changed — and the cycle's real product was the **prior-art re-read of the rank-1 candidate**, which
returned a *sharper* null, one published conditioner that competes with IDEA-011's own state, one piece
of adverse premise evidence recorded against it, and — because it produced a dated, prospective route
rather than another null — the **opening of G-32**.

## Cycles 56–59 frontier-family screen log (Cycles 56–58 backfilled by Cycle 58; Cycle 59 added at the Cycle-59 wrap-up)

**This sub-section is a state-integrity repair.** The screen log above ends at Cycle 52, but Cycles 56, 57
and 58 each ran a family screen whose full rationale was written into `rejected_and_parked.md` and whose
verdicts were recorded in the source ledger's gap-verdict blocks. The log itself was not extended, so the
register's own screening history was **incomplete on its face** — the same defect the register repaired
for the Cycles 53–55 gap-verdict block (Cycle 56) and for the Cycles 36, 38–39 and 44–50 backfills
(Cycles 37, 40, 51). The three entries below are **summaries**; the reasoning, the sources and the dated
re-screen conditions live in `rejected_and_parked.md` under R-035, R-036 and R-037 and must be read there.
**Cycle 59's screen (R-039) was added to this log at the Cycle-59 wrap-up**, because the same defect
recurred: the screen's reasoning was written into the cycle log and the rejection log, but the register's
own screening history was not extended, and the screen's **decision line and known-baseline row were never
written at all** — the identical defect Cycle 58 had already repaired once for the cross-session row
(R-036). The three earlier entries below are therefore **summaries**, and the fourth is too.

**Cycle 56 — the tax-driven year-end flow family (R-035), bounded negative.** The first screen of a family
in which no participant is obliged to act and the flow's existence and strength are set by a published tax
rule: capital-loss harvesting under loss-carry-forward rules, and the paired institutional window
dressing. Instances run: the US rule (net capital losses offset ordinary income only to the lesser of
$3,000 or the total net loss, remainder carried forward), the Swedish carry-forward change, the Japanese
2003 capital-gains-tax reduction, and the multi-regime Swedish evidence. Bounded negative on five
independent reasons: the mechanism is published prior art with a **rule-dependent sign** and a published
**null**; the sign-determining state is **private tax-lot data**, so any cross-sectional condition is a
construction; the constrained flow is the **seller's own**, so there is no third-party concession; the
**rule-dated natural experiment has already been run**; and the tradeable window is hostile, with the
well-known year-end reversal an artefact of the same flow rather than a concession to a third party.
Dated re-screen condition: a thin-literature jurisdiction publishing a loss carry-forward regime change
with an effective date and a published expected-flow measure; or tax-lot-level or broker-level flow data
becoming publicly disseminated; or a published dated *flow* at a year-end in an uncovered jurisdiction.

**Cycle 57 — the cross-session price-formation family (R-036), bounded negative.** The first screen of a
family in which the tradeable object is a *stale price* between a closed session and the next open. Five
independent reasons: the mechanism is published prior art with an **information-driven sign**; the
sign-determining overnight news is **not known at decision time**; the venue's own **opening call auction**
supplies the intermediation at the restart; **no unclaimed sign** meets the strict bar; and the **cost
floor is hostile** (a minutes-wide window, in the thinnest after-hours liquidity, with wider spreads
outside regular hours). The screen's evidentiary basis was **repaired in Cycle 58** — see the source
ledger's "Cycle 58 state-integrity repairs" block, which struck an unverifiable working paper (S-290) from
the basis and replaced it with S-305, and which corrected the titles of S-287, S-288 and S-289.

**Cycle 58 — the contractual-barrier family (R-037), bounded negative.** The first screen of a family in
which the discontinuity is **contractual**: the published knock-out or knock-in level of an autocallable, a
barrier reverse auto or a knock-out structured note, whose passage changes the issuer's or the dealer's
hedging discontinuously. Instances run: down-and-in knock-out structures; up-and-out and auto-call
structures; the European retail market (regulator market structure, KID publication and exchange listing
documentation); the Korean retail ELS market; the Chinese retail autocallable market; and the
barrier-option hedging literature itself. Six independent reasons: the mechanism, its sign and its
magnitude are **published prior art with numbers** (a peer-reviewed event-day effect of −6.4 % and an
event cascade); the published evidence says the flow is **anticipated**, which forecloses the register's
concession template; the discontinuity is a **modelling convention the industry removes by design** (the
barrier shift); the state that sets the magnitude is **not publicly measurable**, and where it has been
estimated the estimate is small, conditional and explicitly non-causal; the tradeable expression is
**hostile on cost and event risk**; and — added in Cycle 58's second verification pass — the family has
already been turned into a **published strategy claim in the damping direction** (S-309). Dated re-screen
condition: a peer-reviewed study of a US or EU retail structured-note market keying the *published*
barrier level to a measurable underlying price effect; a published source of outstanding autocallable
notional by barrier level and maturity; a published study showing the effect is **not** anticipated in a
specific venue or that the barrier shift is not applied there; or a published study showing the dispersion
expression in S-309 does not work.

**Cycles 56–58 summary of the screening log.** Three families, three bounded negatives, **zero new
dossiers, zero scores changed** — and one candidate rejected outright (IDEA-002, R-038, on an executed
gap rather than on a screen).

**Cycle 59 — the published-price-of-a-constraint family (R-039), bounded negative.** The register's
**first screen run in the explicit state-first order** — the order the Cycle-58 handoff prescribed for
Cycle 59, in which the state is asked about *before* the mechanism is, and the first screen of a family
whose *object is the state itself*: a continuously published, decision-time **rate** that prices a funding,
balance-sheet or capacity constraint. Instances run: repo specialness; securities-lending fee curves; CDS
financing spreads; standing-repo award rates; and the public reference-rate and repo-operations series the
register already holds (S-041, S-042). Bounded negative on five independent reasons: **(i)** the published
rates are **indicators** — states that condition effects whose sign and existence are already claimed —
rather than an unclaimed edge with a clean sign-determining condition; **(ii)** the *decision-time* state a
candidate would need at the required granularity is **not cleanly public** where the family's economics
live: securities-lending fee data is fragmented and largely proprietary, and repo specialness is episodic
and specialised rather than a continuous series; **(iii)** the family conditions the **magnitude** of an
effect more often than it changes the **sign or the existence** of one, which is **G-12's binding limb** —
the same failure mode that rejected IDEA-002; **(iv)** where the rates are public and widely observed, the
effects are **anticipated or competed away**; and **(v)** without a clean published decision-time state that
is not a public real-time by-product of the constrained flow's own trading (the **Cycle-52 third test**),
no candidate in the family could pass the strict bar. **No `S-` identifier was consumed** — the screen's
conclusions are structural, not citation-dependent. Dated re-screen condition: a continuously published,
decision-time series of a funding, balance-sheet or capacity constraint becomes available at the granularity
a candidate would need, with documented cadence and coverage, and the literature shows an unclaimed
sign-determining effect that it conditions.

**Cycles 56–59 summary of the screening log.** Four families, four bounded negatives, **zero new
dossiers, zero scores changed** — and one candidate rejected outright (IDEA-002, R-038, on an executed
gap rather than on a screen). The most recent screen (R-039) is also the **first one run in the explicit
state-first order**, and its verdict is consistent with the register's dominant pattern: the binding
constraint is the **sign-and-existence** limb of the published state, not the availability of any series.
